The command line also has tests of its own now (`InterCat.Cli.Tests`). They run `icat` in-process, as a script would, and read both streams and the exit code. They cover this revision and the behaviour revisions 297 and 298 could only verify by hand: an interval longer than a tick count refused by every command that reads one, an interval past the clock's range answered, and each refused metric naming its option.

Revision 300 lets an option keep its value wherever it is written, found while sweeping the commands' arguments. Twenty-two commands read their session directory before their options. A value written first therefore lost its place to the directory: `icat metric --metric observations <session>` looked for a session named "observations", and `icat processes --top 2 <session>` for one named "2". Every command now reads its options first. The argument reader refuses to read an option after a positional argument, so a command that regresses fails the first time its arguments are read, and the command-line tests read every command's. `measure` and `verify` read their mechanism as a leading verb, since it decides which options follow. A negative number is an operand, so a saved view of an investigation can start before its epoch (`icat workspace view <workspace> early -5 10`), where `-5` was refused as an unknown option. A bare `--` begins operands, so a note may start with a dash.

Revision 301 keeps a session's ranking in the investigation it was opened from, as revision 280 keeps its pins (§26.3's sort, `workspace-v12`). A member's layout now holds, beside its pins, what its rows are ranked by and whether per second. A layout that keeps only a ranking is kept; one that pins nothing and ranks by records counted whole keeps nothing and is removed. Opened from the investigation again, the session gets its ranking back, and its status says what was put back ("which put back 1 pin and its ranking by bytes sent per second"). Opened on its own, it ranks by records. A file before version 12 ranks nothing, and one that names `Records`, or no metric §6.1 offers, is refused. `icat workspace show` states each layout's pins and ranking (`workspace-resolution-v14`), in the same words as the window, which now share one name for each ranking. §26.3's lane grouping and view filters have no control in the window yet, so nothing of them is kept.

Revision 302 has a refused argument say what it was. Every command called whatever it could not take an "unknown or incomplete option", including an operand past the last it takes, and a note that starts with a dash, which revision 300's `--` would have taken: `icat workspace note <workspace> "-> retry storm"` was refused as an option with no word of how to write it. The command line now words this refusal in one place. An option it does not know, or one missing its value, is named as before. An operand that starts with a dash says to write it after `--`, as in `-- "-> retry storm"`. An extra operand is an unexpected argument.

Revision 303 has every machine-readable answer name its contract, found by a test that reads every command's `--json`. Each answer was one JSON document on stdout, but two were bare arrays naming no contract. `icat profiles --json` listed the capture intents and `icat metric --matrix --json` the metric matrix, so a reader could tell neither format apart from a later one, and neither could grow a field without breaking its readers. They are now `capture-profile-catalog-v1` and `metric-matrix-v1`, each an object with its `contract` beside its list. The command-line tests read every read command's `--json`, and refuse anything but one object that names a contract, schema or report version.

Revision 304 has a reader's refusal of a value read as its own words. A malformed evidence or channel cursor, an export's unknown row key, or a metric over two clocks' readings is refused by the reader that cannot take it, with an `ArgumentException`. .NET appends the parameter's name to such a message, so `icat evidence <session> --cursor abc` said "The evidence cursor is malformed; restart from the first page. (Parameter 'cursor')", naming the code's variable rather than anything a person typed. The command line now prints the reader's words alone, in each of the eight places it reports one.

Revision 305 shows a part that is not whole with its gaps in place: M8's explicit missing-range and truncated states, on the one source that numbers its buffers. Since revision 242 such a part named what it lacked and was shown one buffer at a time, so reading a response body that had lost one buffer meant opening each of its records in turn. A part is now its pieces in order: each recorded buffer's kept bytes, and a gap wherever the part's bytes are not. A gap is buffers never recorded, before the first recorded one, between two or after the last, or the rest of a buffer the record limit cut, or a buffer recorded with none of its bytes kept. Its length is stated only where a record states it. A buffer never recorded states none, so where anything after it begins in the part is not known, and is not given. The viewer's toggle and `icat content --part --reveal` show such a part buffer by buffer. Each buffer has a heading that names it, its ends and, while it is known, its bytes in the part, and each buffer's hexadecimal is numbered from its own first byte. Each gap is a line of its own that begins with "--", as no line of hexadecimal does, so no kept byte can pass for one. Buffers are chosen by number, where bytes are chosen by offset, and at most 64 KiB are shown at once: the view stops at the start of the buffer that would pass them, and says so. The text view is not offered there, since text has no line a gap could hold that its bytes could not imitate. Such a part is never saved as one file (P2), and `content-view-v1`'s part now lists its gaps, additively, for a program.

Revision 306 lets a person release a session's kept content on its own, keeping its metadata (IC-016, content-v1 §2). Since revision 234 content went only with the journal chunk whose records it belonged to, so the only way to drop the payloads a capture kept - an HTTP exchange's cookies or authorization headers - was to give up those records' journal too, or to delete the session. A content release publishes a generation that names no content chunk and keeps everything else: every journal, row and derived file, the plan, the ledger, the calibration and the committed boundary. Its retention record, of a new kind, `Content`, names the chunks, their bytes, how many records' content they held and why they went. A chunk holds each record's content facts as well as its bytes, so both go, and every record and its size stay. A release is refused when nothing is kept. It is also refused while the capture has not finished, since a recorder still writing would find the session changed beneath it and fail, and one that stopped without finishing cannot be told from it. `icat retain <session> --release-content` measures it first and performs it only with `--confirm` and a stated reason, as a journal release does. Readers say what happened rather than that nothing was kept: while the release's generation is current, a record's content states when and why it went, and an HTTP record, whose source carries its message, no longer says its source carries metadata only. `icat session` now states any retention record of the generation it reads, which it never did. The record lives in its own generation's manifest, so a later publication leaves it behind; carrying a release's record forward is store work all retention kinds share.

Revision 307 fits a deep rung into the minimum window (R15), found by reviewing the ladder's rendered frames at 1080 by 700. Three things collided there. Back named the whole rung it returned to, so at an operation its face read "Back to Channel: 127.0.0.1:44310 → 127.0.0.1:8443 (Esc)". Its 280 px cap cut that mid-glyph, since the style meant to trim it never reached the text, and the session's title shrank to "Synthetic disco…". Back now names the rung by its kind, "Back to Channel (Esc)", as Forward already names none, and the rung in full is its tooltip and accessible help. The rail's heading set the rung's badge in a column of its own, so "L4 · OPERATION" lay over "RANKED TABLE"; the badge now wraps under the heading where both do not fit. And the timeline wrote its peak rate in the plot's top-left corner, where a wide one - 10,000,000/s, or a byte rate - lay over the tallest bars; it now stands in the band above the plot, whose notes stand at its right.

Revision 308 gives the rail the empty state §3.1's first step specifies, found in the same review: with no session shown, the sessions the user saved stand where the ranked table will be. The ranked table's heading, summary, hints and search stood there instead, with nothing to rank or search. At the minimum window, when the launch also offered to finish an unfinished capture, its card and the Explore card took the rest of the rail. So the saved sessions showed only their heading, and the Explore card ran past the window's edge, cutting off its last button. With no session shown the rail now holds no ranked table's chrome. The empty rung's card appears only while a capture is on its way to its first view, under "NOTHING RECORDED YET", since otherwise the Explore card says what to do. The rail's actions scroll within the height its list leaves them, which keeps a row or two of ranked rows or saved sessions in view. That holds with a session shown too, where an unfinished capture's card beside the Explore card had squeezed the ranked rows to a sliver. The saved sessions now follow the workspace's own emptiness rather than whether a capture's overview was read, so a session shown by any route hides them.

Revision 309 brings `icat --help` up to date with the commands it summarizes, found by comparing each command's own help with its entry there (§20.4). The summary had fallen behind its commands. It said `--part` showed a part only when it was whole, which revision 305 changed. It named neither `retain --release-content`, which revision 306 added, nor `package --original`, one of §11.3's three presets. It listed five of `workspace`'s fourteen subcommands, two of `record`'s five profiles and one of `follow`'s two forms, and it still called itself M1's. Each entry now gives every form of its command, and the summary ends by saying that `icat <command> --help` lists every option. A test reads every command's own help and requires the summary's synopsis of it to name each form that help gives - a subcommand, or a word like `--original` that sets one form apart - so a form added later reaches the summary or fails the build. On a system other than Windows, `icat capture`, which needs the capture broker, now says so and what can be done there, with the capability exit code, where it was refused as an unknown command.

Revision 310 stops a reader writing into a folder that holds no session, found by pointing the Desktop and `icat` at the wrong folder. A reader holds the session's evidence guard before it reads the pointer, and when no guard was there it made one, so that sessions from before the guard existed could still be read (§8). It did that in any folder: `icat session ~/Documents`, documented as read-only, left a `session-evidence-lease.lock` there, and so did the Desktop's *Open saved session*. A reader now establishes a missing guard only where a session pointer shows a generation was published. Anywhere else it is refused and writes nothing. The refusal reads "No InterCat session has been published in this folder. It is not a session's folder, or its capture has not published a generation yet; nothing was written to it." That replaces "This session has published no generation, so there is nothing to acquire. An empty session is not a generation with no data." The commands that read a session, and the Desktop's open, now all give these words.

Revision 311 has a workspace or settings file that cannot be read say where to look, since §26.3 makes both hand-editable. A workspace that was not JSON answered with the parser's own words: "'h' is an invalid start of a value. Path: $ | LineNumber: 0 | BytePositionInLine: 0". One that held the wrong fields was worse: "JSON deserialization for type 'InterCat.Application.InvestigationWorkspaceFile' was missing required properties including: …", naming a type no reader of the file can see. Now text that is not JSON gives the line and character where it stops being JSON, counted from 1. JSON that is not a workspace gives the fields it lacks, or those it holds that this version does not know, or else the path of the value that does not fit: "it lacks the fields contract, workspaceId, …", "it holds a field this version does not know: colour", "its value at $.members is not of the kind that field holds". The settings file's notice gives its line and character the same way.

Revision 312 has every command name a wrong input for what it is, found by running each one against a folder that holds no session and against a session copy with one byte changed (§20.4). Revision 310 gave the commands that read a session one sentence for a folder that holds none, but their exit codes still disagreed. `overview`, `channels`, `evidence`, `timeline`, `export`, `processes`, `operations`, `exchanges`, `metric` and `rederive` gave 3, the code for a permission or capability failure. `package`, `retain`, `compact` and `checkpoint` gave 2, and `recover` gave 4, the code for corrupted input. `raw` and `content` gave a warning and 1, which tells a script it got a partial answer. Four of these did not use the sentence either: they said "This session has no published generation to compact", naming as a session a folder that may not be one. Naming the wrong folder is a mistake in what was asked, like naming one that does not exist, and that is already invalid invocation everywhere. Every command now refuses it in the sentence and with 2. The store now raises it as an exception of its own, derived from the one it raised before, so the Desktop handles it as it did and the command line can tell it apart. `icat session` still reports on such a folder, because it shows what opening a folder found. It now says "Acquired: nothing", where it said it had acquired "the generation the pointer names" though there was no pointer, and it uses the sentence where it said "That is an empty session". A workspace member whose folder holds no session gives the same reason. The changed byte found a second wrong code. `icat export` reported a damaged session as a capability failure, 3, because it caught the store's corrupted-input exception with its own refusals. It now leaves damage to the shared handler, as every other reader does, so a damaged session is 4 everywhere. Its one refusal that used that exception, a session replaced during the export, now uses the exception the other readers use for that. Three wrong inputs are now named for what they are. `import` given a folder said "ETL evidence not found" and now says it reads one trace file. `verify` given a folder said "Only 'tcp' and 'udp' verification exist in this milestone". It now says it names a mechanism, and that a session is verified whenever it is opened. `measure` given an unknown mechanism no longer speaks of milestones.

Revision 313 lines up a report's values, found by reading `icat staging` and `icat session` where revision 312 left them (§20.4). A field's label took a 22-character column. A longer one kept two spaces before its value, so as not to run into it, and that value then started a column or more past the others: "Abandoned staged files  0" stood over "Marker-only files     0". Twelve labels in seven commands are that long. Fields written one after another are now one group, written together when anything else is written or the command ends. Each group's values start two spaces past its longest label, and never nearer than the column a short label takes. Whatever else is written first writes out the group before it, so a report still reads in the order it was written. That holds for a heading, a line, a note or a table, and for a warning or progress on stderr.

Revision 314 has a damaged session say what changed, once, in a person's words, found by changing one byte of a session copy (§20.4, store-v1 §6). Every reader refused it with "This session has no complete generation. Its pointer is unusable (dependency 'seg-0000000001-0000.icats' computes sha256:d678… where generation 2 recorded sha256:2d7f…) and its last-known-good is too (…)". The second parenthesis repeated the first for generation 1, because both generations need that file. One changed byte cost four 64-digit digests and the words pointer, dependency and computes. A reason now names the file and what is wrong with it: "'seg-0000000001-0000.icats' does not match what generation 2 records (its SHA-256 begins d678662e9244, not 2d7f44f53556)", "(it is 2,831 bytes, not 2,832)", or "'…', which generation 2 records, is missing". It no longer gives a missing file's whole path in .NET's words. Each digest is given by its first twelve digits, which tell two apart; the whole recorded one is in the manifest. A verification problem now carries the file it concerns. When the generation kept to fall back to fails on the same file, the refusal says it "needs the same file" rather than repeating the reason. One that fails for another reason gives that reason, and when none is kept the refusal says so. Every refusal ends by saying the session's files are left as they are. The original package's copy and the reading of an index before the session is hashed now use the same words. The Desktop, `rederive`, `metric` and a workspace member put the reason in parentheses, which would now nest, so each gives it after a colon instead. A workspace member whose newest generation fails was said to hold "an earlier copy put in its place", which was untrue. It now says its path holds the last complete generation, and why.

Revision 315 lets a release outlive the generation that made it, a gap revision 306 recorded (IC-016, store-v1 §8). A retention record belonged to the generation that published it, so the next generation - a checkpoint, a compaction, another release - left it behind. A record's content then said only that "a retention may have released it", and `icat session` stated nothing. A journal release fared worse. Re-derivation's readiness checked the generation's own record alone, so after any later generation `icat session` said re-derivation could be attempted, and `icat rederive` refused only after reading every segment. Every later generation now carries the latest release of each extent nothing can rebuild - a journal prefix and kept content - with the generation that published it. A generation that releases one itself carries no earlier one of that kind, so a manifest carries at most one of each. Derived files are not carried, because they can be rebuilt. The list is written and hashed only when it holds a release, so a manifest that carries none is the same file, with the same digest, and an older reader still reads it. A carried content release is stated as the generation's own is. Re-derivation refuses as the release did, before reading a segment, and `icat session` says which generation released each.

Revision 316 asserts three rules the coverage index still listed as uncovered, though what they constrain now exists (§13.5). I10 and P9 waited on M4, whose alignment revision 254 began. I10 says a locally measured duration is unchanged by any alignment revision. It is now asserted across four states: before the member is aligned, aligned within a bound, aligned again at two instants whose rate then places it, and withdrawn. In each, two instants of the member compare exactly on its own clock, while their placement in the workspace's time does move with the measured rate. P9 says no cross-host order may be stated that the combined uncertainty does not support. It is asserted at the uncertainty's edge: a difference exactly as large as the uncertainty is no order, and its words state none; a nanosecond more is an order. Where the uncertainty is unknown nothing is stated, not even the difference. That includes two members aligned through a third whose rate is not stated, a branch no test had reached before. P5 says two records at one instant with one size must never become one observation. It waited on IC-006's correlators but holds without them: both such records count, and both are listed as evidence. R4 stays uncovered, with its reason corrected. A relation carries its strength, and its index names its rule, but a relationship does not yet carry its rule's identity and version, or its evidence, itself (IC-015).

Revision 317 keeps room for shown bytes in the smallest content viewer, found by drawing each secondary window at its minimum size (§3.7). At 700 by 480 the viewer's facts - eleven of them - and its notice on sensitive bytes took about 230 pixels above the bytes. Once the bytes were shown, the hex view had room for two of a 2,460-byte message's 154 lines. The facts and the notice now share a scrolled block. While the bytes are shown, it gives up as much room as leaves them the tabs and ten lines, and scrolls within what it keeps, never less than about three rows of facts. Before the bytes are shown, and in a taller window, the facts keep all the room they need: at the default height of 700 nothing scrolls. The raw-record window, drawn the same way, fits its minimum size as it was.

Revision 318 has what InterCat says to a person name the product rather than its plan, found in the align dialog that the investigation's tests draw (§20.4). The dialog said a session "was imported, packaged, or captured before revision 255", and `icat session` said the same. `icat exchanges` spoke of "a redacted package made before revision 250". A field the admission plan cannot hold was "not admitted in this milestone". The capability report and the source catalog's notes spoke of "the M0 plan" and "(IC-005)", and `icat --help` of "the IC-010 baseline". A person cannot look these up, and their meaning changes as the plan moves. Each now says what it means: an earlier version of InterCat, this version, the active profile, a capture that requests the source, the performance baseline. A test now reads every string literal in the product's source and refuses any that cites a plan revision, a milestone or a backlog item. The tools that qualify a build still speak to their engineers in the plan's terms: the benchmark, the fixture measurements, their workloads and probe journals. The test names them as the exception. The investigation window's tests now let a dialog that sizes to its content finish sizing before they save what it drew. Their saved frames had shown the align dialog half black and the compare dialog's buttons cut off, though the dialogs themselves were whole.

Revision 319 has every text in the main window read whole, found in a frame the evidence tests saved at the minimum window (§6.8). A channel's rung has no rows, so it offers its records' step beside the reason, and that step read "Show source records (E". A button's own words never wrap, and the rail's card was narrower than they were. With the rail dragged to its narrowest, 220 pixels, the cards' headings lost their last letters too: "EXPLORE THIS MACHINE" and "NOTHING AT THIS LEVEL". The step's label now wraps, and so does every heading in that style where its place is narrower than its words; where it has the room it is one line, as before. A test walks every rung at the minimum window, from the machine to a channel's records, with the rail as it opens and at its narrowest. It refuses three things: a text wider than its own box that neither wraps nor ends in an ellipsis; one that a card or panel cuts off; and one that ends in an ellipsis with no tooltip to complete it. Every ellipsis the window draws today has its tooltip.

Revision 320 applies revision 319's test to the windows a record or a channel opens, each at its minimum size (§6.8). The channel browser names each channel by the processes it joins, and a long executable name ran its row past the list's edge. "server.exe · PID 200 ↔ client-with-a-long-name.exe · PID 100 · :8080 ↔ :50000 …" lost its endpoints and its record count, with no ellipsis or tooltip to say so. A row now wraps within the list. The content viewer, before and with its bytes, and the raw record window cut off nothing, and a second test now holds all three to that, with the longest names their rows can carry.

Revision 321 fits the investigation window to its smallest size, found by drawing each of its pages and dialogs there (§6.8). At 680 by 460 the timeline page gave its chart no height at all. Its introduction, squeezed beside two buttons, the zoom row and the lanes in words took the whole page, and the chosen column's readout, set beside five buttons, had no width either. The candidate joins kept about 44 pixels and the sessions list one row. The minimum is now 680 by 600, the smallest size at which each page keeps two of its sessions, lanes or notes in view, with overlaps stated and a session missing above them. The timeline's tools wrap above the chart, and the chosen column reads on its own line beside its Open button. The chart and its lanes in words scroll together, so the tools and the chosen column stay on the page however many lanes and notes there are. The investigation's path is one line that gives up its middle folders, with the whole path in its tooltip. The overlaps scroll within about three lines. The caveats on naming sessions and grouping hosts now stand on the sessions page they are about. The selected session's line says where it is, or was last found, with its path trimmed the same way, rather than repeating its row. Three dialogs cut off a long session name: the align and compare pickers cut it mid-word, and the note dialog pushed its instant out of the window. Their choices now end in an ellipsis with the whole name in a tooltip, as the channel-end picker's do, and the note's session takes only the room its instant leaves. The timeline's axis set its last label 60 pixels before its tick, so at the minimum width "2.25 s" and "3.00 s" ran together. Each end is now set against its own tick, and a label between them is drawn only where it keeps clear of its neighbours. A lane's label keeps to its lane, ending in an ellipsis where a reason is too long, and a session of one record no longer reads "1 records". The legibility test now refuses three more things. The first is a wrapped text whose box is shorter than its lines: that is how the readout vanished, into a column one pixel wide. The second is a text that a panel cuts off at either side, in part or whole, as the unwrapped toolbar would have lost its last button past the window's edge. The third is a text with no room at all. A view that scrolls sideways, as the crumb trail does, holds a text wholly past its edges a scroll away, but one it cuts across its far edge is still cut off. Two tests hold the window, every dialog it opens and the axis to all of this.

Revision 322 applies the legibility test to the prompts the windows build for themselves, each drawn with a package saved three folders deep (§6.8). Five held a fixed height, chosen for their words at one font and one length of path. They were the redacted report's and the redacted package's questions, the saved original's and the saved redacted package's results, and the question asked when the window closes while Explore records. None cut text off with that path in the test container. But the saved original's result had nine pixels to spare, so a longer path or larger text would have run its buttons past the window's foot. The redacted package's question, meanwhile, left 122 pixels empty below its buttons. Each now sizes to what it says, as the original package's question and the investigation's prompts already did. The question asked on closing now gives its safe answer, to keep recording, on Escape, and has that answer focused as it opens, as every other prompt does. Each prompt is built by a method of its own, so a test draws all seven with the long path. It holds each to the legibility test and to being as tall as what it says. The legibility test now also refuses a text that a panel or the window cuts at its top or bottom edge, as it already refused one cut at either side. A list that scrolls holds its rows past its edges a scroll away.

Revision 323 gives every relationship the rule that derived it and what it rests on (R4, and the explanation §6.2 asks every mark to be one action from). A relation carried its strength, and the index that derived it named its rule, but the relationship the graph draws could not say how it was made. Each now carries its rule as an identity and a version, read from the name its derivation records, so the two cannot drift. A TCP relationship is transport-endpoint-relation version 4, and an RPC one rpc-call-peer version 1. The tour's relationships carry synthetic-tour version 1: they illustrate, and no capture derived them. A TCP relationship also carries, in order, the key of each connection it rests on. That key names the first record of the connection at either end and opens every record of it, as its channel's rung does. Two reads of one generation still yield equal relationships, their evidence compared key by key. The relationships table's evidence column now explains each one in its tooltip, such as "Correlated evidence, derived by transport-endpoint-relation, version 4, from 2 channels: …". It names at most three keys and counts the rest. A screen reader hears the rule after the evidence, and an edge's hover card states the rule beside how many channels it rests on. An RPC relationship names no evidence yet, because the persisted overview keeps its links only as counts, and it says so. R4 stays uncovered until the links keep the calls they join.

Revision 324 completes R4: an RPC relationship now carries evidence too. Revision 323 left it naming only its rule, because the persisted overview keeps its links only as counts. It now names itself. Its key, which already identified the two processes its calls join, opens every call its links join, at both ends and in either direction, under the same evidence policy that counts it. Nothing about the links had to be kept to do so: the key is resolved against the generation's calls when it is opened. A policy that admits only direct evidence admits none of its links, since a link is correlated at best. A key naming a pair that no link joins opens nothing. Both are refused in words, as an RPC channel the policy hides already was. `icat evidence --channel` takes any relationship's evidence key, a TCP channel's or an RPC relationship's, as well as an RPC channel's or call's, and labels an RPC one as such. The relationships table and the edge's card say an RPC relationship rests on the calls its links join, by its key. The linked-calls session the RPC tests share now lives in the test sessions, so the command line's tests use it too. R4 is now asserted: every relationship carries its rule's identity and version, its strength, and evidence keys that open its records at both ends.

Revision 325 puts a chosen relationship's explanation where a keyboard can reach it (§6.2, R15). Revision 323 gave each relationship's evidence a tooltip naming the rule that derived it and what it rests on, but a tooltip answers a pointer. A person moving through the relationships table by keyboard, as R15's table equivalent is there for, never saw it, and a screen reader heard only the rule. The relationship chosen in the table, by keyboard or by pointer, is now explained in a line beneath it, in the tooltip's own words, and the line goes when none is chosen.

Revision 326 fixes an export that named one scope and held another (§6.4, R18). An evidence rung reached from an RPC channel or call, or from a process's HTTP exchanges or one exchange, reads its records by that key, and its pages did. Its export did not. The one-pass read an export makes was handed the rung's channel, owners and interval but not its key, so it read every record of the session, up to the export's limit, under a context naming the call. A person sharing one call's two records would have shared the session's. An evidence rung's scope now reaches every read through one mapping, the same for its pages and its export, on the desktop and in `icat export` alike, though no path the command line descends reaches those rungs yet. A scope that states a problem names no channel, owner or key, just as the whole session's scope does. The read itself now refuses it with that reason, rather than trusting every caller to check first. A test exports every kind of rung: a process's records, a paired channel's, an RPC channel's whole and under a brush, one call's, a process's HTTP exchanges' and one exchange's. Each export must hold exactly the records the rung lists, every page loaded, in the order it lists them, each with the owner the rung resolved for it.

Revision 327 makes the relationship table open what the graph's edge opens (§6.7, R15). R15 asks that every canvas affordance have a keyboard path and a table equivalent yielding the same result set. The table's rows were documented as yielding their edge's, yet no key or click on a row opened its relationship: Enter there fell through to the rail's own descent, and a row did not even carry its relationship's key. Enter or a double click on a relationship now opens it as a double click on its edge does. The rung it opens takes the keyboard: its rows, or, at a channel with no operation rung, the step that lists its records, so a second Enter reads them. Each row says what it opens, to a screen reader and in the line beneath the table, in the words the edge's card uses. An RPC relationship, from its edge as from its row, used to open only its source process, from which a person had to find its channel and calls by hand. It now opens the calls its links join, which revision 324 made its own key read. The source process's evidence step, scoped by that key, lists the linked calls' records at both ends, and Esc climbs to the process. Without the session's records an RPC relationship opens nothing, rather than an empty rung.

Revision 328 gives the saved views the keys their double click and button imply (§8.4, R15). A saved view could be shown by a double click, or by choosing it and then the Show button. Enter on it did nothing, so a person moving through the list by keyboard had to leave it for the button. Enter on a view now shows it. Enter in the name box saves the view shown under that name, as the button beside it does, and the list and the box say so to a screen reader. A view saved on another time reference used to ignore Enter and a double click without a word. It now says why it cannot be shown, and the dialog stays open. §6.7's interaction reference now states what revision 327 made an edge's double click open, and that a relationship's row opens the same.

Revision 329 makes a relationship something a person can choose (§6.7, §6.4). §6.7's reference says a click on an edge selects it and updates the inspector, yet a click on an edge did nothing. Choosing a relationship in the table chose its source process instead: the graph ringed that process, the inspector described it, and the timeline highlighted its records rather than the relationship's. A click on an edge that stands for one relationship now chooses it, as choosing its row does, and the relationship is the selection. Its edge is haloed in the selection's accent and its row is chosen in the table. The inspector names its two ends, explains it in its tooltip's words and says what opens it. The timeline highlights its records, as it does a selected process's: those of the one channel it rests on, at both ends, or an RPC relationship's linked calls, read by its key. Choosing anything else, unchoosing it in the table, clearing the selection or moving to another rung lets it go, with its halo and its highlight. A new count, a brush or bytes read restate its row but keep the choice by key, where a rebuilt list could drop it. An aggregate edge stands for several relationships and chooses none.

Revision 330 completes the choice revision 329 made possible (§6.4, I5). With a relationship chosen, the inspector's evidence card still said a process was selected and that no evidence was, because it knew only processes, groups and aggregates. It now states the relationship: the records its row counts, from both ends, and what was sent across its channel, read for the scope as a process's bytes are. For an RPC relationship it says instead that a call carries no size. At the machine rung E lists the records behind what is selected, a process's or a group's. With a relationship chosen it now lists the relationship's: those of the one channel it rests on, or the calls its links join, read by its key. A relationship resting on several channels names no one scope, so E there reads the machine's records, as with nothing chosen; its row opens its source process, whose rows list them.

Revision 331 root-causes the warm-aggregate allocation test's second intermittent failure (R11). In one full Debug suite the interval count measured 149,976 bytes at 10,000 rows and 168,272 at 30,000, which the test read as 0.9 bytes a row. Six re-runs passed. In 600 isolated calls the 10,000-row count allocated 168,352 bytes on its calling thread every time, so the failing run's smaller figure was 18,376 bytes light, not the larger one heavy. That is the worker's tally: 100 instances by 22 mechanism slots of eight bytes, its channels and the loop's bookkeeping. A query counts its segments side by side, and the test measured only the calling thread. When the pool took the single segment, the tally was allocated where the test did not look. The test now measures every thread's allocations, which its collection's running alone keeps clean, and takes the least of five runs rather than three. A per-row allocation made on a pool thread, which the old measure could miss, is now caught: one byte a row in the interval or focused count's segment pass fails it.

Revision 332 audits the windows beside the main one as revision 131 audits the main one (R15, §6.5). The audit walks the tree UI Automation exposes. Every element a screen reader lands on, one the keyboard can focus or a list item, must have a name, and none may be named by a record's fields. It covered only the main window. The content, raw record and channel windows had never been audited, nor the investigation window's four pages, its eight dialogs, or the seven prompts the windows build. All of them passed but the raw record window. Its record, the text a reader lands on first, had no name; it is now named for what it holds.

Revision 333 makes each control's spoken name begin with the words it shows (R15, §6.5; WCAG 2.5.3, label in name). About fifty buttons, toggles, menu items and choices across every window were named by descriptions that left out their own label. "Show source records (E)" was announced as "Show this rung's source records in the ladder", and "Withdraw alignment" as "Withdraw the selected session's alignment". A person using a screen reader heard words the screen did not show. A person driving the app by voice, saying what they see, could not reach those controls at all. Each is now named by what it shows. Its description moves to the help text a screen reader reads after the name, so nothing it said is lost. The theme menu reads "Light theme" rather than "Theme: Light", and Back is named by the rung it returns to. The audits enforce it. A control that shows a label must be named by it first, less a shortcut in brackets, a trailing ellipsis and an access key. A symbol such as the rate toggle's "/s" is no label a person says, so its name says what it stands for. Tests that find a control by name now find it as a screen reader hears it.

Revision 334 makes each window's status line announce what it says (R15, §6.5). The investigation window, its dialogs and the content, record and channel windows each report in a status line what an action did or found: "Saved as 'The first datagrams'.", a refusal, "Reading every session's connections…". A screen reader heard none of it unless its user went looking, so a save, a refusal or a finished search passed in silence. Each such line is now a polite live region, which a screen reader announces as it changes without moving the keyboard. That covers the status lines, the candidate search's summary, a comparison's answer, and the main window's capture headline. The main window's detail line stays quiet, since it counts a package's progress by the percent. The audits hold to it. A text named as a status line must be announced, and every window must announce something, but for the prompts and the package chooser, which reports its save in a prompt of its own.

Revision 335 puts a process's count one action from the rule behind it (§6.8, R22). Since revision 323 a relationship has said which rule derived it, but nothing said how a process's records came to be its own. A record names its process by PID, and process-binding, version 4, binds it to the holder of that PID whose lifetime holds it. A reused PID's later holder binds such a record only as a candidate, since it could be a late record of an earlier holder, and the default evidence policy counts no candidate. So a later holder counted only its own creation and exit, and its row read as a quiet process although it had sent. Each process now carries which holder of its PID it was, of how many, and how many of its records the policy left out of its total, from the derivation or its checkpoint alike. For a selected process the inspector states, beside its lineage, the rule and version that bound its records, how strongly, what was left out and why, and the capture's coverage. These are the whole session's facts, as it says, whatever the scope. A group's, a channel's and a timeline cell's explanations remain.

Revision 336 explains a paired channel where it is chosen (§6.8, R4). Chosen among a process's rows, a TCP channel was described by its endpoints and direction, with nothing of how its two ends came to be one connection. Each channel now carries the rule that paired it, how strongly the pairing holds, and whether the capture saw the connection open and close at both ends, from the derivation or its checkpoint alike, and `icat channels --json` writes them too. The inspector states them and the capture's coverage, and last the key E lists its records by, since a key that long buries any sentence after it. "Open before the capture and after it" says what a channel's count could not have seen, in the words a one-sided connection already used. The explanation follows the inspector's selection. On a channel's own rung the inspector still describes the process it was opened from, so the process's explanation is stated there. A group's and a timeline cell's explanations remain.

Revision 337 explains an executable group where it is chosen (§6.8). A group's row counts its members' own records, but nothing said how its members came to be one group or what its total left out. Chosen, a group now says it gathers the processes whose records name one executable, compared without case. For the processes whose records name none, it says they are gathered apart rather than under a guessed name. It names the rule that bound each member's records. When a reused PID's later holder is among them, it says how many records the evidence policy left out of its total. The tour's groups illustrate, and nothing explains them. Of §6.8's marks, a timeline cell is now the only one explained by its hover card and the interval table alone.

Revision 338 puts the evidence policy one action from the explanation that names what it left out (§6.8, R22). Revisions 335 and 337 say where a reused PID's later holder's records were left out of a total. Nothing could count them, though every query has taken a policy since revision 31. Wherever its explanation says records were left out, the inspector now offers "Count them as candidates". One click projects the shown generation again under the candidate policy and keeps the rung, selection, pins and interval, as a newer publication of the session does. Every read the window then makes binds under the policy its overview was projected under: a brushed count, a byte, call or peer ranking, the timeline's focus and lanes, a process's RPC, HTTP and one-sided connections, the records E lists and an export of them. A live capture projects each later publication under it. While candidates count, the rail says so in accent ink, a process's or group's explanation says its total includes them, and an export says so in its caveats. One more click, "Count correlated evidence only", returns to the default. A session opened or captured afterwards starts there too, since a setting is remembered per workspace. Direct evidence only, and conflicting bindings too, stay with `icat`'s `--evidence-policy`.

Revision 339 keeps the evidence policy where §26.3 keeps a session's other settings: in the investigation it was opened from (`workspace-v13`). Revision 338's choice lasted only while the session was open, so a session reopened from its investigation showed other totals than the person had left, and nothing said why. A member's layout now keeps its evidence policy beside its pins and ranking. Opened from the investigation again, the session is projected under the kept policy before its first view, so its totals never show the default first and then change. Its status says what was put back ("which put back 1 pin, its ranking by bytes sent per second and its counting of candidates"), and the rail says candidates count, as it does whenever they do. Opened on its own, it counts correlated evidence. Only `IncludeCandidates` is kept: correlated evidence is the default and keeps nothing, and a file that names it, or a policy no view offers, is refused, so a reader never puts back a policy it cannot show. A version 12 file is read as one whose layouts count correlated evidence, and written as version 13. `icat workspace show` states a layout that counts candidates (`workspace-resolution-v15`).

Revision 340 names a reused PID's holders apart wherever the window names a process (§7.2, R5). Two processes that held PID 100 in turn read alike: two "client.exe" rows over "PID 100 · created during capture", and two lanes named "client.exe · PID 100", though one of them binds its records only as candidates (revision 335). Each is now numbered by its lifecycle epoch, as `icat processes` numbers it: "PID 100 #1" and "PID 100 #2" in its row, its lane, its graph node and card, the inspector, and the crumb and filter that opening it adds. E pressed with a process selected at the machine rung named its scope by the executable alone, so two instances of one executable read alike there whether or not they shared a PID; it now names it as its row does. A PID no other process held keeps its plain "PID 200". `icat overview` names its busiest processes so too, since it shares the window's caption.

Revision 341 titles each end of a graph edge's card as the relationship table names it (§6.3, R5). The card named its ends by their node labels, so a relationship between two instances of one executable read "chrome.exe ↔ chrome.exe", while its row in the relationship table, and the inspector once it was chosen, named each end with its PID. A process end is now titled as its node's own card titles it, "Browser · PID 8204 ↔ API host · PID 5120", a reused PID's holder numbered; an aggregate end keeps its label.

Revision 342 gives each timeline lane its own scale, one click from the legend (§6.2's normalization scope, §6.8). Every lane was read against the busiest bar of any lane, so beside a busy process a quiet one drew only its floor, and its shape could not be read. The legend now keys the heights - "HEIGHT records per second, one scale for every lane" - beside a Per lane toggle. On, each lane's bars are read against that lane's own busiest bar in view, at every rung that draws lanes: the machine's mechanisms, a group's processes, a process's directions and a channel's ends, and their bytes under a byte ranking. The legend then says "each lane to its own peak", the axis states no single peak, and each lane's card says its height reads against the lane's own busiest bar, which no other lane's heights compare with. One scale stays the default, since only it lets heights compare. A newer publication of the session keeps the choice, as it keeps the ranking; an investigation does not keep it yet. §6.2 asked for `vScale` in the legend too: the axis and the cards state it, since it changes with every pan and zoom, and a legend that changed with them would be laid out again each time.

Revision 343 keeps each lane's own scale where §26.3 keeps a session's other settings: in the investigation it was opened from (`workspace-v14`), beside its pins, ranking and evidence policy. Opened from the investigation again, the session's lanes are read as they were left, and its status lists what was put back ("which put back 1 pin, its ranking by bytes sent per second, its counting of candidates and each timeline lane on its own scale"). The status now says its "pins and view settings" are kept, since the list of them keeps growing. The window keeps those settings as one record, compared and written together. A version 13 file is read as one whose lanes share one scale, and written as version 14; `icat workspace show` states a layout that reads each lane on its own scale (`workspace-resolution-v16`).

Revision 344 lets either main pane fill the column by an explicit command, as §6.1's collapse floor allows (§6.1). Each pane's header has an Expand toggle, and F11 expands the pane holding the keyboard; the toggle again, or F11 from anywhere, gives both their places back at the split the person dragged. Nothing hides a pane by itself. The section's layout rules are amended to the shell as built: §6.1 had the two panes side by side, 50/50 by width, stacking below a 1,100-pixel window. The window places the ranked rail and the inspector beside the panes instead of beneath them, so side by side each pane would have under half of the 900 pixels between them at 1,456 pixels wide: a timeline that narrow loses the time resolution its columns need, and a graph that narrow its labels. Stacked, each keeps the column's whole width, and the splitter and Expand give either the height. The split, and which pane is expanded, are not kept per workspace yet; revision 360 keeps them.

Revision 345 has E list what the inspector's evidence card counts below the machine rung too (§6.4, I5). Revision 330 had E follow a chosen relationship, as the machine rung already followed a selected process, but only at the machine rung: at a group's rung the card above "Show source records (E)" counted a process chosen among the group's members, or a chosen relationship, while E listed every member's records. E now lists a chosen relationship's records at any rung, and a process chosen among a group's members, its filter saying at which rung it was chosen. At a process's rung, a channel chosen among its rows leaves the card counting the process, and E lists the process's records, as before; Enter opens the channel.

Revision 346 has E list a multi-selection's records, as Enter does (§6.7). With several processes chosen, the card above E counted the set, but E listed the one process the keyboard was on, or the rung's own records. E now lists exactly the chosen processes' records, the set its visible filter, and the set is spent. Choosing a set and choosing a relationship each let the other go, so the card and E never have both to choose between.

Revision 347 has an investigation's window say what the investigation keeps of each session's view (§26.3). `icat workspace show` listed each member's pins, ranking, evidence policy and lane scale, but the window that opens members listed none of them. A session it opened with its rows ranked by bytes, or a reused PID's candidates counted, surprised a person until the notice said so. Each member's row now ends with what opening it puts back, "Opens with 1 node pinned on its graph and its rows ranked by bytes sent per second, as it was left here.", and changes as the main window writes it, without reading the investigation again. The command, the notice and the row use one set of words, defined beside the layout, and name only what differs from a session opened on its own.

Revision 348 has a row chosen among a process's rows be what the evidence card counts, the timeline highlights and E lists (§6.4, I5). The inspector described a chosen channel, one-sided connection, RPC channel or the process's HTTP exchanges, and a channel's pairing explanation said "E lists its records at both ends", yet the card beneath counted the process and E listed the process's records. The card now counts the row's records, a channel's as its own rung states them ("240 observed records at its two ends · 7.7 KB sent across"). The timeline highlights them, and E lists them, its filter naming where the row was chosen. Clicking the process's own node, which changed nothing while a row was described, now makes the process the selection again. On a channel's own rung the card's selection is still the process the rung was opened from, and its level line counts the channel's records, which E lists.

Revision 349 tests what revision 342 left untested: the live preview's bars on each lane's own scale (§6.2, §12). On one scale a preview bar is read against the published bars' peak, so previewed records at half the busiest lane's rate stand half as tall, never as tall as the preview's own busiest. On each lane's own scale a lane's preview is read against that lane's own busiest bar, so a quiet lane's preview, busier than its own bars, reaches the top of its row. Beside byte lanes the preview, which counts records, keeps a records scale of its own. A test in the window reads each from the pixels drawn, and five mutations of the drawing, each breaking one of these, fail it.

Revision 350 has the timeline state its time base, which §6.2 asks for at all times. Its axis read "10.0 µs" to "19.1 µs" with nothing to say what those count from. Between its first and last instants it now says "session time". Where the capture recorded its wall clock (`contracts/clock-calibration-v1.md`), the axis adds the moment session time 0 was by that clock, in the reader's zone with its offset from UTC: "session time since 09/29/2026 12:00:00 UTC". Where the axis has room for less, it says "session time" alone. The timeline's spoken status adds the same words after the range in view. An import, which recorded no wall clock, says "session time" alone and guesses no moment. The axis's last instant is now measured rather than estimated, as this section asks of every label. A host-local wall-clock axis, which the rule also names, is not offered: the instants stay session time, and the wall clock says only when they began.

Revision 351 has `icat timeline` state its time base in the window's words: a "Time base" field after its interval, "session time since 09/29/2026 12:00:00 UTC" where the capture recorded its wall clock and "session time" where it did not. Its JSON document is unchanged. It also corrects §6.7's row for `Esc`, which said Esc clears the selection, against §3.2 and the window, where Esc ascends a rung and clears the selection only at the machine rung. The row now says what Esc does, and that cancelling a drag in progress is not built.

Revision 352 has Esc cancel a drag in progress (§6.7), which revision 351 recorded as not built. The window takes Esc before any pane does, and it ascended mid-drag: a person who began a pan, a brush or a node's drag by mistake could only finish it. Esc now cancels the gesture where it began, and only with none in progress does it ascend. A pan, or a move or resize of the minimap's brush, gives back the view it began from, a press beside the brush included. A brush being drawn is dropped, and a press selects nothing when released. A node's drag pins nothing, and the press's selection stands. Two presses at one point still make a double click, which zooms, even with a cancelled drag between them.

Revision 353 has the evidence card name the records E lists when nothing is selected (§6.4, I5). With no selection the card read "Selected process: No evidence selected", while E lists the rung's own records: the whole session at the machine rung, a group's instances at its rung. It now reads "Records E lists", over "Every admitted record in this session" or "Records owned by the 3 instances of client.exe", in the words the evidence rung then uses. The words take the scope the rows count, a brushed range included. The tour keeps its card, since it holds no records, and so does the evidence rung, whose records are already listed.

Revision 354 tests what revision 338 left untested and fixes what testing it found (§6.8). Each read a process's rows, the call and peer rankings and the lanes make now has a test under each policy: a reused PID's later holder's RPC channel, its calls, its HTTP exchanges and its one-sided connection are its own, ranked, laned and drawn only where candidates count. Where none counts, its rung lists no row, yet its empty-rung message said its one-sided and other-mechanism records were "still one step away", while E lists only its creation. That empty rung now says its records were left out as candidates, in the words its binding explanation uses, with "Count them as candidates" beside E's button: what is true, why, what remains possible and the one action that changes it, as §6.8 asks of a designed state. The call rankings' note, which counts the calls no admitted process holds, no longer says "1 more completed calls belong". A count should agree with its noun wherever a person reads it; the status document lists the places that still do not.

Revision 355 has a channel's own rung count, on the inspector's card, the channel E lists from there (§6.4, I5). Revision 348 made a channel, RPC channel or HTTP exchanges chosen among a process's rows what the card counts. Opened with Enter, its rung's level line counted the channel and E listed its records, but the card went back to the process it was opened from. It now heads "This channel", "This RPC channel" or "These HTTP exchanges" and counts what the row counted where it was chosen, over the scope the rung counts, a brush included. The inspector still names the process, which the channel belongs to. A call chosen on an RPC channel's rung, or a process clicked in its graph, leaves the card on the channel, since E still lists the channel's records. A chosen relationship, or both ends chosen together, is what the card counts instead, as E then lists them.

Revision 356 makes an aggregate chosen in the graph what the card counts, the timeline highlights and E lists (§6.4, I5). An aggregate is processes with no relationship counted in one node, a group's undrawn members, or everything outside a rung's focus. The timeline already highlighted its members' records. The card counted its relationships, "No admitted paired TCP relationship among these processes" when it had none, and E listed the rung's own records. It now stands for its processes, as the same processes chosen together do. The card counts their own records, their relationships and what they sent and received, as a multi-selection's does, and E lists exactly their records through the same set filter. On a channel's own rung it is counted rather than the channel, as a multi-selection is. A collapsed executable group was already the group itself.

Revision 357 has a count agree with its noun and verb wherever a person reads it, as revision 354's status listed. A count of one is common in a small capture or a narrow brush. A one-record connection, a single process, one record outside the capture or one stop without its start read "1 records", "1 across 1 PIDs" or "1 stops in scope are paired". The window, the command line, the metric caveats both state, and the overview's disclosures now say "1 record", "1 PID, never reused", "1 record in scope has its other end outside this capture's records … It is left out" and "1 TCP row has no admitted peer". `CountText` gives every layer one way to say a count with its noun and the verb that agrees with it. Plural wording is unchanged. A caveat whose sentence goes on about its records, "they are candidates", "include them", says it of one.

Revision 358 counts a double click only after a plain click (§6.7). The platform makes a double click of any two presses close in time and place, so a second press where a pan, a brush or a drag given up with Esc began zoomed the timeline. Two quick pans begun at one place were a pan and a zoom, and a node's drag cancelled and begun again opened the node, leaving its rung. Revision 352 recorded the case. The timeline and the graph now remember whether the last press ended as a plain click, neither a drag nor cancelled. Only then does a second press at its place zoom, or open what is there. Two plain clicks still zoom and still open.

Revision 359 has E from an aggregate name it (§6.4). Since revision 356, E from an aggregate chosen in the graph lists its processes' records through the set filter a multi-selection uses. Its chip and scope read "4 selected processes", as if the person had chosen four processes one by one. The filter now carries the aggregate's name, so the chip reads "Records of: Rest of the machine" and the scope "Records owned by the 4 processes of Rest of the machine". The scope counts the members a newer generation still holds. A multi-selection reads as before.

Revision 360 keeps the window's panes where §6.1's persistence row and §26.3 put a workspace's layout: in the investigation a session was opened from (`workspace-v15`). The split a person dragged between the graph and the timeline, and the pane Expand or F11 let fill the column, lasted only while the window was open. They are the window's rather than a session's, so an investigation keeps them once, for every session opened from it: the graph's share of the height the two share, to a ten-thousandth, and the pane filling the column, if one does. A drag of the splitter is kept once it is let go, a key on the splitter as it moves it, and a pane filling the column or both given their places back as it happens. A session opened from the investigation puts them back, and its notice lists them with what else was put back ("which put back 1 node pinned on its graph and the timeline filling the column"). An investigation that keeps none leaves the panes as they are, as a session opened on its own does. Its window says beneath its sessions what opening them puts back, "Its sessions open with the timeline filling the column and the graph at 37% of the panes' height when both are shown, as the panes were left here.", and its least height grows by those two lines, so two sessions stay in view. A split now leaves each pane its least height, 160 logical pixels for the graph and 180 for the timeline. A drag could squeeze either to nothing, hiding it although only an explicit command may (§6.1's collapse floor), and a kept share could then hide one on a smaller screen. A version 14 file is read as one that keeps no panes and written as version 15. A file whose panes keep nothing, give a pane none of the height, or name a pane the window does not have, is refused. `icat workspace show` states them before each session's layout (`workspace-resolution-v17`). Lane grouping, pinned lanes and column widths, which the persistence row also lists, have no control in the window yet, so nothing of them is kept.

Revision 361 makes I18's reader test independent of the thread pool (§13.6). Revision 360's full Release run failed "a reader waits out a writer's removal instead of failing its lease" once, its reader giving up after the second it waits. The test's writer let go of the guard 200 ms in from a thread-pool continuation, which runs only once a worker is free. Load alone did not repeat it: 25 runs of the storage tests beside three other suites looping, and 20 pinned to one processor shared with six busy loops, all passed, since the scheduler wakes a sleeping thread at once. Holding the pool repeated it every time: with the pool capped at its four workers and eight blocking work items queued, the reader gave up 1,004–1,013 ms in with the suite's message, and the continuation let go of the guard only after that, 1–42 ms later. The writer now lets go from a thread of its own, and under the same hold the reader had its lease 225–241 ms in. The store is unchanged: a removal that really holds the guard past the second is still reported rather than waited on.

Revision 362 states beneath the inspector's time scope what the capture covered there (R21, §6.5's capture coverage). With a range chosen, the card stated the range and its counts but not whether the capture saw it. Brushed between two epochs' readings, a process read "No own record admitted · 0 paired TCP observations" and the level "0 observations", and the card's only coverage words, "Coverage over the session: partial gap", were the whole session's, so a range the capture delivered nothing in read as a quiet one; only the ranked rows said "unknown coverage". The card now says it under the range, mechanism by mechanism as metrics-v1 §7 asks of an all-mechanism answer, never as one rolled-up state: "Coverage: covered for process lifecycle and TCP · no other mechanism collected"; a state short of covered with the fact behind it, "a partial gap, not extrapolated, for process lifecycle and TCP: the session reported 1 lost event, which may be any mechanism's"; and, where nothing is known of any mechanism for one reason, that reason once with what it means, "Coverage unknown: outside the readings the capture's sources delivered, so a count of none here is not proof of inactivity". The whole session's comes with its overview and a range's with its counts (`SessionIntervalCounts.MechanismCoverage`). Until a range is counted the card says its coverage is being read, rather than carry another scope's, and a range that could not be counted, or that no source is there to count, has none said for it. A live publication's counts standing in for the same range bring their coverage with them, in plain ink, since the new generation's ledger did not judge them. A limit is in the caution ink, as the health strip's; a generation without a ledger, which has judged nothing, says so in plain ink (§6.6). An interval narrower than one reading of a coarse clock holds no reading, so every mechanism is unknown there rather than given the whole session's coverage. E's empty rung now points to the card's statement instead of the health strip, whose intervals are the timeline's rather than the scope's. `icat evidence` and `icat export` still state no coverage for the range they read.

Revision 363 has `icat evidence` state its page's coverage as the inspector does (R21, R18). A page that listed no record in a range the capture delivered nothing in printed "Rows on page 0" and a caveat sending the reader to the session's coverage ledger. Each page now carries each mechanism's coverage over its time scope, its interval or the whole session, with the fact behind it (`evidence-page-v3`), and the command says it beneath the page's fields in the inspector's words: "Coverage unknown: outside the readings the capture's sources delivered, so a count of none here is not proof of inactivity". The words, and the placing of an interval on the capture's readings, are now in one place both layers call (`CoverageText`), as a count is said through `CountText`. The caveat says what the page's coverage states rather than where to look. The query a cursor names is unchanged, so a cursor issued before still continues. `icat export --interval` still states no coverage for its range.

Revision 364 says a coverage state in words wherever a caveat or a note states one, through one mapping (R5). Sentences printed an enumeration's name: under a call ranking the note read "RPC's capture coverage over this scope is UnknownCoverage: this generation publishes no coverage ledger.", a rate's caveat "Capture coverage for Tcp over the selected interval is PartialGap: …", and `icat metric` "Tcp covered; Process lifecycle covered; 19 not collected" beside the projection "Tcp", where the window says TCP. A mechanism's coverage is now one sentence form, `SessionCoverage.Sentence`, naming the mechanism as a sentence names it and ending with the fact that decided its state: "RPC's coverage over this scope is unknown: this generation publishes no coverage ledger.", "TCP has a partial gap over the selected interval, not extrapolated: …". The ranking notes, the export's call caveat, the metric caveats and `icat metric`'s answer for one mechanism say it so; its answer for every mechanism states its coverage as the inspector does, and its projection names the mechanism as a lane does. A mechanism's names are one mapping in the domain, `MechanismText`, beside `CountText`. The window's row labels for a state, kept twice, and `icat timeline`'s coverage column, which prints the enumeration's name, are not yet that mapping.

Revision 365 gives a coverage state and a mechanism one set of names in the window's rows and the command line's tables, as revision 364 did for sentences (R5). The window kept three copies of a state's label, two for rows ("unknown coverage") and one for hover cards ("Coverage: unknown"), and the command line printed enumerations: `icat timeline`'s table read "Tcp" and "PartialGap", `icat session` "Mechanisms Tcp", a coverage table of "Tcp Covered" and a list of what was not collected beginning "ProcessLifecycle, ThreadLifecycle, Udp", and `icat capabilities` and a content request's preview the same. A state now has one value, said where something names it as coverage ("unknown"), and one label for where it stands alone ("unknown coverage"), in `CoverageStateText`; a mechanism has one name for a lane and one for a sentence, which lowers a capital that is no name or abbreviation ("named pipe", but "Unix socket"), in `MechanismText`. JSON documents keep the enumerations' names, which a tool reads. `icat session` also names the epoch each line of losses is of, where it printed every epoch's under one unlabelled heading.

Revision 366 finds why §6.2's lane-scale window test failed now and then at a pixel check after a descent, the second time in revision 365's Release run, and takes the cause away (§13.6). The test rests the pointer on the machine row to read its card, then shrinks the window to its least size and restores it. Whether the timeline still has the pointer over it afterwards depends on when the window reads the pointer's place again, and the headless suite runs window tests together on one UI thread, so another test's work decides that. Run alone the test passed 60 times out of 60, but three copies of it running together failed 2 rounds out of 15, twice: each time the pointer still rested at the quiet process's creation, and the card it drew there covered the lower part of the bar the check read, so its two pixels differed. The product was right, since a card follows what lies under a resting pointer; the test now takes the pointer off the timeline before every pixel it reads, and says first that no card is drawn. Three copies together then passed 45 rounds out of 45. Revision 357's step, which asked for the timeline's detail at once after each descent and waited for it, is gone: this test never zooms, and at the whole extent a focus is counted on the overview's columns whatever the plot's width, so the request it feared was always the one already answered.

Revision 367 has an export state its scope's coverage as the inspector does beneath its time scope (R21, R18). R21 forbids exporting absence as observed zero, yet a ranked export of a range the capture delivered nothing in listed every row at 0 observations beside the disclosure alone, and an evidence export of the range said "Every record of this scope is included." of none and stopped there. Each export's context now carries each mechanism's coverage over its time scope, its interval or the whole session: `intercat-export-v1`'s JSON context gains a `coverage` list, each mechanism with its state and the fact behind it, named as the enumerations name them for a tool, an addition a reader of the contract may ignore. Its caveats end with the inspector's words, such as "Coverage unknown: outside the readings the capture's sources delivered, so a count of none here is not proof of inactivity", which `icat export` prints beneath its report. The window and `icat export` read the same coverage, the counts' own for ranked rows and the read's own for records, so their files stay byte-identical. CSV, which carries no caveats, keeps each ranked row's coverage as before, and the redacted sharing report, whose allowlist admits no source caveat, keeps its rows' and states no scope's, until revision 372 has both state it.

Revision 368 gives a record's kind and layer, the reason a field holds no value, and a capability's state, tier and overhead one display mapping each, which the window and the command line's tables read (R5, R3). `icat session`'s rows printed "RequestStart" and a missing size "unknown (NotExposed)", its summary "Layers Transport", `icat metric`'s evidence rows "Tcp" and "Receive", and `icat capabilities`' tables "DisabledByProfile", "TrafficVisualization" and "Moderate". The window, for its part, called every missing size "size not exposed", whether its source withheld it, the capture's profile left it out, the capture was denied it, a lost event took it, its schema could not be read or a package redacted it. Each is now said for what it is, "size redacted" or "size lost with its event", in the rail, a record's spoken name and the inspector, whose Size field says the reason alone, and `icat` says the same inside "unknown (…)". Kinds read "request start", layers "transport", states "disabled by the profile", and tiers as §14.2 names them, "traffic visualization"; a code this version does not know is said by its number. JSON documents keep the names for a tool, and §23 now says which is which.

Revision 369 gives a direction and an operation's state one set of words each (R5). A direction had three: a channel row said "both directions" where the timeline's direction rows said "Bidirectional" and a hover card "bidirectional", and the rows called a code no version knows "No data direction", a guess §23 forbids. Every direction now reads as one word, "outbound", "inbound", "bidirectional", "unknown direction" or "no data direction", raised as a label, and an unknown code by its number. The tour's L4 operations printed their enumerations, "Send Outbound · 2 s" and "OpenAtBoundary · no byte domain", in the first view a new user explores; they now read "Send · outbound · 2 s" and "open at capture end · no byte domain", an operation's state in the words a real RPC call's state already used: an operation whose completion the capture did not see is open at capture end, censored rather than failed, and one whose start it did not see is "start not observed".

Revision 370 has a channel's own rung name, in the inspector, the channel its card counts (§6.4, §6.5, I5). Revision 355 left the inspector on the process a channel was opened from: opened with Enter, a channel read "client.exe" and "PID 100 · created during capture" above a card headed "This channel", and its explanation went back to how the process's records were counted, the process's lineage beneath it, where a step earlier, chosen among the process's rows, the same channel was named by its endpoints and whom it connects, and its pairing was explained. The inspector now names an opened paired channel, RPC channel or HTTP exchanges as its row named it where it was chosen, rebuilt in that rung's words, and says how a paired channel was paired; the process's lineage steps aside. The process a rung was reached with is its context, not a choice made there: one chosen there afterwards, as in the graph, is described instead, while the card still counts what E lists. A newer publication of a live session, restored where the last one stood, keeps describing the channel.

Revision 371 gives a selected record its own coverage in the inspector (R21, §6.5's capture coverage). A record was stated by its time, owner, endpoints, size, content, source and quality, and nothing said whether the capture could have missed records beside it: a send read in an epoch whose session lost events read like one read where the capture lost nothing. Its fields now end with its mechanism's coverage where it was read, the epoch whose delivered readings hold its reading: "covered: 2 records from its 1 admitted descriptor, and nothing was reported lost", or "partial gap, not extrapolated: the session reported 1 lost event, which may be any mechanism's". An evidence page reads the ledger its generation published once, with its records, and answers each record from it; the latest page's serves the whole list, since a later ledger only adds epochs. A generation without a ledger says it judged nothing, beside every record. `icat evidence` states each mechanism's coverage over an interval as narrow as one reading, the record's among them.

Revision 372 has the exports' CSV and the redacted sharing report state their scope's coverage too (R21, §11.3). Revision 367 left both silent. A CSV of an evidence range the capture delivered nothing in was its header and no line, which said neither what it held nor that its none was no proof of inactivity, and a sharing report's recipient, who cannot open the session to look, could not tell a mechanism nothing used from one nothing collected. Both CSVs now end every line with two columns, after every column they had, so each of those keeps its place: `row_present`, and `scope_coverage`, the inspector's words for the scope's coverage. An `intercat-export-v1` CSV of a scope with no row writes one line that holds its context, `row_present=false`, as the sharing report's always has, and a ranked one names its ranking there. The sharing report's JSON context gains the `coverage` list the detailed export's has, each mechanism's state and the fact behind it, and a `coverageSummary` in the same words, and `icat export --share-redacted` prints them beneath its report as the detailed export does. This widens the report's allowlist deliberately ([`SHARING-REPORT-REDACTION.md`](SHARING-REPORT-REDACTION.md)). A reason is one of `coverage-v2` §4's fixed templates, whose only variable parts are counts, so it holds no provider name or identifier from the ledger it was judged from; the redacted session package already carries the same states and reasons (`redacted-session-v1` §6). A leak test judges every reason the rule can give from ledgers whose one provider is named with a secret, and pins the reasons, so a new or reworded one is weighed against the policy before a report carries it.

Revision 373 has every listing of what a capture's records establish say what the capture covered of what it lists (R21).
