R21 states coverage independently from data, yet `icat operations` told a session with no RPC call record that "its capture did not include the RPC source", and one with no ALPC record that the capture "collected no ALPC", each read from the records' absence, which a capture that collected the source and saw nothing would hold too; `icat exchanges`, `icat channels` and the Desktop's channel browser said nothing of whether what they list none of could have been seen. Each now reads its mechanisms' coverage over the session from the ledger, RPC's and ALPC's, HTTP's, TCP's, lists it in its JSON (`coverage`: each mechanism, its state and the fact behind it) and says it in the words `icat metric` uses: "RPC was not collected over the session: no admitted descriptor records it." A listing that resolves no call's other end says the capture did not collect ALPC only when the ledger says so, and otherwise that the generation holds no ALPC record, with ALPC's coverage. The channel browser announces TCP's coverage as the reason an empty list holds none, and states it beside the caveat under a list. A call's other end with no ALPC record to follow, and a metric that needs one, say that rather than what the capture collected (`operations-v1` §5c).

Revision 374 has `icat processes` say what the capture covered, and never that a process sent nothing (R21, R18, P1). It answers from the grouped metric `icat metric --group-by process` reads, which states each mechanism's coverage beside its counts, yet it stated none; and for an instance asked about by PID it said "It sent and received no transport bytes in this session." whenever none of its transport bytes was measured, so a send whose size its source withheld, or a capture that collected no transport at all, read as a process that sent nothing. Its report now states the coverage in the words `icat metric` uses of the same answer, its JSON carries the same coverage document (whether a ledger was published, the rule, and each mechanism's state and reason), and an instance none of whose transport bytes was measured is said to be that: "No transport byte it sent or received was measured in this session, which is not proof it sent or received none."

Revision 375 has `icat overview` say what the capture covered over the session, mechanism by mechanism, in the words the window's inspector states beneath its time scope (R18, R21). It said only whether a coverage ledger was published, though its bundle carries each mechanism's coverage, the list the window's snapshot reads, so a person learned that the ledger existed and not what it covered. It now says, for example, "Coverage: covered for TCP · no other mechanism collected", or that a generation without a ledger judged nothing; the field that only named the ledger is gone, since the words say as much.

Revision 376 says why a record binds to no process in one set of words, and what direct evidence only leaves out as what it is (R5, R22). The window's evidence rows and the command line each had their own words for a binding's reason, "earlier than this PID's first lifecycle record" beside "before the PID's first lifecycle record", and `icat operations` and `icat exchanges` named an unattributed group by the enumeration itself, "PID 1960 · NotAdmittedByPolicy". One mapping (`BindingText`) now serves the window's rows and `icat processes`, `metric`, `operations` and `exchanges`, and says a reason this version does not know by its number. Its words for a binding the policy does not admit no longer call it a reused PID's candidate: the reason marks any binding weaker than the policy asks for, so under direct evidence only it holds every record but a lifecycle record's own. The metric caveat made the same mistake, telling `--evidence-policy DirectOnly` that every record it left out lay in a reused PID's later instance; it now says that those records bind by where their readings fall, not by a lifecycle record of the process, and that the default policy admits those in a PID's first instance.

Revision 377 has `icat session` say its coverage ledger's facts in the words its coverage reasons use (R5). It turned the enumerations' names into words by splitting them, so an imported file's epoch read "Etl import", its losses "Source session 0 records; Consumer buffers 0 buffers" beside reasons that say "the session reported 1 lost event, which may be any mechanism's", and a delivery the policy did not admit "Unrequested provider". One mapping (`CoverageLedgerText`) now says how an epoch was acquired, "ETL import" or "live capture"; what each loss layer reported, its reported zeros included and in layer order, in the words the coverage reasons use; and why a delivery was not admitted, "a provider the capture did not request". A value this version does not know is said by its number.

Revision 378 has `icat metric` name what an answer counts in the window's words (R5). It printed "Basis: Source observations" by splitting the enumeration's name, "Byte domain: TransportObserved", "Accounting: SendSide - sender-accounted: …", and a table headed "Contributions in TransportObserved" whose sides read "SendSide" and "ReceiveSide", while the window's ranking selector heads a basis "SOURCE OBSERVATIONS" and a record's size says "carried by the transport". One mapping each (`MeasurementText.Basis`, `MeasurementText.Domain`, `MetricCompatibility.Label`) now serves the selector and its caption, a record's size, and `icat metric`, which says "source observations", "carried by the transport" and "sender-accounted: each transfer is measured at its sending end", and names its table's sides "sender-accounted" and "receiver-accounted". `icat measure` and `icat verify`, which run on Windows, name a computed tier as the window does.

Revision 379 has `icat processes` say a process's transport bytes as the window's ranked rows do: the measured sum, "unmeasured" when its records recorded no size, or "no sends" and "no receives" when it made none (R21, R18). It printed "-" for both, so a process whose sends its source left unsized read like one that sent nothing; and in a session none of whose sends measured a size every process read so, since the answer, unavailable with nothing measured, was not read at all. Its JSON now carries each instance's measured and unmeasured send and receive records (`sends`, `receives`), from the same grouped answer, whose groups an answer with nothing measured still holds.

Revision 380 has `icat`'s call, exchange and peer tables say what an empty cell means, as the window's rows do (R21, R18). `icat operations` printed "-" for the durations of a group none of whose calls was timed and for the other end of a server group no client call reached; `icat exchanges` printed it for a median none of whose exchanges was timed, and named a process whose image no record names "PID · 4242"; and `icat processes --pid` printed it for an end's bytes whether its records recorded no size or there were none, and left out every end of an instance none of whose bytes was measured. They now say "untimed", "not reached" or "no ALPC", "executable not witnessed · 4242", and "unmeasured" or "none"; each end's measured and unmeasured records are in the JSON, and an instance with no transport record at all says so, which is not proof it was idle.

Revision 381 has the investigation's merged time show and say where a session's capture saw nothing, as the session's own timeline does (R21, §6.6). Each lane counted its records over the investigation's columns, and each column already carried its capture's coverage from the ledger, but the lane drew only its bars and its words said only how many records a column held: a column a capture lost records in, or never reached, read as a quiet one, and a session without a ledger as one that did nothing outside its few columns of records. A lane now hatches each run of columns its capture did not cover as one band, across any records it saw there: through the lane where it lost records or collected none, and as a thin strip at the lane's foot where what it covered is unknown, as outside every reading it delivered. The column the cursor stands on says its coverage beside its records, and only a column with records says Enter opens them; opening a column of none says, where its capture did not cover it, that this is no proof of inactivity; each lane's sentence counts its columns in each coverage state; and the page says what the hatching means. Drawn column by column, 160 narrow columns read as a comb of outlines rather than a band, which is why a run is drawn whole.

Revision 382 has a one-sided connection, and a selection in the inspector, say a direction's bytes as an interval's row does (R21, R5, R18). A connection's line - on its process's rung, in `icat channels`, and at each end of a candidate join - read "0 B sent" when every send it recorded stated no size, and when it recorded none, with "1 transfer of no stated size" after both directions; `icat workspace correlate` printed each end's sums without even that, and its document wrote 0 for both. The inspector said "nothing received" of a selection whose records held no receive, which claims what was not observed beside a capture that did not cover it (§10.3), where the interval table already said "no receive recorded". Each direction now reads the measured sum with how many stated no size, "2 sends unmeasured" where none stated one, or "no receive recorded"; a count of one agrees with its noun; `correlate` says each end in those words, "not the only match" as the window does; and its document (`workspace-correlation-v5`) counts each direction's transfers and those of no size, and leaves out a sum none of them measured rather than writing 0.

Revision 383 has an investigation's candidate joins state what each compared capture covered of TCP and UDP, the mechanisms a candidate joins (R21, §8.3). A candidate is proposed only where another capture holds a connection's mirror, so a capture that lost records, never collected UDP, or publishes no ledger can leave a connection with no candidate though its other end was there; the window and `icat workspace correlate` said that no connection had its mirrored end in another, with nothing beside it. Each compared capture's coverage of both is now read from its ledger under the lease its connections are read under, so it is of the same generation (I16). Where one falls short, the window says so in the caution ink beneath the candidates - how, why, and that a mirror may be missing for that alone - and no candidate is said to be no proof there was none; where every one covered both, its notes say that a connection with no candidate has no mirror in what they recorded. `correlate` states each capture's state of each mechanism and the same sentences, and its document (`workspace-correlation-v6`) carries each state with the fact behind it.

Revision 384 brings the investigation's merged time to the command line, as R18 asks of every analysis result the window shows: it was the one M4 view with no `icat` form. `icat workspace timeline` draws each member's lane over the investigation's time - in the 160 columns the window draws, or `--columns`, over the whole or an `--interval` of its ticks - in the window's own words, which now live in one place both read (`InvestigationTimelineText`): each lane's place, records and coverage, the overlaps of one host's captures, and the notes where they are pinned. Beneath a lane's sentence it lists the columns that hold its records and the runs of columns its capture did not cover, which the window hatches; its document (`workspace-timeline-v1`) carries every column of every lane, in the investigation's time and the session's own, with its records and coverage, and the snapshot vector it answers (I16).

Revision 385 has `icat processes` say how each instance's records were bound, in the inspector's words, and what the evidence policy left out of a reused PID's later holder (R18, R21). The inspector explained a selected process - the rule that bound its records and how strongly, what the policy left out of its total, and the capture's coverage - while the command line listed a later holder with only its lifecycle records counted and said nothing of the records bound to it only as candidates, so it read as a quiet process; a caveat counted them for the whole session only. The explanation now lives in one place both read (`ProcessBindingText`), from the overview projected under the same policy: `icat processes` says it beneath its table for each instance the policy left records out of, with the option that counts them, and beside each instance `--pid` details; its document counts each instance's PID holders, its candidate records and those the policy withheld, and carries the sentence.

Revision 386 has `icat channels` say how each paired channel was paired, as the inspector does (R18, §6.8): the rule and version that paired its ends and how strongly - correlated, or a candidate where an end binds only as one - whether the capture saw the connection open and close, and the capture's coverage. It listed each channel by its key, its endpoints and its records alone. The words live in one place both read (`ChannelPairingText`); the window adds the key E lists its records by, and the command line already ends by naming `icat evidence --channel`. A channel of one record now reads "1 observed record".

Revision 387 lets a person pin a process's lane at the top of its group's timeline (§6.2: pin lanes). A group's lanes were drawn only in the ranked table's order, so in a group of many instances - one real capture's svchost.exe held 97 - two being compared could sit dozens of lanes apart. With a process's lane selected, `P` with the keyboard in the timeline, or the timeline header's **Pin lane (P)**, pins it: it is drawn first, beneath any lane pinned before it, with a pin's head after its name as the graph marks a pinned node, and the caption counts the group's lanes pinned ("4 process lanes, 1 pinned at the top"). Again unpins it, and it goes back to its row's place; the control says which it would do. The ranked table keeps its own order, and a lane a pin moves stays in view. Under a byte ranking the group's lane bytes were read for its instances in the order drawn, so after a pin the next change of view would have read them all again; they are now read in an order of their own. `P` anywhere else, or with no lane selected, still pins the selected graph node. A later publication of the session keeps the pins as it keeps the graph's; keeping them in the investigation, as §6.1's persistence row asks, is the next step.

Revision 388 keeps a session's pinned lanes where §6.1's persistence row and §26.3 put its graph's pins: in the investigation it was opened from (`workspace-v16`). Each member's layout lists the process instances whose lanes are pinned, in the order pinned, which is the order they are drawn in and which no sort of theirs restores. A session opened from the investigation pins them before any group's lanes are counted, so they are drawn first, and its notice lists them with what else was put back ("which put back 2 process lanes pinned on its timeline"); the investigation's window and `icat workspace show` say it in the same words, and the show command's document lists them (`workspace-resolution-v18`). Unpinning the last removes a layout that keeps nothing else. A lane of an instance a later generation does not draw stays pinned, for when it is drawn. A version 15 file is read as one that pins no lane and written as version 16; a file that pins a lane in an earlier version, twice, by the empty identity or more than 1,024 of them is refused.

Revision 389 draws only the timeline rows in view (§6.2: virtualize rows), which revision 122 left open. A group's lanes are counted up to 200 rows, of which the pane shows a few, and every repaint drew all of them: measured headless, a frame of a 200-lane group took 73 ms against 24 ms for a group of eight, and its drawing allocated 10 MB against 0.44 MB. The timeline now draws the rows its scroller shows - a group's machine row and lanes, a process's direction rows, a channel's end lanes and the machine rung's mechanism lanes - and a frame of a 200-lane group costs what one of eight lanes does. A scroll moves the timeline, which draws it again; a view that grows or moves without moving it, as when the pane is made taller or fills the column, draws again as soon as it shows a row the last repaint did not draw. Every count and peak is still computed over all the rows, so the shared scale does not change with what is in view.

Revision 390 lets a search find a group's lanes (§6.2: search lane names). In a group of a hundred lanes a person looking for one process scrolled the gutter for its name; the search found it, but Enter opened it, leaving the group's lanes. While a search is typed at a group's rung the timeline now marks the lanes of the processes it finds with a bar in the strongest ink at the gutter's edge, counts them in its caption ("120 process lanes, 1 found by the search") and scrolls the lane of the selected hit - else the first lane it finds - into view, so a PID typed is a lane shown. A process is found by the rule its search hit is ranked by, now one predicate both use (`WorkspaceSearch.Finds`), so every lane the search finds is marked, not only those of the 50 hits the rail lists. Nothing is selected: Enter still opens the hit, and a click on the lane's name selects it, where `P` pins it. A search that finds no lane of the group says so in the caption, and clearing it clears the marks.

Revision 391 writes §20.6's support bundle, which M5's support diagnostics and M13's privacy-reviewed generator ask for and nothing made: a person whose capture or view went wrong had `icat session` to send, whose document names the session's full path, the command that re-derives it and the source it came from. `icat support [<session> ...]` writes one JSON document (`support-bundle-v1`): InterCat's version, the machine's operating system, architectures, runtime and processor count, its capability report, and for each session named its folder's name, generation and files with their sizes, its rows by mechanism with what the capture covered of each in `icat session`'s words, its coverage ledger's counters, its clock and its recording. Every fact is chosen from an allowlist, so nothing a record holds can reach it: no payload, endpoint, command line, raw record, process, pipe or resource name, host or user name, path or source identity (P16). What it holds and what it leaves out are listed before anything is read or written, and `--check` lists them and does nothing else. A session it cannot read is said to be, in its reader's words with its path said as its folder, and the bundle is still made. The Desktop does not offer it yet.

Revision 392 lets the window save the support bundle, which revision 391 left to the command line: a person whose view went wrong had to find a terminal and their session's folder to make one. "Save support bundle…", beside the window's sharing actions, first asks whether to save one, saying what it holds and leaves out in the words `icat support` lists them in (R18), and only then asks where; the bundle holds the session shown, by its folder's name. The window runs no capability probe - the probe belongs to the capture sources, which the Desktop does not reference - so its bundle's `capabilities` is null, its listing does not claim a report, and the question says that `icat support` adds this machine's. Both makers now build one `SupportBundleV1` through `SupportBundle.Make` and write it with one serializer, so the window's bundle is the command line's document rather than a lookalike of it. Escape and the question's focused Cancel choose no file.

Revision 393 discloses a session's growth, as §12.1's S5 asks and nothing did: a person could not see how large a session was, what each record cost on disk, which tier it had reached, or how long a recording had before a limit stopped it. A session's size is the sum of the lengths its current manifest measured of every file it names - never a figure per record multiplied out - with the records its segments hold, the bytes each takes, and the tier its size is in: T1 up to 2 GiB, T2 up to 20 GiB, T3 up to 100 GiB, where a full search or a new graph expansion may answer progressively, and T4 past it, which no release is qualified at yet. `icat session` states it as `On disk` and in its JSON `size`; the window states it in the capture card, and while a capture records, on a line of its own, when its first limit stops it: its length, which is certain, or its journal or the disk's reserve at the rate the session has grown so far, the session's own bytes counted against the disk when they share a volume, then the limits after it. A stop a minute away, or a session past T3, reads in caution ink, and `icat capture`'s progress states the same in the same words. The policy captures have stops at its limits rather than evicting, so the point S5 asks for, where retention would begin evicting, is the point the capture stops; a rolling policy will owe its own. Sizes on disk and capture limits are now stated in one set of binary units, as the limits are set and the tiers drawn, where the recent sessions list, the sharing results and the investigation package read decimal megabytes and the capture's summary "1,024 MiB".

Revision 394 covers three prohibitions the contract index declared uncovered, each by what the code can be shown to hold, in tests that run on any host, since the broker runs only on Windows. P18: the elevated broker's project closure is exactly the capture path - the domain, the broker protocol, the store, the capture adapter and the recorder - so the importer and live follower, the content views, the analysis and the window are out of its reach, nothing it is built from draws one, and the one reader of an ETL file in all it reaches is the replay the importer drives, which neither the broker nor the recorder names. P19: a capture answers only the identity that started it; a prepared token presented from the same user's other logon session starts nothing, knowing a capture's ID lets another user or logon read, renew or stop nothing, the session's name proves nothing without the secret it was made with, and an authenticated identity carries no process ID to trust. P20: no product code reads another process's memory, duplicates its handles or lists the machine's; it opens a process only to hold its ID while a capture names it, with synchronize and limited-query access, and connects to no pipe but the broker's, after checking the server is the broker it launched. I17, I20, P12, P13 and P25 remain declared uncovered, each with the work that will cover it.

Revision 395 makes the demo investigation M5's exit gate ships: "a reproducible demo investigation showing multiple transports and two hosts, with explicit unsupported rows". `icat demo <directory>` writes two sessions and the investigation that holds them (`contracts/demo-investigation-v1.md`): a client whose browser and sync processes send TCP to a server and UDP to a name server neither recorded, and a server whose api answers them and asks its db over loopback, its capture begun 0.75 s after the client's and two records lost. The investigation names both hosts, aligns the server to the client by their recorded wall clocks and finds the two candidate joins between them; each session's ledger collects lifecycle, TCP and UDP only, so every other mechanism is a row not collected. The sessions are the same, to the digest of every file, each time the demo is made. InterCat generates it, so it says so wherever it is read: its sessions are published under the source identity `intercat-demo-v1` with records of a provider named for the demo, and the window's strip, title, disclosure and saved sessions list, the investigation window, `icat session` and `icat workspace show` each say that nothing in it was captured, in one sentence. Writing it found a unit the row type did not state: a row's session-relative time is in nanoseconds, as the normalizer converts it, not in source ticks; a test now places the demo's records where their readings put them.

Revision 396 lets the window open the demo, so the gate's demo is reachable where a first-time user starts, not only from a command line: Explore the demo, beside the saved sessions and investigations whenever no capture runs, makes it the first time and opens its investigation beside the window, which says in the demo's words what it is. The window keeps it in a folder of InterCat's own beside the sessions folder, never in it, so the saved sessions list holds only what a person saved; later it opens the demo it finds there rather than writing another, and a demo folder holding anything else is left as it is while the next free one takes the demo (`contracts/demo-investigation-v1.md` §4). The capture card's words where live capture cannot start now name the demo among what remains available. Opening a demo found on disk exposed a gap in making it: its investigation file was created first and completed by six more writes, so a write stopped between them would have left a file that read as the demo without its alignment or note. The file is now made under another name and takes its own last, so a folder that holds it holds the whole demo.

Revision 397 makes the interval-scoped redacted package §11.3's open item asked for, so a session above the ten-million-row bound, or the part of any session that matters, can be shared (`contracts/redacted-session-v1.md` §11). `icat package --redacted --interval start:end` holds the rows whose session time lies in the interval, read as `icat evidence` reads one, and from outside it every lifecycle record of each PID a row in it belongs to. That is more than the plan asked: keeping only a held instance's own records would have kept its name but not its PID's other instances, and a reused PID's later holder would then have bound as the first, so the package would have counted records its source leaves out as candidates; a PID's instances come from its lifecycle records alone, so keeping all of them keeps every binding of the interval's rows as the source derives it. A parent with no record in the interval is named and not linked. The bound now applies to the rows a package holds, counted by a first read of five columns, and the field join is sized by them. Coverage was the part the plan did not name: copying the source's ledger would have claimed the source's coverage over time whose records the package left out, so each epoch now speaks only for the part of the interval it spoke for, and one that spoke for none of it is dropped. That exposed an assumption every reader made: a whole session's coverage was its epochs', ignoring time, true only while a generation's records lie within their readings. An interval package's lifecycle records from outside its interval do not, so its whole-time coverage is now unknown for every mechanism, wherever a reader asks (`coverage-v2` §4), while the health strip and `icat session` state what its ledger says within the interval. Every reader says what the package holds in one sentence: `icat package`, `icat session`, and the window's status card and disclosure. Leaving rows out still reads them, so no pseudonym is one of their process or thread ids, ports or addresses, and the byte scan searches for their names, providers and schemas; their pseudonyms are never issued, so the package's counts say nothing of the rest of its source. The window's own interval choice is next.

Revision 398 gives the ranked list back the rail's room once a session is shown. Revision 397's Release run failed a multi-selection test whose third ranked row was not realized: at the smallest window, 1080 × 700, with a session shown, the rail's capture card wanted more height than the rail allows it and left the list its 120 px floor, under two rows, so the test passed only while a layout pass was still pending. The card had grown by revision 393's size line and revision 396's demo button, and it always kept the sentence on what exploring records and two lines that are empty until a capture runs. Now what helps before a session - that sentence and the demo button - shows only until one is shown, the demo stays in the Investigation menu, and a line that says nothing takes no room: the card shows all it holds unscrolled, and the list grows from 120 to 163 px there, two rows and the start of a third.

Revision 399 gives the window revision 397's interval package. "Share redacted session…" offers the inspector's time scope - a brushed or kept range, or the view zoomed in - as the package's interval, first whenever there is one, with the whole session still a choice; what the package will keep is said in the sentence its readers will use, and the saved result says it again. A session above the ten-million-row bound is shared only an interval at a time: without a time scope the prompt says so, keeps nothing and cannot go ahead.

Revision 400 makes a zoom read only the segments its interval meets, the first of P25's interactive paths to stop scanning a whole session. The zoomed detail opened every segment - reading its time column - and built every segment's tiles, wherever the zoom was, so the first zoom of a reopened session cost as much as the session was long. Each segment's checksummed header already declares the first and last readings its rows span, so a zoom now reads those 128 bytes, or the open reader's, and opens only the segments that can hold a row of its interval; a focused zoom, whose focus is resolved over every segment, still builds tiles and counts only for those. Measured on a reopened session of a million rows in twenty segments, a zoom to a hundredth of it took 3 to 17 ms where it took 86 to 237 ms, opening one segment instead of twenty, and its cost no longer grows with the session. The skip trusts that a row's session time is the one its clock gives its reading, as every writer makes it (I3, I8); eight window tests' rows stretched their session times without their readings, and now stretch both. P25 stays uncovered: a brush's counts, an evidence page and a search still read every segment.

Revision 401 extends that skip to the other reads of one interval. A brush's counts, the byte rows of the lanes beneath it, and the rankings of a time scope by bytes or by peers each opened every segment of the generation: the derivations they need took every segment as their argument even when the checkpoint held them, and their pass then read every segment's time column. They now take the instances and channels the derivation already made or the checkpoint holds without opening a segment, open every one only when neither does, and read only the segments the interval meets; a focus resolves its rows the same way. Measured on a reopened session of a million rows in twenty segments, a brush over a hundredth of it took 11 to 64 ms to count where it took 39 to 105 ms, its byte ranking 10 to 23 ms where it took 99 to 133 ms and its peer ranking 12 to 42 ms where it took 70 to 175 ms, each opening one segment instead of twenty. A writer cuts segments as records arrive, so two segments' spans can overlap, and each is judged by its own. The calls of an RPC capture are not checkpointed, and a call's request and response can lie in different segments, so its first brush pairs them over every segment, once per generation. One checkpoint test gave two records session times their readings could not give, and now gives them theirs, as revision 400's window tests do. P25 stays uncovered: the call ranking, a time scope's HTTP and RPC listings and an evidence page still read every segment. Revision 400 named a search among them, wrongly: the window's search reads the overview's processes and relationships, never a segment.

Revision 402 makes an evidence page open only the segments it reads. A page opened every segment of its generation before reading a row - for the derivations, which the checkpoint held, and for the capture and derivation each segment names, which its header holds - and a page of a time scope then walked every row from the earliest to the last, testing each one's session time. A page now names its evidence and orders its merge by what each segment's checksummed header declares, opens a segment only when the merge reaches it, passes over one that ends before its cursor or holds no reading of its time scope without opening it, seeks within a segment to the scope's first reading, and stops at the first reading past the scope, since no later reading's session time can lie in it (I3). Its identity is the one the open readers gave, so a cursor issued before still holds. Measured on a reopened session of a million rows in twenty segments, a page of a hundredth of it took 10 to 22 ms where it took 171 to 244 ms, and its owner's page 9 to 15 ms where it took 151 to 196 ms, each opening one segment instead of twenty, and a first or second page opened one too. P25 stays uncovered: a process's connections, the call ranking and a time scope's HTTP and RPC listings still read every segment, an RPC capture's first brush and a page of its calls or of HTTP exchanges pair them over every one, and a zoom builds a met segment's tiles from its rows.

Revision 403 makes a process's connections read only the segments their lifetimes meet. The list of a process's connections to other hosts, which its rung shows with each one's records and bytes, derived its channels from every segment and then counted every segment's rows, so a process with one brief connection cost as much as the session. Its channels now come from the derivation or the checkpoint, and since a connection's records all read between its first and last readings, only a segment whose declared readings meet a listed connection's lifetime - and the time scope's readings, when there is one - is opened. Measured on a reopened session of a million rows in twenty segments, the connections of a process whose one connection spans a hundredth of it took 8 to 64 ms where they took 127 to 221 ms, opening one segment instead of twenty. P25 stays uncovered: the call ranking and a time scope's HTTP and RPC listings still read every segment, an RPC capture's first brush and a page of its calls or of HTTP exchanges pair them over every one, and a zoom builds a met segment's tiles from its rows.

Revision 404 derives §6.3's grouping by user session, which `metrics-v1` §6 had listed as not derived. A Windows process runs in one terminal session, which its creation and rundown records name, and the session sets the machine's services and system processes, in session 0, apart from the processes of each signed-in user. A grouped answer now places each record under the terminal session of the process it is attributed to, as grouping by executable places it under the process's witnessed path: a process whose lifecycle records name no session is unattributed by that reason, `SessionUnknown`, never placed in a guessed session, and a generation whose records name none at all is not grouped by session. Summed totals, peers and channels, and RPC calls group so, and `icat metric --group-by session` names each group "Terminal session N" in words the window will share. It is the first half of §6's lane grouping; the window's control, which regroups the ranked list and the graph, is next.

Revision 405 gives the window that control. A **Group by** selector beneath Rank by groups a session's processes by executable, as projected, or by the terminal session their lifecycle records name, offered once one of them names one: a session whose records name none has one grouping and no selector. It stands at the machine rung, whose rows are the groups, and nowhere else, since a group's rung lists processes, which a grouping does not change. A choice regroups the overview already projected and reads no record. Each group is named in `icat metric --group-by session`'s words, "Terminal session 1", and the processes whose records named no session are one group, "Terminal session not recorded", never placed in a guessed session; the ranked rows, the graph's groups and the card of a folded one, a session's rung and timeline, the inspector's account of how a group was formed, a search's hit and the evidence step follow it. The two measures a group's total cannot be summed from its members' - its distinct peers and the median of its calls' times - are read for both groupings at once, under keys that never collide, so a choice reads neither again. The view stays at the machine rung with its time, selection, ranking, pins and scales, and the way forward ends, since it named groups that are gone. That keeps §6.4's promise that back and forward restore a rung's lane grouping: a grouping changes only where no rung lies behind, and none ahead remains. A live capture groups each later publication the same way, and a session opened or captured afterwards starts by executable. Where it is offered, the selector costs the smallest window's ranked list about half a row; two rows stay in view. Two gaps stay open. Nothing keeps the choice in the investigation yet, nor does `icat workspace show` state it, which is next. And `icat export` reaches a rung through the row keys of the grouping by executable, so a session's rung exports from the window alone until the command takes a grouping too (R18).

Revision 406 keeps a session's grouping where §6.1's persistence row and §26.3 put a view's settings: in the investigation it was opened from, beside its pins and ranking (`workspace-v17`). A member's layout names `UserSession` when its processes are grouped by terminal session; grouped by executable, as projected, it names none. A session opened from the investigation is grouped so from its first view, where its processes still name a session, and its notice lists the grouping with what else was put back ("which put back its processes grouped by terminal session"); the investigation's window and `icat workspace show` say it in the same words, and the show command's document carries it (`workspace-resolution-v20`). Grouped by executable again, a layout that keeps nothing else is removed. A version 16 file is read as one grouping by executable and written as version 17; a file that groups in an earlier version, names executables by name or names any grouping but a terminal session is refused. `icat export` taking a grouping is next (R18).

Revision 407 closes the parity gap revision 405 left (R18). `icat export --group-by session` groups the processes as the window's Group by does before it reads a row key, so `--at session:1` reaches terminal session 1's rung, its processes and their records, and the file is the window's, byte for byte, at the machine rung, a session's rung and its evidence. A ranked export names its grouping beside its ranking, `"groupedBy": "session"` or `"executable"` (and its redacted report the same), and one grouped by session says how its groups were formed among its caveats; its report states "Grouped by" beside "Ranked by". Grouped by executable, as by default, a session's key names no row, and a session whose records name no terminal session is refused rather than grouped into one group of processes whose session was not recorded.

Revision 408 reads a generation's paired RPC calls and grouped HTTP exchanges without opening a segment. Once a generation's calls were paired, each later query of them - the call ranking, a process's RPC channels and their spans, a page of its calls, a process's HTTP exchanges and their spans - still opened every segment and source-field segment to hand the derivation, which returned the pairs it held without reading any of them, and an evidence page or a timeline focus of one call or exchange did the same before reading its records. The segments are now handed over as a list that opens each one only when it is first read: a derivation already made is checked against the segments it was made from by their published names alone, so a query that reads it opens none, a page of calls opens only the segments holding its calls and their other ends, and an evidence page of an operation passes over every segment holding none of its records without opening it. Measured on a reopened session of 500,000 calls, a million records in twenty-one segments, one call's evidence took 21 to 29 ms where it took 421 to 656 ms, opening one segment instead of twenty-one, and the call ranking of a hundredth of it 12 to 14 ms where it took 20 to 28 ms, opening none. P25 stays uncovered: an RPC capture's first brush, ranking or listing pairs its calls over every segment, since a call's request and its response can lie in different ones, and nothing persists the pairs; a timeline focus on a busy channel's calls gathers their records anew at every zoom, about 110 ms at 250,000 calls; and a zoom builds a met segment's tiles from its rows.

Revision 409 gives a timeline cell the explanation §6.8 asks of every cell, edge and ranked row, and fixes what building it found (R13). A cell's hover card ended "Click makes it the analysis interval", but a click chose the machine column beneath the pointer, which in a group's process lanes counted coarser than the view is not the cell the card described: that cell could never be chosen, and its card went on offering it. A click now chooses the lane's own cell, and the inspector says beneath the time scope how the cell was counted: which of its lane's records it holds - every record of a mechanism, those process-binding, version 4, binds to a process as their canonical owner, those of one source direction, or those made at one end of a channel the relation rule paired - what its bar plots, their count or the ranking's bytes per second, the column it was counted in, the overview's, the zoomed view's own or the lanes' own coarser ones, and the capture's coverage over it, an empty cell's included. An interval a step or the interval table chose is explained as a cell of the lane they follow, or of the machine row where one is drawn; a brushed range, which is no cell, is not. Another interval, lane, row, end, process or rung chosen takes over from a clicked cell, and a publication of the same session keeps the lane it was chosen in (`WorkspaceNavigationMemento`). The tour, which no rule derived, explains none, and a real session's card now says a click explains the cell.

Revision 410 gives an evidence page, its timeline focus and `icat evidence` the narrowing a timeline cell's exact contributing evidence needs (§6.4): one mechanism's records, one source direction's, or those made at one end of a paired channel, as the timeline's mechanism lanes, a process's direction rows and a channel's end lanes count them. A rung's filters carry them (`mechanism`, `direction`, `end`), and each narrows whatever entity the latest entity filter names, so removing one widens the scope one visible step; an end of anything but a paired channel is a problem the scope states, never a narrowing ignored. A narrowed page has an identity of its own, so its cursor continues it and restarts any other query, while an unnarrowed page's identity, and every cursor issued before, is unchanged. The evidence rung's timeline focus is narrowed the same way, so it counts exactly what E lists. `icat evidence` takes `--mechanism`, `--direction` and `--end`, by the names a person types, states each beside its scope and carries it in its next-page hint, and its JSON page names them (`evidence-page-v4`). The window's E from a chosen cell, which these scopes are for, follows.

Revision 411 makes E from a chosen timeline cell list exactly its records (§6.4), with the scopes revision 410 gave the evidence query. With nothing else selected, a cell of a mechanism's lane opens the rung's records of that mechanism within it, a cell of a group's process lane that process's records, a cell of a process's direction row its records of that source direction, and a cell of a channel's end lane the records made at that end, each narrowed behind a filter chip named in words ("Mechanism: TCP", "Direction: Outbound", "End: 127.0.0.1:8080") whose removal widens it to the rung's records. The inspector's evidence card names the same records before E is pressed, and the evidence rung's timeline counts exactly what it lists. The machine row, whose cell draws the rung's own records over the machine's, still opens the rung's records, and a selected process, group, aggregate, relationship or row still decides what E lists. The records are the ones `icat evidence` lists with the same narrowing.

Revision 412 keeps an operation's records with its generation and reads their rows alone (P25). An evidence page or a timeline focus of an RPC channel, call or relationship, or of a process's HTTP exchanges or one exchange, gathered the operation's records from the generation's pairs anew each time it was asked for, as one set of every record, and then tested every row of each segment holding one against that set: each zoom of a busy channel's focus gathered its records again and read every other process's rows beside them. The records are now held by segment, each segment's rows ascending, and the generation keeps those of the last four operations asked for, under each evidence policy apart, since the pairs they are read from never change within it. A page's merge steps from one of the operation's rows to the next, reading no other, and a focus counts only the operation's rows of each segment it meets. Measured on a reopened session of 500,000 calls, a million records in twenty-one segments, a zoom of a hundredth of it focused on a channel of 250,000 calls took 4 to 9 ms after the first, where each took 123 to 167 ms, and one of the whole session 62 to 101 ms, where it took 203 to 251; a page of fifty of the channel's records within that hundredth read 51 rows in 7 to 14 ms, where it read 103 in 137 to 143 ms. P25 stays uncovered: an RPC capture's first brush, ranking or listing still pairs its calls over every segment, since a call's request and its response can lie in different ones, and nothing persists the pairs; and a zoom builds a met segment's tiles from its rows.

Revision 413 makes §6.8's "returning to defaults is one command" true. A setting a person changed from its first-run default - what the rows are ranked by and whether per second, each timeline lane on its own scale, the processes grouped by terminal session, a reused PID's candidates counted - was shown only by its own control, and only the evidence policy had a command that put it back; returning the view to its defaults meant finding each control again. The rail now says how many settings differ from their defaults, beside a Restore defaults command, on one line, since the rail's height is its ranked rows'; the line's tooltip, and a screen reader, name each one in the words an investigation uses when it keeps them (R5). Per second is counted only under a ranking that has a rate. The command puts every setting back as its own control would, so the time, the selection and the pins stay, a regrouped view returns to the machine rung, and a session opened from an investigation keeps the defaults there. The tour, which no person set, names none.

Revision 414 records the processes that collected a capture, the identities §19.5 asks for. InterCat's broker and `icat record` are processes on the machine they observe, so their own activity can appear in their own capture, but nothing in a session said which processes they were: a record names a process only by its PID, which the machine reuses, and an image name proves nothing. A live capture now publishes with its first generation the process collecting it - its broker, or `icat record` itself - by PID and the creation time the operating system keeps for it, the instant a lifecycle record of the same instance carries (`contracts/collector-identities-v1.md`, the store's dependency kind 11). Every later generation carries it, re-derivation and checkpoints keep it, a follower mirrors it once, from the first chunk that has it, and retention never releases it, so a capture interrupted before its last publication still names its collector. A redacted package never carries it, since a collector's PID and creation time would link the package to its machine, and its leak scan looks for the file's digest. `icat session` names each collector by its role, PID and creation time. What follows, in order: the broker names the client that asked it to record, the window or `icat capture`, from its control pipe; a reader matches each collector to the process instance whose lifecycle records carry its PID and creation time, and labels that instance's records as InterCat's own wherever they are shown; and the window offers a visible view filter that sets them aside, counting what it set aside, never removing a record.

Revision 415 has the broker name the client that asked it to record, the first of the steps revision 414 left. The broker's control pipe names its client's PID (`GetNamedPipeClientProcessId`), which `contracts/broker-v1.md` keeps out of every authorization decision and the broker only logged. It is now carried from the connection's dispatcher through the start of a capture to its runtime, which names it, with the creation time the broker reads from the process while it runs, as the capture's `Client` collector beside the broker itself. A replayed start request starts nothing again, so it names no other client, and a PID the pipe could not name, or one that is not positive, is named by no collector. The PID labels the client's activity in the capture; it is never an identity the broker trusts, and a client that has exited by then is named without a creation time, which no instance can be shown to match.

Revision 416 labels InterCat's own processes wherever a process is described, the second of §19.5's steps. A reader now matches each collector a capture names to the process instance whose PID is the collector's and whose lifecycle records carry the creation time it named (`collector-binding-v1`): a PID alone is never taken for one, so another holder of the broker's PID is not labelled, and a collector whose creation time was not read, or that no instance carries, is kept as unfound rather than given a guessed instance. The instance's ranked row and the inspector's caption end with which collector it is ("PID 4120 · running at start · InterCat's broker"), the inspector's explanation says that its records are InterCat's own activity, counted as any process's are, its graph node's card names it first, and `icat processes` names it beside its executable, in its explanation and as its document's `collector`, in the same words (`CollectorText`, R5, R18); the overview's bundle, which `icat overview --json` writes, carries which instance each collector is and the collectors no instance is. Its records are not removed or set apart: an interaction with InterCat can be what is investigated. What remains of §19.5 is a visible view filter that sets them aside, counting what it set aside.

Revision 417 is §19.5's last step, the visible view filter. The rail names InterCat's own processes - "InterCat's own: intercat-broker.exe · PID 4120" - beside one command, Set aside, which takes them out of the ranked rows, the graph and the channels, with every relationship and channel one of them is an end of and a group left with none. The line then counts them, "InterCat's own set aside: 1 process, 312 records", and its tooltip names them and says that no record was removed: the timeline, which counts every record, and the evidence still count theirs. Show them puts them back, and either returns the view to the machine rung, as a regrouped view does. The filter is a view setting, so the rail names it among the settings changed from their defaults and Restore defaults shows them again; a session opened afterwards shows them. What a ranking states over the rows shown leaves them out too: the processes with a peer, and a group's or the machine's peers and call times, which the peer and call measures now total without the instances a view sets aside, each of which keeps its own. A process's lineage names a parent or child set aside as InterCat's own, where it said that a set-aside parent was not in the capture and counted no set-aside child. An export of the view says what it set aside: a caveat counts the processes and their records, a JSON export carries the two counts as `setAside`, a CSV, which carries no caveat, ends each line with `set_aside_processes` and `set_aside_records`, and the redacted report keeps the counts, which name nothing (`docs/design/SHARING-REPORT-REDACTION.md`); `icat export --set-aside-collectors` builds the same file (R18) and says when a capture names no collector to set aside. An evidence export, like the evidence rung, lists every record. The default stays everything shown. §19.5 lets default exploration hide them, but a view that hid them unasked would need every reading of the session, `icat` and its exports among them, to hide them too (R18), and its rows would not add up to the timeline without a person having chosen that; the rail names InterCat's own processes one command away instead. What remains of §19.5 is a statement, in the inspector and `icat overview`, of a collector no instance is; an investigation does not yet keep the choice, which needs a workspace contract revision.

Revision 418 says which collector a capture names that no process instance is. A broker's capture names its client by the PID its control pipe names, and that client's creation time cannot always be read - it may have exited - while a capture whose lifecycle records carry no creation time leaves even its broker unmatched. Such a collector was listed by `icat session` and written in `icat overview --json`, and said nowhere a person looks at the process holding its PID, which read as any other process. The inspector's explanation of each process holding a PID a collector names, and `icat processes`', now say so - "The capture names the InterCat process that asked to record as PID 7008, but its creation time was not read, so no process can be shown to be it, and none is labelled as it (collector-binding-v1)." - for every holder of that PID, a reused one's too, since a PID alone is never taken for a collector. `icat overview` names each collector under Collected by, by the instance it is or why none is, in `icat session`'s role names, and its busiest processes carry the window's label; the rail's tooltip ends with the same sentences. What remains of §19.5 is an investigation keeping the set-aside choice.

Revision 419 keeps the choice to set InterCat's own processes aside where §26.3 keeps a view's settings: in the investigation a session was opened from, beside its pins, grouping and ranking (`workspace-v18`). A member's layout names `collectorsAside` when they are set aside; a session opened from the investigation sets them aside from its first view, and its notice lists it with what else was put back ("which put back InterCat's own processes set aside"); the investigation's window and `icat workspace show` say it in the same words, the show command's document carries it (`workspace-resolution-v21`), and the rail names it among the settings changed from their defaults in those words too. Shown again, a layout that keeps nothing else is removed. A version 17 file is read as one that shows them and written as version 18; a file that sets them aside in an earlier version is refused. The contract's version line had said that versions 1 to 15 were read, though 16 was too, and its list of what is not defined still named lane grouping, kept since version 17; both are corrected. §19.5 is complete.

Revision 420 collapses a group's lanes past the lane bound (§6.2: collapse groups). A group with more members than the 200-lane bound drew no process lane at all - the query refused them with its reason - so a large group, such as a terminal session's under revision 405's grouping, which holds every process that session ran, showed only its aggregate row. The view now names the lanes it draws: its pinned lanes, in the order pinned, then its busiest by their records over the whole session, as the ranked table orders them, 199 in all. The query counts every other member together in one folded lane beneath them, on the lanes' own columns and within the same cell budget, so the lanes and the fold partition the group's records column by column and none is dropped. The caption says how many are folded and where ("199 process lanes, and the other 31, least busy, folded into one lane beneath them"); the row is named in the words the graph uses for an opened group's members not drawn on their own ("31 other members"); its card states what it counts and that no bytes are summed for it; and a click chooses its cell, which the inspector explains as the fold's and from which E lists exactly the folded members' records. A search says how many of the members it finds are folded, since they have no lane to mark. A folded member selected in the ranked table is offered the pin, which counts the lanes again with it drawn at the top; unpinned, it folds again if it is among the least busy. The fold's name stands for many processes, so a click on it selects none. A query that names no lanes for such a group is still refused with its reason, and one that names more lanes than the bound allows is an argument error. Folding lanes below the bound on request waits on a capture that needs it: drawing only the rows in view, pins and search already make a group of 200 lanes workable.

Revision 421 reads a session's instants on the wall clock (§6.2's time base). Revision 350 stated the time base under the axis - session time since the capture's recorded start - and left a wall-clock axis waiting on a reason to read instants in wall-clock time. The reason is the one a person investigating a machine's IPC meets first: matching what the timeline shows against that machine's own logs, which keep its wall clock, where "14:32:05" had to be worked out from "session time since 14:03:12" by hand. Where the capture recorded its clock calibration, a Wall clock toggle beside the timeline's Expand reads every instant the view states on the wall clock its machine read, in this computer's zone with the offset from UTC each such time is stated with: the axis's edges and its time base, which names the day ("wall clock on 10/06/2026, UTC+02:00") and both days where the view crosses midnight, the cards, the time scope, the interval table, an RPC call's and an HTTP exchange's row, the evidence's scope and rows, and the inspector's record, which states its date and offset too. One mapping does it everywhere (`SessionClock`, R5). An instant is placed through the calibration (`clock-calibration-v1`): on the line through the capture's start and stop readings, so it reads each of them exactly, or from the start reading at the capture's own clock's rate where no stop was recorded; the toggle's tooltip says which, how closely each reading was taken, and that how right that clock was is not known. A time of day is cut, never rounded into the next second, to the digits its span needs (tenths, milliseconds, microseconds, 100 ns), and a range across a change of its zone's offset states each end's. Nothing counted changes. The choice is named among the settings changed from their defaults, put back by Restore defaults and kept across a later publication of the session; a session whose capture recorded none - an import, a redacted package - offers no toggle. `icat timeline --wall-clock` and `icat evidence --wall-clock` read the same (R18) and refuse a session that recorded none. The axis rule below asked for a host-local wall clock, which cannot be said yet: no capture records its host's zone, so the reader's zone, with its offset stated, stands in until one does; the rule now says so. An investigation keeping the choice (`workspace-v19`) and going to a wall-clock time are next.

Revision 422 keeps the time base where §26.3 keeps a view's settings: in the investigation a session was opened from (`workspace-v19`). Revision 421's Wall clock lasted only while the window showed the session, though an investigation puts back every other view setting of a session opened from it. A member's layout now names `wallClock` when its times are read on the wall clock; a session opened from the investigation reads them so from its first view, and its notice lists it with what else was put back ("which put back its times read on the wall clock") where its capture recorded a wall clock to read; the investigation's window and `icat workspace show` say it in the same words, and the show command's document carries it (`workspace-resolution-v22`). Read in session time again, a layout that keeps nothing else is removed. A version 18 file is read as one that reads session time and written as version 19; a file that reads the wall clock in an earlier version is refused. Going to a wall-clock time is next.

Revision 423 goes to a moment (§6.2's time base, §6.7's navigation). Reading a session on the wall clock made a machine's own logs readable beside it, but finding a logged moment still meant panning to it. A time typed into the search (Ctrl+F) - a time of day on the wall clock the capture's machine read, "14:32:05.120", with its date or its offset from UTC where they are needed, or session time, "312.5 s" or "250 ms" - is now offered first among its hits, named where it falls on the wall clock, with its date and offset, and in session time. Enter goes there: the timeline centres on it at the span it had, and the cell holding it becomes the analysis interval as a step with `[` or `]` would choose it - the selected lane's, which the inspector explains and E lists, else the machine row's, else at the machine rung, which draws no machine row, the column holding it. A time the session cannot place is said with why, ahead of any names it also matches: outside the session, with the wall clock's reading of its span; on more than one of its days, asking for the date; skipped or repeated by its zone's change of offset, asking for the offset; a time of day in a session that recorded no wall clock, asking for session time. A bare whole number is still a PID, and the search's placeholder now names times. One reader places a moment (`SessionMoment`), and reads a wall-clock reading back to session time through the rate the calibration measured, the inverse of the placement revision 421 reads instants with. Going there found an older defect. A zoom counted a lane only for a mechanism with a record in view, and the timeline, which draws a zoom's lanes only when it holds every lane the overview draws, drew all of them at the overview's resolution instead: a zoom into any span where one of the session's mechanisms was quiet - on a real capture, with its sparse process lifecycle records, most short spans - never refined, and a cell chosen there was a sliver no bar matched. A zoom now keeps a lane for every mechanism the overview draws, empty where none of its records falls and judged by that mechanism's coverage, and a cell is explained from the zoom's lanes only while they are the ones drawn. `icat timeline --mechanism` counts a quiet mechanism's lane the same way, each column saying what the capture covered of it rather than that the lane has no bucket, and names the mechanism and a direction as the window does ("UDP records", never "Udp records") (R18, R21). `icat evidence` taking a moment is next (R18).

Revision 424 takes a moment on the command line (R18). `icat evidence --from <moment>` reads its text as the window's search reads one - a time of day on the wall clock the capture's machine read, with its date or offset where they are needed, or session time with its unit - places it by the same reader against the same extent, the timed records' that the persisted overview holds or the segments' tiles count, and lists the rows from it on, within `--interval` where one is asked for. It names where the moment fell on the wall clock and in session time, as the search's hit does, and a moment the session cannot place is refused in the search's own words, as is one after the interval's end; its next page continues from the same moment. Its JSON (`evidence-page-v5`) carries the instant in ticks and as the wall clock read it.

Revision 425 lets the command line's intervals be typed as the window states them (R18). `icat evidence`, `timeline`, `export`, `package --redacted` and `workspace timeline` took `--interval` only as 100-nanosecond ticks, while the window states an interval in seconds and `icat metric` took one with its unit: a person reading "1.484 – 1.523 s" in the inspector had to multiply it out. Each bound may now be a time with its unit - `1.5s`, `250ms`, `40us`, `800ns` - as well as a tick count, taken outward to whole ticks, a start down and an end up, so the interval holds every record of the time typed; a bound that is neither, or an end not after its start, is refused in one sentence that says what an interval takes.

Revision 426 leaves the keyboard where a moment gone to is (§6.7, R15). Enter on a moment cleared the search and gave the keyboard to the ranked table, as a hit that opens a rung does, though the moment had moved the timeline and chosen a cell there: to step on from it with `[` or `]`, or pan around it, a keyboard user had to Tab to the timeline, which then said the range in view but not the interval chosen. Enter on a moment now gives the timeline the keyboard, and the timeline says the analysis interval wherever one is chosen - by a click, a step or a moment gone to - beside the range in view. What the search says as a time or a name is typed - its matches, or why a time places nothing - is a polite live region, heard without leaving the box. Whether a zoom itself needs announcing stays with the real screen-reader pass.

Revision 427 fixes what a rendered tour of the demo session found in the interval table (§6.2's table equivalent, R15). Read on the wall clock, its Window column - 160 pixels, enough for session time's "11.966 – 12.044 s" - clipped "09:00:11.966 – 09:00:12.044" where the count beside it began, so a row read "09:00:120"; and an interval holding no record drew a "?" as its glyph and was read aloud as an unknown mechanism. The column is wider, a window across days wraps within it, and the bytes take the width that is left and trim to it rather than running past the table's edge at the smallest window; an interval with no record keys no mechanism, by glyph or by name. The revision's Release run also failed, once, to open a package just made: a test before it had made and removed a package of the same name in the same second, and the viewer's registry of open sessions (§20.1) handed out the store it kept for that folder's earlier session, whose every read then failed. A kept store is handed out only while a lease of its folder still holds its session; a folder written again with another session, or emptied, is opened afresh.

Revision 428 is what a rendered tour of the demo investigation found in its window (§8.2, §6.2). Its merged time's axis divided the interval in four and rounded each label to the digits a quarter needed, so a tick drawn at 14.76 s read "15 s" and the axis's start, at 0.2 s, read "0 s": no label but by chance named the instant it was drawn at, against this section's 1–2–5 ladder. The axis now labels its two ends as the session's timeline labels its own, in the unit and digits the span needs, and between them ticks round instants of the ladder, each named to exactly the digits its width needs and centred on its instant: the narrowest width whose labels, measured, keep clear of one another, and of those only the ones clear of both ends. Its words said times to the 100 ns tick in up to seven decimals, and a lane's records ran "to 59.0300001 s", the tick past its last record, which no record is at. A lane's records now run from its first record's instant to its last's, one record is said at its instant, and a column, the interval shown, a saved view and a column opened are said as the session's timeline says an interval, "10.863 – 11.231 s". The window's smallest height grows from 640 to 650 pixels: at 640 its sessions fell 3 pixels short of the two whole rows it promises at its smallest, which a test had failed on since revision 361's baseline. §26.3 and §6.1's persistence row still said view filters had no control and nothing of them was kept: revisions 417 and 419 gave the window one, InterCat's own processes set aside, and kept it, and revision 422 keeps the time base beside it, so both say so now.

Revision 429 is what a tour of the window in its light and high-contrast modes found (§6.1, R15, R21), and what the suites kept finding. Every mode read clearly, but the relationship table of a rung with no relationship - the demo's client, whose only connection runs to another host - was a blank box under its headers, which reads as a table still loading or as processes that exchanged nothing. In place of its rows it now says why: that no record pairs two of the session's processes, that a connection whose other end is outside the session is listed on its process's rung, and that every record is in the timeline; at a rung whose drawn processes have none while others exist, that the others are listed at the machine rung; and with InterCat's own processes set aside, that none pairs two of those shown. The full suites had failed, now and then, a test of the window's model that awaited a count on a pool thread while the workspace's own continuations ran beside it, as four did in revision 427 and one in revision 428. Every such test of the Desktop project, 66 more, now runs on the single-threaded context the window's dispatcher stands for, and a test of the project fails any test that awaits otherwise.

Revision 430 is what a tour of the investigation's dialogs found (§8.2, §8.4). The Align dialog opened on one or two instants a person reads, every box empty, even for a session aligned by its wall clocks: it read as a session never aligned, and aligning it again meant stating everything anew. It now opens on how the session is aligned - its method, its reference and what was stated, each written back so that it reads as stated, 2,100,000 ns as "2.1 ms" - and says the alignment in force above them; aligning from what it opened on states the same alignment again. Each box keeps its own height beside a hint of several lines, where the drift's stretched to its hint's. The known translations and the saved views, where an investigation keeps none, were an empty box; each list now says so in its place.

Revision 431 gives the session's timeline the ticks this section asks of its axis (§6.2). Since revision 350 it named only its two ends, with the time base between them, so no instant inside a view could be read without hovering a column. Between its ends, and either side of the time base, it now ticks round instants of the ladder, each named to exactly the digits its width needs and centred on its rule: in session time the multiples of the width, "10 s", "40 s"; on the wall clock the times of day the capture's machine read, "09:00:13", each placed at the instant of session time the calibration says it read them, and named from the round time itself rather than from the instant it maps back to, which could read a hair early. The width is the narrowest whose labels, measured, keep clear of one another, and the ticks stand only where they keep clear of both ends and of the time base, which the axis states first, so at the smallest window a long time base can leave room for none. Above ten seconds the ladder is now the one this section names - 10, 20 and 30 s, then 1, 2, 5, 10 and 30 min and 1 h - continued by 2, 6 and 12 h and whole days, so a view of minutes ticks whole minutes, the investigation's axis among them. The ticks are laid out again only when the view, the plot or the time base moves, so a repaint still allocates nothing (R11).

Revision 432 is what a tour of the window's first run and its prompts found (§6.8, R15). Before any session was open, the inspector offered Export this view, Share redacted report and Re-layout graph as though each had something to act on, and an export said only after the click that there was nothing to export. Each is now offered only while it has something - a rung listing rows or records, a graph drawing a node - and otherwise says why in its tooltip, shown on the disabled button as the others' are: open or record a session first, or that the rung lists no row. The header's Forward button's face is one word, its rung and its shortcut in its tooltip and accessible name: beside Back, "Forward (Alt+Right)" left the session's title 121 pixels at the smallest window, under the 150 a test had asked since revision 361's baseline, which now passes. The prompts themselves read as they should. Every failure the baseline still lists needs Windows: the broker's own tests, whose paths and pipe are Windows', the capture's clock and host readings, and the follow tickets, which rest on a file sharing Linux does not enforce.

Revision 433 is the last thing the tours found in the graph (§6.3). A node's name stood 3 pixels outside its disc, and a selected node's ring is drawn 6 pixels outside it, so the selected node - the one whose name §6.3 always shows - had its name drawn across its own halo. A selected node's name, or that of an aggregate partly selected, is now placed outside the ring, as far past it as any other name stands from its node, with its second line or without it; no other label moves.

Revision 434 begins rolling retention (§20.2, S6, IC-016a) with the release it needs first (ADR-043, `contracts/store-v1.md` §8). Every release so far gave up records and kept their rows, so a session that shed evidence still grew by its rows and could not run within a bound. An interval release gives up a session's oldest interval, records and rows together, a whole journal unit at a time - a recording's chunk, a single journal's batch - each of whose rows reads before the instant asked for, and states its own boundary, one nanosecond after the latest row it gave up, from which every record is retained. What later records mean rests on records before them: a process instance is derived from its PID's lifecycle records, keyed by how many came before, and a connection's incarnations, key and holder from its connects, disconnects and every record of it. So the release keeps, as evidence rather than a summary, the lifecycle records of every process a staying row belongs to and of its parents, the first record of a process no lifecycle record names, and at each connection end a staying row names and its mirror, its lifecycle records, each incarnation's first record and the first record of each holder and strength no staying row has; a kept row stays as a retained one does, so this repeats until it keeps nothing more. A test over 120 random recordings, delivered late as a busy processor's buffer is, found the repetition needed: a process's first record can be a connection's, whose other end then has to stay too. Every row that stays then binds to the same instance at the same strength and has the same other end and channel key; the publications holding a row that goes are rewritten, every index goes with them, and the record, a new `Interval` kind every later generation carries, states the boundary and the rows it gave up and kept. Re-derivation is refused while kept rows remain. Nothing offers the release yet: readers must first say that the interval before its boundary is released rather than quiet, and operations open across it are I20's.

Revision 435 keeps an operation open across an interval release's boundary whole, which is I20's retention clause (ADR-043 decision 6). An RPC call one of whose records stays keeps the other, and an ambiguous run every record, so a call open across the boundary is still completed, with its duration and interface, rather than one whose start was never observed; a call whose records all go takes nothing from its key's walk. A completed client call that stays keeps what its other end is read from - its window's ALPC sends on its thread, the receives of its one message id, the first server call the receiving thread began - and a server call that stays keeps every client call that reached it, so a call two clients reached stays neither's. An HTTP exchange one of whose buffers stays keeps every buffer. A test over 80 random recordings of linked calls and exchanges found one more rule: a use of an exchange number opened only by repeating a buffer of the use before it needs that use, since its going would join the later use to an earlier one. All of these are kept within the rounds that keep processes and connections, so a kept operation's process and a kept row's operation are kept in turn.

Revision 436 has a session's readers state the interval before an interval release's boundary as released rather than quiet (ADR-043 decision 7, R21, S5). Coverage is where a reader learns whether an absence means anything, so a generation's reader places the latest release's boundary on its coverage ledger, at the first native reading at or after it, and coverage over any scope reaching before it - a mechanism's, a timeline column's, the whole capture's - is no better than a partial gap, because the records read before it were released by retention but for those kept as the evidence of later ones (`contracts/coverage-v2.md` §4). A worse state stays, and a scope already a partial gap says the release before its own fact. A redacted package is a reader that publishes what it read, so one holding time before a boundary states the boundary in its policy, and its own readers say the same partial gap and that its source had released those records (`contracts/redacted-session-v1.md` §6-§7). The window's size line says from when the session keeps every record, and when, how many and why the records before it went; the timeline hatches the columns before the boundary as it hatches any gap, and the status line counts them. A tour of a released session found the interval table's coverage column too narrow for "partial gap, not extrapolated", which every column before a boundary now states, so it ran under the bytes; the column fits it, and a longer state wraps within it.

Revision 437 offers the interval release from the command line (ADR-043, `contracts/store-v1.md` §8, S5): `icat retain <session> --release-before <moment>` places a moment as `icat evidence --from` places one - a time of day on the wall clock the capture's machine read, so a time copied from that machine's logs finds its place, or session time with its unit - and discloses what a release before it gives up and keeps: the records in its oldest units, the rows derived from them, the rows kept as the evidence later records rest on, the boundary from which every record is kept, each instant on the wall clock and in session time, and the derived files it rewrites. It performs the release only with `--confirm` and a stated reason, as every release is, and only once no recorder can still be writing the session, since a release published beneath one fails its next publication (ADR-024): a capture that stopped without finishing cannot be told from one still recording, so it is measured and never released.
