Writing it found that the earliest boundary a preview names is a bound rather than a promise - before it nothing can go, but after it everything may still be kept as evidence - so it is no longer offered as a boundary that releases something, and `icat session` now says "1 row" rather than "1 rows" of a release.

Revision 438 lets a follow go on across releases of the session it derives into (ADR-044), which rolling retention of a live capture needs first: in a broker capture the session a person watches is the follower's, so a rolling release has to be the follower's own, published between two mirrors (ADR-024). The follower matched its chunks to the evidence's by position, so a release of its session's oldest chunks stopped it, and a follower opened afresh numbered every later record from the wrong journal index. It now finds the chunks its session holds among the evidence's by their bytes - starting after the evidence's first only when the session states a release - and counts the capture's chunks followed, released ones included, so a follow still completes holding fewer chunks than the evidence and the live edge previews the right ones. A fresh follower reads the released chunks back from the evidence, checked by digest, and numbers the next records as the capture did. Evidence that released its own records is now refused explicitly, since even a follow into an empty session would number what it derives from the wrong record. The plan's IC-016a row is corrected to what ADR-043 decided: it still asked for the summary checkpoint and censored operations that keeping evidence replaced.

Revision 439 gives a follow its rolling retention (ADR-045, §20.2, S5): `icat follow --keep-last <seconds>` and `icat capture --keep-last <seconds>` keep the newest stretch of session time that long. Once the derived session holds a quarter more than its window past its oldest record or latest release, the records read before its newest window go by interval release, published by the follow's own writer between two mirrors (ADR-024, ADR-044), and nothing is asked again until the session has grown by another quarter, since a release works in whole chunks and planning one reads every row. Each release's reason names the window, the command prints the window's size-line sentence saying from when every record is kept, and its document states the window, the releases and the latest boundary. The broker's evidence still keeps every record until its quota or duration stops the capture: releasing it needs the broker to know what its follower mirrored, which is next, and the window's own rolling capture should start its timeline at the window rather than draw the released time as a long gap.

Revision 440 keeps a finished session's RPC calls, so a reopen's first RPC view pairs none (P25, §12.1 S3). A reopened session's first call ranking, RPC listing or brush paired every call record of every segment before it could answer, since a call's request and its response can lie in different segments and nothing kept the pairs: the first RPC view of a finished session cost as much as the session was long, however few of its records were calls. A writer that publishes a generation's checkpoint and overview now publishes its operation index with them (`contracts/operation-index-v1.md`): the calls under `rpc-call-operation-v1` and their other ends under `rpc-call-peer-v1`, in the canonical order of their first records, without process bindings. A reader binds each call to the checkpoint's instances by its first record's reading, as a derivation does, so the index depends on no instance numbering and every answer is the one pairing gives; the step that orders bound calls into their groups now places each call at its first record's canonical rank and deals the calls into their groups in that order, with no sort, for a derivation and a reader alike. Revision 229 kept the call list out of the overview for its size, about a hundred bytes a call; a call's first record is named here by its ordinal and one of a handful of origins - the stream, epoch and fact key a capture's call records share - so a completed call takes 71 bytes, and the index is 7.9% of the segments' size, read only when calls are asked for. Measured three times on sessions in which a fifth of the records are calls, each in a process that had read nothing, a process's first RPC channel list after a reopen of 10M records in 40 segments, 2,000,000 calls, took 1.10 to 1.21 s where pairing them from the segments took 3.45 to 4.50 s, and of 1M records, 200,000 calls, 0.34 to 0.41 s where it took 0.80 to 0.99 s; the indexes were 151 and 15 MiB beside 1.9 GiB and 192 MiB of segments. An index that cannot be read, covers other segments, or is handed segments in another order is paired around, and the overview says why once the calls were asked for; a generation naming none, as every one published before, is published again by its writer or `icat checkpoint`, and calls beyond 1 GiB are not kept, which the index says. P25 stays uncovered: a live generation's first RPC view still pairs every call, a reopen's first HTTP listing groups its exchanges over every segment, and a zoom builds a met segment's tiles from its rows.

Revision 441 keeps a finished session's HTTP exchanges in its operation index too (P25, §12.1 S3, `operation-index-v1` minor 1). A reopen's first HTTP listing of a process grouped every WinINet buffer of every segment, and before that opened every segment to compare them with the checkpoint's instances. The index now keeps each exchange as the grouping made it - its number, first buffer, readings and session times, each part's buffers, bytes and wholeness, and where its buffers are - without its process binding, which a reader makes from the checkpoint's instances by the exchange's first buffer, as the grouping does. A reopen's first listing, a page of exchanges and their spans then open no segment and answer as grouping every segment does, and the calls and the exchanges come from one read of the index. An index of minor 0, as revision 440 wrote, gives its calls and keeps no exchanges, which are grouped from the segments, and a writer's next publication or `icat checkpoint` replaces it. The calls, and the exchanges after them, are each kept when they fit in what the index may hold. P25 stays uncovered: a live generation's first RPC or HTTP view still derives its operations from every record, and a zoom builds a met segment's tiles from its rows.

Revision 442 lets a person keep what a session holds from a moment through every retention (I18, S6, ADR-046, `contracts/store-v1.md` §8): the pin §10.1 and §20.2 describe. Since revision 439 a follow keeping a rolling window released whatever was read before the window, and nothing kept an interval a person had found worth keeping. A pinned lease held one generation's files, in the process that took it, for seven days at most, and kept files from being removed rather than records from being released. A pin names a session time and an allowance. While it stands no interval release passes it - one asked for past it plans to it and says so, or says the pin is why it gives up nothing - and no release by record number, which cannot say when its records were read, is made. Pins lie beside the generations in a file of their own, replaced whole under a lock every release that could give up a pinned record holds until it has published: a generation published beneath a follow fails the follow, and a pin is most needed while a capture records. A pin placed while a release planned is therefore honoured by it, or placed against what it published. A pin before the latest release's boundary is refused, naming the earliest moment a pin keeps from, and so is one allowing less than the session holds. Releases take leading runs, so a pin keeps everything after its moment: a follow keeping a rolling window that a pin holds back says so once and, when the session outgrows what the pin allows, stops - `icat capture` stops its capture - rather than release the pinned records or grow past what the person allowed, which is §20.2's "stop recording with an explicit reason". Pins that cannot be read refuse every release and stop a rolling follow; kept content stays a person's to release (ADR-036). `icat pin` lists, places and removes pins, `icat session` lists them and `icat retain` names the pin that held a release. The window's pins - its time scope pinned and the pins in its size line - are next.

Revision 443 gives the window the same pins (ADR-046, R18). **Keep records…**, beside the inspector's sharing actions, offers to keep every record from the time scope's start - a brush, or the range a zoom shows - or from the session's first record without one, never before the latest release's boundary, which it names when the scope began before it. It asks why, which a pin always says, and how large the session may grow, at least what it holds and in whole MiB, and lists the pins standing in `icat pin`'s words, each removable from there. The size line states the pins beneath the session's size and what its latest release kept: from when the earliest keeps every record, and the least any allows, as every projection of the session now carries it; a refusal - a pin allowing less than the session holds, pins that cannot be read - is said where the capture's status is. The dialog was checked rendered: it states the moment once, in the view's time base, and its action does not repeat it.

Revision 444 lets a follow mirror the content a capture kept (ADR-047, `contracts/content-v1.md` §2), the first of the two steps revisions 235 and 239 left between the broker and content. A recording keeps content beside the journal chunk whose records it belongs to, under that chunk's generation number, so that releasing the chunk releases it; a follow mirrored journal chunks alone and refused evidence that named a content chunk, so an elevated recorder could keep content only by deriving its own rows, and the broker, whose captures are evidence only, never. A follow now mirrors each chunk's content in the generation that mirrors the chunk, under that generation's number: the evidence's bytes unchanged, since a content chunk names its capture and no generation, checked against the digest the evidence recorded and read whole - every fragment, and the capture's identity - before it is staged. The derived session's releases therefore give up a chunk's content with it, a rolling follow's included, and its records find their content as the recording's own reader would. Evidence that released its content is refused, as evidence that released its records is, since the records' content would be gone without the session saying so. `icat record --evidence-only` now takes a content profile, and `icat follow` derives the session with its content. The broker still refuses a Content request it previews: its prepared plan admits the reviewed metadata-only policy alone (`contracts/broker-v1.md` §2) and its digest covers no content policy's limits or inspection consent. Lifting that - the prepared plan, its digest, the content limit as a stop reason and `icat capture`'s request - is next, and the window's content capture after it.

Revision 445 gives the window the rolling capture `icat capture --keep-last` has had since revision 439 (ADR-045 decision 6, S5), and draws a session that released an interval from where it keeps every record. ADR-045 had held the window back for one reason: its timeline would show the released time as a long partial gap before the window it keeps. The Explore card now asks what a capture keeps - every record of ten minutes, still the default that needs no configuration, or the last 10 minutes or the last hour of a capture that records until it is stopped, for up to a day - and says beneath the choice what it records and keeps and that the broker's journal limit can stop it sooner, since the broker's evidence keeps every record until the capture stops. The live follow runs the same policy between mirrors by its own writer, says the window while it records and, once a pin holds the window back, the pin; when the session outgrows what such a pin allows, the capture stops with the pin's reason and how to record on. A session that released an interval now begins its overview's extent, timeline and minimap at the first presentation tick wholly at or after the latest release's boundary: the rows kept from before it, as the evidence of what came after, are still its records, counted among its rows and in its rankings and evidence, but are not drawn, so no column reaches into what was released and none is a partial gap for a few nanoseconds of it. The whole session's coverage stays a partial gap, which the size line and every coverage statement say. A persisted overview records those rows as counted before its extent (`overview-index-v1` minor 4), so its columns and they add up to its timed rows, and one counted before this revision, from a kept row, is counted again; `icat evidence --from`, `icat retain --release-before`, `icat pin --from` and the window's search say of a moment in the released stretch that it lies in what the session released, from when it keeps every record, rather than that it is outside the session. With a session shown the card drops its heading with the rest of its first-run guidance, so its Keep choice leaves the smallest window's ranked list its three rows. Releasing the broker's evidence in coordination with its follower, which lets a rolling capture outlast its journal quota, is next.

Revision 446 lets a follow go on across releases of the evidence it follows (ADR-048), the first half of releasing the broker's evidence in coordination with its follower, which lets a rolling capture outlast its journal quota. A follow refused evidence that had released any chunk (ADR-044 decision 4): a follower numbers each record by counting every record before it, and nothing said how many records went before the chunks that remained, or where among the capture's chunks they lie. A chunk release now states what its recording gave up in all (`contracts/store-v1.md` §8), as `recording: { chunks, records }`: its own chunks and records added to the previous chunk release's statement, so the latest release, which every later generation carries, says it all. A release after one that stated nothing, or after a journal prefix rewrite or an interval release, which give records up without counting whole chunks, states nothing, and a follow still refuses its evidence. The follower places the derived session's chunks by their bytes - its oldest among the evidence's, or, where the evidence released the run's start, the evidence's oldest among the derived session's, which the statement must allow - and compares every chunk both hold; it counts the capture's chunks whole, the released ones among them, and a fresh follower takes the journal index of the evidence's oldest chunk from the records the statement gives, reading back only the chunks the evidence still holds before the session's. A derived session that holds none of the chunks the evidence keeps, and a follow into an empty session of evidence that released its first chunks, which would begin after records the session never held, are refused, saying why; a crashed follow's card counts the capture whole and its finish goes on from what remains. `icat session` says the statement beside the release. The revision's Release run caught a window test failing one run in six: at the smallest window the ranked list kept two rows and the start of a third only while the card's session path wrapped onto two lines, and a path that wrapped onto three - a temporary folder's name decided which - left the third row out of view. The path now takes one line, its middle segments giving way to an ellipsis and the whole path on hover, so the card's height no longer turns on it. Measured with a session opened as a person opens one, though, the card still leaves the list its 120 px floor: revision 398's 163 px held for the one-word status detail its test gave, not the sentence a saved session shows. Giving the list that room is next, and then the broker releasing the chunks its follow gave up, between its own publications and never the chunk its boundary names, told by the capture's owner as it renews its lease (ADR-048 decision 5); until then the broker's evidence keeps every record until the capture stops.

Revision 447 gives the smallest window's ranked list its room with a saved session opened as a person opens one (§6.1). Revision 446 found the list at its 120 px floor there, under two of its rows, because the card's detail for an opened session was a five-line sentence on what the graph draws; revision 398's 163 px had held for the one-word detail its test passed. That sentence now lives where the relationships are drawn. The graph pane's summary ends with what they are drawn from, in the ranked table's words - "paired TCP only", or "paired TCP and RPC calls" where calls linked through ALPC are drawn - and says in full on hover and to a screen reader that the graph draws admitted paired TCP and those calls, that a process's RPC calls are on its rung, and that every other observation is in the timeline (R21). The card says in one line which generation is open. Measured through the window's own open path at 1080 × 700, the card shows all it holds unscrolled and the list has 168 px, two rows and the start of a third; the window test now opens its session that way and checks that at least 12 px of the third row are in view. The broker releasing the chunks its follow gave up (ADR-048 decision 5) is next.

Revision 448 has the broker release what its follow gave up (ADR-048 decision 5), so the journal allowance of a capture that keeps a window bounds what its evidence holds rather than everything it recorded. A capture prepared with the new retention policy `ReleaseFollowed` (`contracts/broker-v1.md` §5.1), which needs live publication, is told by its owner, as the owner renews its lease, how many of the capture's oldest chunks its follow's session gave up: an optional, bounded field a broker that predates it skips, and a hint for the running capture that is never persisted. Between two publications - the one moment no chunk holds a generation's name - the recorder releases those it still holds by its own writer, whole, oldest first and never the newest, each release stating what the recording gave up in all, and stops counting them against the allowance; a release that fails is said with the stop and ends the releasing, never the capture. The window and `icat capture --keep-last` ask for the policy whenever the session keeps a window and say on each renewal what the follow gave up; the Keep choice, `icat capture`'s help and the capture's review say that the broker releases its own copy of what the session gave up. Measured with a scripted capture, a recording of 30,000 records whose follow gave up every chunk but its newest stayed within a 1 MiB allowance that 10,000 such records overrun when every chunk is kept, and a follower, the same one or a fresh one, numbered every record as the capture did. The live publication interval still follows the capture's maximum duration - every 85 s for a day-long rolling capture, with only the live edge in between - where the window it keeps should set it; that is next.

Revision 449 lets a rolling capture publish as often as the window it keeps needs (`contracts/broker-v1.md` §5.1). Its live interval had followed its day-long limit - 85 s, so for most of a minute the window's exact view waited and only its live edge moved - because one capture was to publish no more than about 1,024 chunks. A capture whose broker releases what its follow gave up holds only its window and a quarter more, so the bound that matters is what it holds: a prepare request now names the window its follow keeps, as an optional field a broker that predates it skips and only for that retention, digested last and only when named so every other plan keeps its digest, and `Live` compiles to the shorter of what its duration and its window need - every 2 s for the last ten minutes, every 5 s for the last hour. The review states the window and checks the interval against it, and the window and `icat capture --keep-last` send it. Since a follow that stops giving chunks up - a pin holding its window back, a stalled viewer - would leave the evidence one more chunk at every publication, such a capture stops once it holds 2,048, twice what its interval is set for, saying why, and its evidence is finalized as a limit's is.

Revision 450 projects a rolling capture's stop from what its window holds (§12.1 S5, IC-016a). The size line still projected a capture keeping a window as one keeping everything: its journal's rate was the session's journal over the whole time recorded, so once the session began releasing, the rate fell as the capture ran, and a stop at its journal limit - which the broker no longer reaches while it releases what the follow gave up (revision 448) - was said to come later at every publication. A session's measured size now says from when it keeps every record, and its rates are taken over the session time it holds, which also corrects a capture that keeps everything after a person released part of its session. A capture's limits carry the window its broker releases for: its session holds the window and the quarter past it between releases, and its evidence what the session holds and, until the owner's next lease renewal says so and the broker's next publication releases it, what the session gave up. At the rate so far that is all its journal and disk grow to, so they stop it only if the window outgrows them, and the line says what keeping the window takes of the journal - "keeping the last 10 minutes takes up to about 76.2 MiB of its 1 GiB journal" - that free disk stays above its reserve, and, before the session first releases and while that comes before the stop, when retention begins releasing: the point S5 asks to be visible. A pin keeps the session past its window once the window passes the pin's moment; the line then projects its journal and disk growing from there, and when a release step finds the session holding more than a pin allows, which stops the capture - the release steps come a quarter of the window apart from the latest release, so the first to find the pin keeping the session may come before the session has held a window and a quarter from the pin's moment, and the projection takes whichever stops it first. A pin placed allowing what the session holds, as the window's dialog offers, stops it about a window later, which the line now says as soon as the pin is placed. `icat capture --keep-last` states the same in the same words, from the effective quota the broker reviewed rather than the one it asked for.

Revision 451 has the broker prepare a content capture, the step revision 444 named next (ADR-049, `contracts/broker-v1.md` §2.1). The broker had refused every Content request: `icat record` runs elevated, so whoever names a process to it may read that process anyway, but the broker runs elevated for clients that are not, and keeping the content of whatever process a client named would let an ordinary user read through it what another user's process, or their own elevated one, sends and receives. A client now names only processes it could read itself - its own user's, in its own logon session, the owner every broker capture belongs to, at no higher integrity - and the broker reads each from the process, never the request (P19): it holds the process open as a content capture's process filter does, the one way the product opens a process, and reads its token's user, logon session and integrity and when it started through that handle. Another user's, another sign-in's, a higher integrity's or one not running is refused by name, under a refusal code of its own. Each process is then named by its ID and its start (R22), which the plan pins, the digest covers - after every other field and only for a content plan, so every other plan keeps its digest, which a test now pins - and the review states beside each ID, so a client can compare it with the process it meant. Preparing one meant teaching the plan's own checks what a content plan is: the request names its sources, the lifecycle its processes rest on and its one admitted source, whose descriptors carry the content policy while every other source's keep metadata only, each with at most one content slot sized by a length field before it; its scope is that source filtered to the processes before anything is kept and the rest lifecycle metadata, which needs no broader-capture consent; and limits or consent the request and the policy disagree on are refused rather than reconciled. Start still refuses a content capture, before writing any intent, saying it is prepared for review only: holding its processes while it records, checking each is still the one prepared, and stopping at the content limit - which the runtime would otherwise report as its duration's - come next, then `icat capture --profile content` and the window's.

Revision 452 has the broker start a content capture (ADR-049 decisions 2 and 4, `contracts/broker-v1.md` §2.1). Start had refused one, before writing any intent, because nothing held a named process while the capture recorded: a process that exited after preparing could have its ID given to another, whose exchanges the provider's process filter would then keep. The runtime now holds each named process from before its session starts until its evidence is finalized, opening it again as preparation did, and checks through the handle that holds it that it is still the process prepared - started when the plan pinned - and still the client's own; one that has exited, whose ID now names a process that started at another time, or that is no longer the client's fails the start by name before any capture directory or session exists, and holds nothing. The lifecycle coordinator now hands the runtime the authenticated client the start came from, so the check reads the same owner preparation did; a runtime that is not told it, or cannot hold processes, keeps no content. Two silences were closed on the way. A session whose content source could not be enabled - its process gone before the filter held it - would have recorded lifecycle alone and called itself started; it now stops at once and fails its start, saying why. And a capture whose kept content reached its session limit stopped, as the recorder already did for `icat record`, but its status said its duration had elapsed; it now says the content it kept reached the limit its request allows, which a test pins with the other stop reasons, now said from one place both the Windows runtime and its tests reach. `icat record`'s help had said `icat capture --profile content` takes the same request through the broker, which it does not yet; that sentence waits for it, and the catalog's description says the broker keeps content from the asking user's own processes alone. Next: `icat capture --profile content`, whose review shows each process's start beside its ID, then the window's.

Revision 453 brings the broker's content capture to the command line (ADR-049). `icat capture --profile content` takes a content request in `icat record`'s words - its source, `--mechanism http`, the processes, the channels, both byte limits and the consent to inspect what is kept - now read for both commands in one place, where a `--pid` names one process or several separated by commas. It reads each named process before it launches the broker, so one not running is refused before Windows asks for approval, and once the broker has prepared the capture it compares the start the broker pinned for each process with the one it saw under that ID: a process whose ID passed to another between the two is refused, both starts said, and the prepared plan is let go unstarted - the client's half of R22, beside the broker's own check at start. The review names each process by what it runs and the start the broker pinned, the channels, both limits and whether a person may see what is kept; the result says how much of how many messages the session kept - whole, cut to the record limit, or not kept once the content limit was reached - and never a byte of them. A content capture keeps what it records until its content limit stops it, so `--keep-last` is refused with it rather than combining two retentions, and so is `--allow-broader`, since a content scope is never broader than its processes. The request's parsing, the review and the result's sums are platform-neutral, so their tests run everywhere; launching the broker stays Windows's. On the way the content validator names a mechanism in words - "does not describe TCP" - as R5 asks. Next: the window's content capture.

Revision 454 gives the window its content capture (§11.1, ADR-049). The Explore card's Keep choice now lists the messages of the person's own processes beside its choices of records, so the card grows no taller at the smallest window, and its start then reads "Choose processes…". A dialog lists the processes of this sign-in whose start the window can read - each by name, ID and start, filtered by name or ID, refreshed on request, at most 64 chosen - with the most one message and the capture may keep, how long it records, and the consent to see what is kept, unchecked until a person checks it; it says first what is kept, over HTTPS as plaintext, and that only the person's own processes can be kept. The broker then prepares the capture, and a second dialog shows its review - each process by what it runs and the start the broker pinned, the limits, the consent, how long it records, what its sources collect - and starts nothing until Start, opening on Cancel. A review takes as long as a person reads, longer than the 30 seconds a prepared plan is held for, so the window prepares it again when they start it and starts that plan only when it keeps what was reviewed, naming what changed when it does not; and, before the review, a process whose ID passed to another since it was chosen is refused, as `icat capture` refuses it. When the capture ends, the card says how much of how many messages the session kept and never a byte of them, in the words `icat capture` now shares through the application layer. What both clients say of a content capture's processes - seen, compared, reviewed, changed - moved into the broker's client library, where its tests run on every platform; the window's own decision between review and start is a step of its runner that tests drive with fakes. The WinINet source and the one channel selector that names every channel are now defined once, in the domain, for every client to name. Next: HTTP/2 and compressed responses through WinINet's capture, and a fixture decoder (§11.2's `DecodedFields`).

Revision 455 is §11.2's small synthetic fixture decoder, which proves the model a protocol's decoder will follow (`contracts/content-v1.md` §4). A person asks for a decoding - Decode in the content viewer once its bytes are shown, or `icat content --decode` - and nothing decodes a record unasked. The decoder, `intercat-content-fixture` at version 1, reads only the records InterCat's own content fixture raises, and only bytes kept with consent to inspect them; asked of bytes kept without that consent, it says it never decodes them. It reads at most the 64 KiB a view shows, within 250 ms, and says where it stopped. A decoding names its decoder and version, the record whose fragment it read and the bytes it read, and each field - the message's number, its conversation's, and its filler - with the bytes it came from, which the viewer shows in its hex view at a button's press. It says what it did not decode and why: the rest of a message the capture cut, a header the bytes end inside, or a byte message, which leaves the header at once. A number the bytes end on is not decoded, since it could have been longer. A decoding is made again each time it is asked for and never kept: `icat content --json` refuses `--decode`, and no package carries one. `icat content --decode` answers partially only where `--reveal` would: bytes kept without consent, or never kept. Next: HTTP/2 and compressed responses through WinINet's capture.

Revision 456 persists the deeper levels of S4's pyramid for a finished session (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md`, M5). A zoom of a reopened session opened every segment its interval met and built that segment's tiles from its time and mechanism columns. Persisting those tiles alone would not have spared it: a column boundary falls among some tile's records in nearly every segment a wide zoom meets, and a segment's columns carry one checksum each, so reading those few records meant reading the whole column. The tile index therefore keeps, for each segment, its finest tiles and each level ten times wider up to one block, with every finest tile's readings and mechanisms beside them, compactly. Each block of 32 tiles, and each block of records, carries a checksum of its own. It is published with the checkpoint, the overview and the operation index, by the same writers, and is read by the block, never hashed whole first. An unfocused zoom counts each segment from its top level down: a tile whole wherever its records fall in one column, a tile's children where a boundary falls among its records, and a finest tile's records where one does. That is exactly what its rows count, it opens no segment, and it reads per boundary rather than per record (S3). At 1,000,000 records the index took 2.0% of the segments' bytes. A reopened session's first zoom of its whole extent in 1,000 columns took 16.5 ms against 56; half of it 10.3 ms against 28; and a hundredth in 256 columns 1.1 ms against 22. A part of the index that cannot be read is refused, never misread: the zoom counts from the segments and the generation's index is not read again. The window opens a session without hashing a file that checks itself, and the command line, which hashes every file, opens the generation before one whose tile index changed. A segment whose readings go backwards keeps no tiles and has its rows read. A focus, which filters by owner, direction or channel, still reads its rows. A generation published before this revision gains its tile index when its writer next publishes, or with `icat checkpoint`. Next: HTTP/2 and compressed responses through WinINet's capture, and a level spanning segments for sessions of many.

Revision 457 lets a finished session's brush open no segment either (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md` minor 1, S3). A brush counts the records inside its interval per process, edge and channel: what the ranked table and the graph show for it (§6.4). Since revision 401 it read only the segments its interval met, but its first touch of each bound every row's owner and channel, and a warm brush still read every row inside it. At 10M rows its first brush took 631 ms, and later ones 273 ms at the 95th percentile, against §12's 250 ms. Each record of a tile index now keeps what a brush reads of its bindings - its owner's instance and the binding's strength, all an evidence policy's admission needs, and its channel's number - and each tile keeps them tallied. A brush counts a segment from its top level down, as a zoom does: a tile its interval holds whole from its tallies, and only the records of the tiles its two ends fall among. That is exactly what its rows count, under every policy. The bindings are kept as the checkpoint's derivation made them, and the index names that derivation: its rules and a fingerprint of its instances and channels in order. A brush counts them only under the same; otherwise it reads its segments, and the writer's next publication publishes the index again, as it does a revision-456 index, which kept no bindings. Where records take turns among many processes and channels, as the scale gate's 200 connections do record by record, a narrow tile's tallies would take as many bytes as its records. A tile therefore keeps its tallies only where they take at most half the bytes of the records beneath it, and a brush otherwise counts it from its children or records, at most twice the reading. Each finest tile's records now carry a checksum of their own, so a column boundary reads only the tile it falls among: a 2,000-column zoom of 10M rows took 7 ms at the median, where reading a whole block of records for the one tile took 12 to 15 ms once bindings had doubled its records. At 10M rows the brushed ranking took 6.3 ms the first time and 9.5 ms at the 95th percentile. The index took 6.1% of the segments' bytes, 10.9 bytes a record, from 2.9%. At 1M rows a brush of the whole extent took 3.5 ms warm against 20 from the segments' rows, and 37 ms the first time against 586. Next: §6.2's density regime, which the 2,000 × 40 lane query waits on, and the working set at 10M rows.

Revision 458 counts a group's lanes from the tile index too (§6.2, S3, S2). Descending into an executable group draws each member's lane beneath the group's own row, and counting them bound every row of the segments the view met: at 10M rows a 40-process group's first lanes took 850 ms, and every segment's bindings then stayed in memory, which kept the session's working set near 1.35 GB. A group's focus - its members' records, of every mechanism or one - is what the tiles' owner tallies hold, so a tile lying in one of the view's columns and one of the lanes' counts there whole, and only a tile a boundary of either falls among has its records read. That is exactly what its rows count, folded members and lanes past the cell budget included. A focus on one process, which also counts its source directions, on a channel, which counts its ends, or narrowed to one direction or end, still reads its rows. Each segment's section is counted by a worker of its own, as a focus that reads rows counts its segments. A dense count reads a block's records at once rather than a tile's at a time, and a reader keeps up to 16,384 decoded blocks, about 25 MB, so a group drawn at 2,000 columns, which reads most of a 10M-row session's blocks, reads them once. On a 4-core machine at 10M rows, the group's lanes at the window's 256 columns took 70 ms the first time and 20 ms at the median after; at 2,000 columns, 48 ms the first time and 78 ms at the 95th percentile. The session's working set at the end of the scale gate fell from 1.35 GB to 263 MB, since none of these opens a segment. Next: §6.2's density regime, which the 2,000 × 40 lane query's cell budget waits on.

Revision 459 counts a process's rung from the tile index too (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md` minor 2, §6.2, S3). Descending to one process draws its records by source direction (L2), which the tiles did not hold, so its first view bound every row of the segments the view met: at 10M rows the busiest process's first direction rows took 2.5 s. Each record of the index now keeps its source direction, and each tile's owner tallies are kept per direction, so a process's focus, its rows by direction, and any owner focus narrowed to one direction count as a group's do: whole tiles from their tallies, the records of the tiles a boundary falls among. That is exactly what its rows count. Only a channel's focus, which counts its ends, and an operation's still read rows. At 10M rows the process's first direction rows took 156 ms, and about 45 ms after against the 30 its cached rows took, opening no segment; the index took 7.5% of the segments' bytes, from 6.1%. A minor-1 index, which kept no directions, is published again by the writer's next publication. Next: a channel's ends, and §6.2's density regime.

Revision 460 counts a channel's rung from the tile index (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md` minor 3, §6.2, S3): the last focus whose first view bound every row of the segments it met. A channel's timeline draws each of its two ends with what it sent and what it received apart (L3), which the tiles did not hold, and at 10M rows the busiest connection's first view took 2.9 s. Each record of the index now keeps which end of its connection it was made at, as the relation rule reads it from the record's own endpoints, and each tile's channel tallies are kept by mechanism, direction and end, so a paired channel's focus and its ends, a one-sided connection's, and either narrowed to one end, direction or mechanism count as an owner focus does: exactly what their rows count. Kept in one run with the owner tallies and judged by one rule, the larger channel tallies would have left fewer tiles keeping either, and brushes and groups would have read more records; so a tile's owner and channel tallies are now kept apart, each only where it takes at most a third of the bytes of the records beneath it, and each query reads only the part it counts. A rule of a half kept tallies on narrow tiles whose records each have an owner and a channel of their own, where reading a tally entry saves nothing over reading a record, and grew the index by two thirds. A record's mechanism, direction and end take one varint byte where its mechanism and direction took two, so the index stays at 7.6% of the segments' bytes, as revision 459's did, while holding the ends. At 10M rows the connection's first ends took 192 ms, and 22 to 84 ms after, opening no segment; the scale gate now measures them, at 27 to 36 ms at the 95th percentile. A focus on a channel and owners at once, or on an operation, still reads its rows, as does one narrowed to a code no direction is. A minor-2 index, which kept no ends, is published again by the writer's next publication. Next: §6.2's density regime.

Revision 461 takes a focus on the whole session's records of one mechanism - what choosing an L0 mechanism lane, or its cell, puts in focus - from the whole timeline's own counts (§6.2, S3). That focus is exactly the timeline's count of that mechanism in each column, which the zoom has already counted from the tiles, yet it read every row of the segments its view met: at 10M rows its first view took 1.32 s and later ones about 120 ms, holding all forty segments open. It now opens no segment where the whole timeline does not, and took 36 ms the first time and 4 to 12 ms after. A focus narrowed to a source direction too still reads its rows, since no count holds directions apart, and so does a code no mechanism is, which no count holds at all. Next: §6.2's density regime.

Revision 462 draws §6.2's density regime for every view a person zooms or pans to, a column per device pixel, with its minimum drawn width and pointer snapping; §6.2 states what it built. Each count now asks up to 2,000 columns where it asked at most 256, so the scale gate times a group's lanes and a connection's two ends at 2,000. Next: the whole session counted per device pixel too, where the overview's 64 columns are still drawn as bars.

Revision 463 counts the whole session a column per device pixel too, wherever that is finer than the overview's 64 columns, which stand in until the count arrives; §6.2 states what it changed. The scale gate's L0 views already begin with the whole extent at 2,000 columns. §6.2 now also says where its band and event regimes are reached, only at sub-tick zoom, and how their intent is met elsewhere.

Revision 464 lists each run of the interval table's empty cells as one row, so the timeline's table equivalent stays readable at a column per device pixel (R15); §6.2 says how.

Revision 465 keeps the interval table's selected row on the analysis interval however it was chosen, so the table never highlights an interval the timeline has left (R15); §6.2 says how.

Revision 466 lists a chosen timeline cell's first records in the inspector, exactly those E lists first from it, and titles the inspector for a chosen cell or time range (§6.2, §6.4, I5); §6.2 says how.

Revision 467 has the window say cell and column, and `icat timeline` interval, wherever they said bucket (R5, R18); §6.2 says which word names what.

Revision 468 opens a record listed beside a chosen cell in E, selected there, on Enter or a double click (§6.4); §6.2 says how.

Revision 469 gives the inspector an edge as the rail has, so its explanations and a record's fields can be read on fewer lines, and keeps the main panes their least width when the window narrows; §6.1's column widths row says how. Keeping the two widths in the investigation, as the split is kept, is next.

Revision 470 keeps them there (`workspace-v20`): the ranked table's width once a person set it by its edge, and the inspector's once widened from its design, in whole pixels, with the split, put back for any of the investigation's sessions and said where the panes are said - its window, the notice of a session opened from it, and `icat workspace show`, whose document carries them (`workspace-resolution-v23`). A width the window fits to itself is not kept: the rail following the window, or the room a narrowed window takes back for its main panes. Kept panes that set no rail width let the rail follow the window again. The contract's package section, which still said a package's file is written as version 18, now names the current version.

Revision 471 highlights in the graph what a chosen timeline cell's records belong to - its relationships, or the nodes of its processes - fainter than a selection (§6.4); §6.4 says how.

Revision 472 walks the investigation by keyboard alone, M5's accessible keyboard workflow, and adds `F6`, which moves the keyboard between the window's panes (§6.7); §6.7 says how.

Revision 473 marks in the relationship and ranked tables the rows a chosen cell's graph highlight stands for (R15), and keeps a multi-selection's mark on a row drawn after it was made; §6.4 says how.

Revision 474 keeps a ranked row chosen when its rows are restated - a brush, an interval's row, another ranking - and gives the keyboard to E's table after Enter on an interval or `E` in a list it rebuilds, and to an empty rung's reason (§6.7); §6.7 says how.

Revision 475 lists every key the window takes, by where it acts, in a sheet `F1` or the status strip's Keys button opens (§6.7, R15); §6.7 says how.

Revision 476 makes a chosen record's facts a list the keyboard reads, a fact at a time, and corrects §6.5's first paragraph, which listed inspector tabs the window never had; §6.5 says how.

Revision 477 makes the graph say the node its arrows move to, and the timeline the moment `[` and `]` step to, while each has the keyboard (§6.5, R15); §6.5 says how.

Revision 478 walks the investigation window by keyboard alone: its timeline says the column its keys move to, it opens with the keyboard on its session and never leaves it on nothing, `Ctrl`+`Tab` shows its pages, and Enter shows a note on the timeline (§6.5, R15); §6.5 says how.

Revision 479 lists the investigation window's keys in a sheet of their own, which `F1` and its Keys (F1) button open (§6.7, R15); §6.7 says how.

Revision 480 holds every dialog and prompt to opening with the keyboard on one of its own controls and closing on Esc, and gives the Align, translation and note dialogs Enter or `Ctrl`+`Enter` as their action (§6.7, R15); §6.7 says how.

Revision 481 gives the keyboard, in the windows a record opens, to what they read once read, and to Close where there is nothing to read (§6.7, R15); §6.7 says how.

Revision 482 keeps the main window's keyboard through a capture and a session opened: on Stop while a capture records, on the ranked table once a session is shown, and on the control that asked when nothing opened (§6.7, R15); §6.7 says how.

Revision 483 keeps it where the held-generation banner and an unfinished capture's card let it go: on the ranked table of the view the banner showed, and on the card's button or Start exploring (§6.7, R15); §6.7 says how.

Revision 484 corrects revision 482's Stop, which a view rebuilt under the keyboard during a recording left the keyboard on, where Enter stopped the capture, and places a keyboard that any control going from under it left on nothing (§6.7, R15); §6.7 says how.

Revision 485 shows the session as saved to a view paused when its capture ends, and never says recording continues once it has stopped (§6.4), and gives the keyboard of a list whose rows are restated back to the row chosen in it (§6.7, R15); §6.4 and §6.7 say how.

Revision 486 announces the main window's detail line too, which revision 334 left quiet for a package's progress. That line is where the window says what a person's action did - an export or a sharing report saved, a package or a pin, a record that could not be opened, a session opened from an investigation and what it put back - and a screen reader heard none of it. It is now a polite live region, and what changes too often to hear is written in silence: a package's progress by the percent, and a recording's newer generation, which restates the capture's count and chunks every second or two, with any notice its view appends, as a rolling capture's release clipping a brush that reached into what it gave up. The capture headline, live since revision 334, had said each generation's count aloud; it now says what changes the capture's state - starting, recording, stopping, saved or unavailable - and what an action did. A detail said aloud is shown from its first line, where a person reading a long one had scrolled its lines; one restated in silence leaves them where they are read.

Revision 486 has the main window say aloud what a person's action did, and never a recording's count or a package's percent alone (R15); revision 334's paragraph says how.

Revision 487 finishes what revision 333 began. Eleven of the main window's controls began their names with their label, as it asks, then went on to explain themselves: the Per lane, Per second and Wall clock toggles, the two Expand toggles, Theme, the interval table's three lane choices, and the two edges that resize the rail and the inspector. The search box's name was its whole explanation, 143 characters a screen reader said at every Ctrl+F, while its help said only its placeholder. A screen reader says a control's name each time the keyboard lands on it, so each is now named in the words a person says - "Per lane", "Search names, PIDs or a time", "Mechanism the interval table lists" - and what it does is its help text, said after it, as the tooltip already gave the toggles. The main window's audit holds every button, choice, field and edge to a name of at most 40 characters at every rung, and the wall clock's toggle where a capture recorded its clock. The investigation window's dialogs name some fields by visible labels longer than that, which a later pass would shorten with them.

Revision 487 names the main window's controls in the words a person says, what each does its help text (R15); revision 333's paragraph says how.

Revision 488 has a view's disclosure say what its evidence holds and leaves out, never how the window reaches it (R18). The disclosure every view of a session states beneath its inspector, and every export repeats as its first caveat, ended with the window's guidance: source records are one step (E) from every rung, Enter on a record opens its original journal entry, and byte previews stay hidden until requested. In a JSON or CSV file those keys mean nothing; in the window the rail's hints and the keys sheet say them where they act, and a record's kept content says itself that its bytes are shown only when asked. The disclosure, a session's or one whose graph joins RPC calls through ALPC, now ends with what TCP and UDP records are, completed transfers with no operation rung, and leaves its lines beneath the inspector to the selected record at the smallest window.

Revision 489 has the investigation's Align dialog name its fields by the words beside them, as revision 333 has buttons named by theirs (R15, WCAG 2.5.3). Its eight boxes were named by sentences of their own - "How sure the instant is, as a duration with its unit" beside the word "within" - which left out the captions a person sees, so one who says what they see could not reach them. Each box is now labelled by its caption, which a screen reader says as its name, and its hint beside it is its help text, said after it. The choice of session to align to is named "Session to align to", and what it may be is its help. The other dialogs name their fields by sentences holding the short words they show beside them, "at" or "Note".

Revision 490 has a package say before it is saved everything its investigation's file carries (§11.3). Revision 263 counted what the file held then - names given to hosts, alignments, join decisions and the notes on them - and the investigation has since kept host confirmations, address translations, its own notes, saved views, each session's layout and the window's panes, which every package carried and its confirmation never named. A translation holds endpoints as a person typed them, and a note keeps every wording it had: a reworded note's earlier words, and a removed note's, stay in the file as revisions. The confirmation and `icat workspace package` now count each kind the file holds, a note once however often it was reworded, and say that names, notes and translated endpoints go as they were written, never pseudonymized - in a package of redacted packages too, whose sessions alone are pseudonymized - and that a reworded or removed note goes with every wording it had. Measuring which lines the suites reach found it: `icat workspace package` had never run in a test, nor had most of the steps M4's two-host check runs on the command line. A test now runs them on the demo's two hosts, each with what it says and the exit code a script reads: adding them, refusing an alignment by boot across two boots, aligning them by their wall clocks, comparing within and beyond their uncertainty, correlating, deciding a candidate and withdrawing the decision and then the alignment, naming a host, confirming two hosts one and stating a translation, each withdrawn as a revision, relinking a moved session and refusing another's, and packaging - measured, written and verified, refused into a folder that exists, chosen with `--only`, and partial, exiting 1, when a session is missing.

Revision 491 ran what `icat` prints for a person where its tests had read only the JSON a script reads, and fixed what that found (R5, R18). `icat profiles` named a profile's admission, a source's decision and its overhead by their enumerations - "MetadataOnly", "Blocking", "Moderate" - and a focused request's mechanism as "Tcp"; they read "metadata only", "blocks the start", "moderate" and "TCP" now, through `CapabilityText.Admission` beside the capability words and the window's mechanism names. Its catalog said the flight recorder was unavailable because rolling retention, its oldest time, pins and freezing were "not implemented yet", all of which revisions 434 to 450 built as a way of keeping a capture; it now says a rolling capture is the flight recorder and how to start one, and §9.4 says so. A content request for a source no contract admits said no source could enforce content rules "yet", where WinINet's HTTP exchanges have since revision 239; it names that source. An unavailable profile's reason was printed twice. `icat package --original` said it reopened one more file than its files line listed, counting the manifest, and says what it verified in the window's words now; a redacted package asked for its review twice. `icat workspace --help` named the investigation's file `workspace-v4`, sixteen versions old, and said every package is unredacted, which one of redacted packages is not. A test runs each of them, with a compaction and a recovery review that find nothing to do. The engineering tools that qualify a build - `icat bench`, `icat measure`, their workloads and probe journals - keep the plan's terms, as revision 318 decided.

Revision 492 has every window flow behind a file or folder choice run whole in a test (§6.4, §8.4, §11.3, §20.6). The windows asked the platform's pickers directly, which a test cannot answer, so measuring what the suites reach found those flows untested past the choice: exporting the view, the redacted sharing report, the support bundle, opening a saved session or an investigation and starting one, the redacted and original session packages, the investigation's package, its adding and relinking of sessions, and saving a record's chosen bytes. Each window now asks through one seam that the platform answers and a test can answer in its place, and a test runs each flow with a choice made, none made, and one the flow cannot use: a file named .csv is written as CSV and one named .json as JSON, a choice declined writes and says nothing, a folder that holds no session is said to hold none, a new investigation is made at the name chosen and never written over one there, a package is a new folder under the one chosen and opens to review, several sessions are added from one choice, a session moved is relinked to where it is now, exactly the chosen bytes are saved, and a file that cannot be written is said. Each flow did what it says.

Revision 493 builds §6.8's motion in the graph: nodes travel to new places in the level change's 200 ms and a new selection's ring settles in the selection's 120 ms, both theme tokens, and the platform's reduced-motion setting draws every change where it ends at once; the paragraph after §6.8's Motion says how.

Revision 495 reads the size line's instants on the time base too, which a tour of the window's later states found it did not. Since revision 421 every instant the view states is read on the wall clock once a person chooses it, but the size line beneath the capture card kept the command line's session seconds for the three it states - from when a session keeps every record after an interval release, from when its pins keep them, and the pin whose allowance stops a rolling capture - so a pin placed from the time scope was confirmed as "+3580.000000 s" and stated a line below as "3580.000 s", and on the wall clock as session seconds beside a wall-clock axis. The window now states all three as it states any instant, on the view's time base, and again when the time base is toggled; the generation carries an interval release as facts beside its sentence, so the window can word it, and the command line keeps its words. A tour of the paused view, a rolling capture holding a pin, a saved session with pins and the content capture's review, at the smallest and the default size in the dark, light and high-contrast modes, found no text cut off and nothing else to change.

Revision 496 measured which of the Desktop's code no test ran, and ran it as a person would: each investigation dialog opened from its button, a session or a column opened from the investigation, and the merged time under the pointer had run only through the methods behind them. Running them found that the investigation's window, opened centred over InterCat's and owned by it, was one Windows holds in front of its owner, so a session it opened - by Open, Enter on a session or a column double-clicked - was shown in InterCat's window behind it while it said the session was opened there, and InterCat's window kept the keyboard on the control that last had it: Start exploring, when it had shown nothing, whose Enter starts a capture. The investigation's window is now a peer of InterCat's, opened centred over it on its screen, one per investigation file: an investigation opened again is brought in front, restored, rather than shown twice, since two windows of one file would each write it, and each closes with InterCat's window, whose sessions it opens. A session opened from it brings InterCat's window in front, restored, with the keyboard on its ranked table, laid out first if it was not yet, and the investigation come back to has the keyboard where it was. Running the merged time found two defects more: a zoom out did not undo the zoom in before it, since halving an odd span and doubling it back fell a tick short of the whole, which stayed zoomed with Whole investigation still offered; and a third click within the double-click time opened a column a second time. A zoom's span is now rounded up, and a column opens on the double click alone.

Revision 501 wrote another session into a shown session's folder, as a package made again at the same name does, emptied one and removed one, and asked the window for everything it reads. Each read failed, as it must, but not in a person's words: a lease said the session identity had changed after its store was opened, which names no next step; one of an emptied folder said that no session had ever been published there, and one of a removed folder that retention might be removing files. The window kept sentences of its own for the case, in three wordings, which no read could reach. And the registry revision 427 made opened the folder afresh for a reader that asked for a session by its identity, and handed it a store of whatever the folder now held: Keep records offered the other session's pins and placed its pin there, Share original session would have packaged it, the channel browser said the generation shown was gone, and counting candidates projected it and let the answer go without a word. A lease of a store that has read a session now says, once its folder holds another, "This folder now holds another session. Open it again to read its records.", and once it holds none, "This folder no longer holds the session opened from it: its files were moved or deleted." The registry refuses a reader of one session the store of another, or of none, in the same words; the package, the pins, the channel browser and every read of the shown session ask for it by its identity, and the window's own checks are gone. A choice of evidence policy that cannot be counted says why on the capture card, and a zoom whose own count cannot be read says that the overview's columns are drawn instead, and why.

Revision 502 changed an investigation's members under its open window, as revision 501 changed a shown session's folder: another session written into one member's folder, another's emptied. The window's rows were read when it opened or was last refreshed, so they still said each was present, and Open, or a column of the merged time, opened the folder as it stood: InterCat's window showed the other session, and the investigation said it had opened its member. A member is now opened by its identity as well as its path. InterCat's window refuses a folder that holds another session, or none, in revision 501's words, and keeps what it showed - another session at that path too, which it no longer zooms as the member - and the investigation reads its sessions again and says why the member did not open, in the words its row now says. Those words were a step short: another session at a member's path said only that it was not the member, and a folder holding no session said that no session had ever been published there, though the member was one when it was added. Each now ends with relinking the member where it is now, and a screen reader, which added that step to every such row, says it once.

Revision 505 changed an investigation's alignments under its window while its merged time was zoomed. The zoom is a range of the investigation's time, which is the clock of the session the first alignment named, and the window kept it whatever changed. With every alignment withdrawn no session was placed, and Zoom in, Zoom out and Whole investigation still waited to act on a range of no clock. With another session's clock made the investigation's time - every alignment withdrawn and another made, here or by `icat workspace align` while the window stayed open - the range was read on the new clock, an unrelated stretch that held nothing. And the whole a zoom is kept within was read only while the timeline showed it, so after a change made while zoomed, Zoom out stopped at the investigation as it had been, or jumped to the whole. Each read of the merged time now states the whole investigation's time and whose clock it is. A zoom is let go once that clock is another's or none, or once no placed session meets it, the timeline showing the whole again and the window saying why after what the change did; a zoom that still meets a session stays, and zooms within the investigation as it is now.

Revision 506 read the capture card at both rail widths in every phase a capture passes through - none yet, one that could not start, starting, recording, stopping and saved - with processes named by long image paths, chosen and down every rung. Revision 319 had let one button's words wrap where the rail was narrower than they were; the card's own primary action had not been read there in the phases that show it. With the rail at its narrowest, "Start exploring (Ctrl+R)" lost its shortcut before a capture and once one was saved, and "Retry exploring (Ctrl+R)" once one could not start - the window's first action, cut off where a person meets it first. Every button's words now wrap where their place is narrower than they are, so no action is cut off, the long names already wrapping or ending in an ellipsis their tooltips complete.

Revision 510 names the main window by what it shows, as the investigation's window has been named by its file. It was "InterCat · IPC Explorer" whatever it showed, so switching between windows listed several InterCat windows a person could not tell apart, and a screen reader said no more of it as it came to the front. It is now named by the session's folder - "InterCat · explore-20260926T153534Z-1" - and while a capture runs by what it does: "InterCat · Starting a capture", "InterCat · Recording" and the folder once it has one, and "InterCat · Saving" and the folder as it stops. Before any session, and once a capture that could not start leaves none shown, it keeps its product's name.

Revision 512 has a dialog the system could not show choose nothing and say why, where it never took a window down. Every flow behind a choice asked the platform's picker from a handler nothing else answered for, and none caught a picker that failed - a shell that refused it, no service to show one - so its failure ended InterCat with every window it had open. The one seam revision 492 gave every choice now asks the platform inside a guard: a picker that fails chooses nothing, as one declined does, and its window says why where it says what its actions did. The main window says "Nothing could be chosen" and that nothing was opened or saved; the investigation's window that nothing was added, relinked or written; a record's content window that nothing was saved. Where a picker would start is a convenience: a system that cannot find that folder still shows the picker, starting where it starts it.

Revision 515 lets sessions' folders dropped on an investigation's window join it, as InterCat's window opens a session dropped on it (revision 514). They are added as the folders Add sessions chooses are, several at once, each refused as it says - already a member, or no session - and an investigation file dropped there opens in a window of its own. A file of another kind is not added, which the window says beside what each addition did. Doing so found that every action that writes the investigation - an addition, a relink, an alignment, a note - read it again only when no reading was under way. One that finished during a reading, as the window opened or its Refresh read, said nothing of what it did, and showed what that reading found, which may have looked before it wrote. A reading asked for meanwhile now waits for that one, then reads again with its own words.

Revision 520 says once what a session's investigation could not keep. A session opened from its investigation keeps there what a person changes - its pins and view settings, the window's panes (§26.3) - and a change its file could not take added a sentence beside the session's status; the next added another, so dragging the split over an investigation that had been moved or hand-edited past reading grew the card a sentence at a time, and once a change was kept again the card still said none was. Now what could not be kept is said once, in place of what was said of it before, however often changes fail, and taken back once one is kept. A coverage pass over the window found the path no test had run; the same pass found a package stopped by its own button partway, or one that could not be made, run by no test either, and they now are: each saves nothing, leaves no unsaved folder, says which, and offers the package again. Reading why the check of a session's files, which follows its first view (store-v1 §6), was left partly untested found that revision 519 had made it wrong: a package begun while the files were checked held the session shown, so the check's reopening of the last complete generation was refused, and the card said no earlier generation was complete. A package asked for now waits until the files are checked, saying so, and is asked about what the check leaves shown; a check that finds the files changed while something holds the session leaves it as it is, as it did for an open or a capture under way.

Revision 522 has what an investigation says read as its window reads. What the workspace said named a session, a capture, a host or a note by all 32 hex digits of its identity, while the window's rows and icat name each by its first eight, the leading part a command names a member by (`contracts/workspace-v21.md` §3); the file writes identities hyphenated, so the 32 matched nothing a person could see in the window or find in the file. A refusal, a member found where another session is, and a problem in the file now each say the eight. Withdrawing the alignment of a session another is aligned through said to "withdraw or re-align its first", three pronouns for two sessions; it now names whose alignment each sentence means. In the investigation's window an action refused, or one its file could not take - a join decided, a session relinked, an alignment withdrawn, a note removed, and each dialog's own - showed only the reason, and left whether anything was done to be guessed; each now says first what it did not do. A file moved or deleted under the window was said in the command line's words, that `icat workspace new` makes one; the window, its dialogs and InterCat's card now say that no file is at its path any more. Sessions that could not be added were followed by a reading of the investigation, whose failure, for the same reason, took the place of what said they were not added; when nothing is added, nothing is read again.

Revision 523 calls an investigation one wherever what it says is read (R5). The window, its timeline's words, a package's disclosure and most of icat's own output said the investigation and the investigation's time; the investigation's own refusals and file problems said this workspace, the workspace's time and not a readable workspace, and icat's help said both a line apart. What a person reads now says investigation, and workspace stays the command's name, the file's extension and the contract's (§22). The main window's "the current workspace is unchanged" meant what it shows, a third sense, and now says that; a channel browser's advice to reopen it names InterCat's window. A refusal from `icat raw`'s second read sent a person to "the current workspace", which the command line has none of; it now says to restart from `icat evidence`, as the first read's does.

Revision 524 runs the record window's and the investigation's untested states (R15, R21) and fixes what they found. A redacted package's record, opened from its row, called itself an original journal record - its title, its heading, "Locating the retained original record…" - until its synthetic entry had been read; its text's accessible name, its status's name and a failure to verify it said original even after, and Reveal offered retained body bytes a package never holds. The main window now tells the window what a package's rows open, so from its first word it says a synthetic record of a redacted package, names its status and text for one, says it could not verify the synthetic record when it cannot, and offers no Reveal; a window not told still turns synthetic once it reads one. A row a package holds no entry for - every row has one, checked as the package was saved - says the package is not as it was saved, never that an original was released. The inspector's record button was named "Open the selected record's original journal entry" whatever its label said; its label is now its name, as WCAG 2.5.3 asks, and that explanation its help text, a package's saying synthetic. A record kept across an interval release whose journal batch the release gave up is now run in the window: the original is unavailable, the reason is the record's text and has the keyboard, and nothing is offered to reveal. An investigation with no session said every session was where it was last found; it now says it holds no session yet, and Package says so when the file it measures names none, and that the investigation could not be measured when its file no longer reads. A Release run failed the call lane's density test once: it pointed at a column, and the lane, counted again as the window settled, held other columns by the time the pointer rested. It and its HTTP twin now wait for the view's own count as well as the lane's, and point again at a lane counted again before reading what the pointer rests on.

Revision 525 has every action of the investigation window that is not offered say why, as revision 432 had the main window's (R15). Eighteen of its buttons were disabled without a word: a session's Open, Relink, Align, Withdraw alignment and One host; a join's Accept, Reject and Withdraw decision; a note's Reword, Remove and Show on the timeline; Compare instants, the three zooms, Open this column, Refresh the timeline and Package. Each now says why on itself - its reason is the disabled button's tooltip, shown as the main window's are, and its help text - and says what it does once offered, where its name does not: add a session or a note first, select one, find candidate joins first, that the session is the investigation's time reference or not aligned, that a decision was made already or none was, that the timeline shows the whole investigation already, that a note about the whole investigation has no place on it, that the investigation is still being read or could not be. Enter or a double click on a session that is not where it was last found did nothing; it now says so and to relink it, as Open's reason does.

Revision 526 calls a session of an investigation a session wherever what it says is read (R5, R18). The window lists an investigation's sessions, yet its refusals and file problems, the reasons a session's row gives for where it stands, and `icat workspace`'s output and help called each a member, the file's word for one: a row said to relink the member where it is now, `icat workspace show` headed a field and its hosts' table Members, and a file naming a session it does not hold was refused as naming no member. Each now says session, by its leading digits - session 0a1b2c3d is not in this investigation; relink the session where it is now; another session's folder is not session 0a1b2c3d - and member stays the contract's word for a session as the file records it (§22).

Revision 527 has every action of the main window that is not offered say why on itself, as revision 525 had the investigation window's (R15). Show source records, Browse paired channels and both Share actions had their reasons as tooltips, which a disabled control does not show unless told to, so each said nothing while it was disabled, and a screen reader heard only what it would do. Go to parent, the children action, Pin node, Re-layout graph and the record action had no reason at all. Each now says why where the pointer and a screen reader find it - its tooltip, shown on the disabled button, and its help text - and what it does once offered: open or record a session first, the capture holds no process that started this one, select a node of the graph first, choose a record first. A window with no session says so on these actions from its first frame, where they said nothing until a session had been shown.

Revision 528 has every action of the record, content and channel windows and of the content capture's chooser that is not offered say why on itself, as revisions 525 and 527 had the investigation and main windows' (R15), through one way of offering an action that these windows and the investigation window now share; the investigation's own dialogs are next. The record window's Reveal, the content window's Copy as hex, Save bytes, Decode and part toggle, the channel browser's Show and Next, and the content capture's Review with the broker were disabled without saying why; some said what they would do, none why they could not. Each now says it on the disabled button and as its help text, from the window's first frame: while the record, its kept bytes or a page of channels are read; when no body byte was retained, or the original record is not retained or could not be verified; when the session keeps no content of the record, no byte of its message was kept or the message held none, the bytes were kept without consent to inspect them, they are not shown yet or they could not be read; when a part is not whole, which is never saved as one; when no channel is in scope or chosen, or no page follows this one; and, for the review, the chooser's own line - choose the processes, or no more than a request names. A channel page that could not be read had left Show saying that no channel was in scope; it now says no page is shown and that the status line says why. A reveal of kept bytes that fails offers itself again, as it did, while Copy and Save say why they cannot. The part toggle's reason - its part's bytes kept without consent, or more than a part is read up to - is not run by a test, which would need a part of more than 64 MiB, and a capture's consent covers all its parts alike.

Revision 529 has every action of the investigation's dialogs and of the pin dialog that is not offered say why on itself, as revision 528 had the record, content and channel windows' (R15), through the same way of offering an action, which now offers a choice or a box as it does a button. Some of these actions said what they would do; none said why it could not: the saved views' Show and Remove with no view saved or chosen, or the one chosen saved on another time reference, and Save the view shown with nothing shown to save; the translations' Withdraw with none stated or chosen, and it and State translation while a change was written; One host's two actions while the word was recorded, or with the hosts one already, or not; the package's Save with no session chosen, and a session that cannot be copied, which said what choosing it would copy; Align, Compare and Add note while they worked; the session to align to when it was the only one; where a note is pinned while it is not to be pinned; and Keep records until a reason is written. Each now says why on the disabled control and as its help text, and what it does once offered where its label does not say it. A dialog opened on an investigation that no longer reads says so on its lists' actions, rather than that the list holds none, and says it holds none once the file reads again.
