The selection and the keyboard's focus were named first and every other name placed after them, so choosing a node, or each step the keyboard took through the graph, could move names across it: revision 588's pass saw OneDrive.exe's name cross the graph when a chrome.exe node was chosen. The names the graph draws with nothing chosen are now placed first, as that frame places them, and the chosen names after: one already drawn stays unless it stands across its node's selection ring, which a selected name stands outside; one not yet drawn takes a free slot, or else the slot it prefers, where the unchosen names in its way step aside - undrawn while it is chosen - rather than move. The focused node, or a single selected one, is still always named, and no two names overlap. With nothing chosen again, every name stands where it stood. Where names go is now a function of its own, `GraphView.PlaceLabels`, which the view measures names for and draws from, so its rules are tested exactly rather than through a rendered frame.

Revision 590 makes two window tests that failed intermittently read what was drawn, or say why they failed. A Release run of revision 585 saw the dense HTTP exchange lane's pixel check sample a colour between the plot and the faint hue it expected. The lane is counted again as the window settles at another width - the test already pointed at a column again for that reason further down - and in a full parallel run that count can land after the test has read its columns, so it sampled where a column no longer stood; it now reads the columns of the count the frame drew, settling until no other count follows. The investigation window's merged-time test failed once in about forty runs and not in thirty-two more; its waits now allow 15 s rather than 5 s, which a machine busy with other test runs needs, and one that times out names the condition it waited for, so a recurrence says where. No product code changed.

Revision 591 shortens a connection ranking's figure to the side it ranks (§6.1). A live pass over the busy machine's tables at the smallest window, 1080 × 700, found the rail's names cut short under a connection ranking - "chrome....", "svchost...." - because each row's figure read "13 connections". It now reads "13 made" or "8 accepted", the side the selector ranks, which is shorter and says more, and the name keeps its room; the row's spoken name and the note beneath the selector still say connections. The same pass found that with a saved session open, its card leaves the ranked list room for two rows at that size; that is still so.

Revision 592 gives the ranked list more of the rail (§6.1, R15), the item revision 591 left open. Measured at the smallest window with a saved session shown, the card takes 267 px, already as tight as revision 398 left it - 9 px of padding, 6 px between its lines - and the list the 164 px left; each row took 75 px, its two lines and 44 px touch target with 5 px margins, and 21 px more of the theme's item padding. A row now takes 62 px, 4 px of padding above and below: the default window, 1456 × 939, shows six rows whole where it showed five, and the smallest two and most of a third where it showed two and a sliver. Three whole rows there would need the card to give up a line it still needs, so that stays as it is.

Revision 593 counts the processes that only reach other hosts by program (§6.3), from a second look at the busy machine revision 588 built. Revision 546 drew the eight busiest of them on their own beside Other hosts and folded the rest, process by process: there the eight places went to ms-teams.exe's four processes, two of svchost.exe's, one of chrome.exe's and ntoskrnl.exe, while chrome.exe's ten other helpers and svchost.exe's seven other services were counted in Other hosts only - half the places spent on one program, and the busiest program hidden. Now the processes of one executable group take one place, ranked by their connections' records together: several are drawn as their group's node, edged to Other hosts once per transport, and one as itself; past the eight busiest places the rest are counted in Other hosts only, as before. One place left over is drawn as itself, since counted in Other hosts only it would save no node and hide its name. A group's members that relate to processes stay drawn on their own, as a kept process stays beside its collapsed group; chosen, the group says how many of its processes the node holds and how many are drawn on their own, as it did, and a node's card now says "no relationship" where it said "0 relationships", as the inspector says it. The same machine now draws chrome.exe's eleven helpers, svchost.exe's nine services and ms-teams.exe's four as three nodes beside the hub, with ntoskrnl.exe, and nothing in Other hosts only. Its fixture now also runs a dozen programs that reach the internet - Outlook, Slack, Spotify, Zoom and others - so its hub keeps eight places and an Other hosts only node, and the window's tests of crowded names keep a crowded graph. When the node budget collapses groups, a group whose node already holds its processes that only reach other hosts folds its remaining members into that node, a node saved for each; the count that chooses how many groups to collapse now credits that, where it would have collapsed a group too many. A property now holds that no two processes of one program are drawn on their own beside the hub.

Revision 594 lists the processes an aggregate counts where it is read (§6.3, §6.7). No relationships, Other hosts only, Other processes and an opened group's Other members fold processes the graph does not draw on their own, and their descriptions sent the reader to the ranked table, which lists every process of the rung with no mark of which were folded where - at the machine rung it ranks executables, so on revision 588's busy machine finding the twenty-two processes No relationships counted meant opening their groups, svchost.exe's thirty instances among them. Chosen, such an aggregate now lists them in the inspector beneath its description, as Other hosts has listed its hosts since revision 558: the busiest over the scope shown first, then by name whatever its case, then by PID, at most a hundred, with a note saying how many more there are and where to read them. Each row names the process and its PID and says its records, its relationships and its connections to other hosts - the whole session's, which a brush does not re-count, as the row then says. Enter or a double click on one opens its rung through its group, as Enter on a process drawn on its own does, and the aggregates' descriptions, the card of Other hosts only's edges and the keys window now say so. A group's node, which Enter opens into its rung's own table, and a focus's context node, which Esc returns from, list none.

Revision 595 makes a process chosen in an aggregate's list its own records (§6.4, §6.7), as a host chosen in Other hosts' list has been since revision 565. Revision 594's list named each process an aggregate counts and opened one with Enter, but choosing one there changed nothing else: the timeline highlighted, the card counted and E listed the whole aggregate's records. Chosen by a click or the arrow keys, a process is now the records the timeline highlights, the card counts as the selected process and E lists, reached from the rung with it chosen among the processes the aggregate counts; a node chosen in the graph, the aggregate again included, lets it go, and a brush restates the list with the process still chosen, its row's records re-counted. Wiring it found a race the host list's tests had not shown: while the list restated its rows the list control let its choice go and read the choice back as still made, so the choice said changed afterwards was no change to it, and the list showed none chosen while the card counted one. Read during a restatement, the choice is now none, as the list then holds none. The tests' sessions also found that records whose native and session-relative times disagree - which no capture writes - are counted by a brush over one instant and listed by E over another; the fixtures keep the two in step.

Revision 596 finds why the merged-time window test failed now and then (R13), which revision 590 had made say what it waited for: Enter on alpha's first column had not opened alpha's session within 15 s. The test zooms in and out with the keys, then chooses that column with Home and opens it with Enter. The merged time reads each zoom's timeline off the window's thread; a zoom asked for while another is read waits for it and is read next, and every reading was drawn in turn, one for a zoom already left included. The test's waits pass on the whole still drawn, so under load the reading of the zoom + asked for could be drawn after 0 had asked for the whole - between Home and Enter. The chart keeps its cursor's column by its place, and the first column of that zoom held none of alpha's records, so Enter opened nothing; a person pressing the keys as quickly would have met the same. A reading for a zoom no longer asked for is now not drawn, and a test asks for a quarter and then the whole before the quarter is read and finds one drawing, the whole's. With three of the machine's four cores kept busy, the test failed 6 of 120 runs without it, each at that wait, and none of 120 with it.

Revision 597 puts revision 596's stress to the other window tests known to fail now and then (R13, R15), with three of the machine's four cores kept busy. The HTTP lane test revision 590 settled failed none of 120 runs. The main window's path tests failed 1 of 40, at a wait of 5 s for a dialog opened from the inspector to become active; with that wait made 15 s, as the investigation window's tests wait, they failed 4 of 60, each where Escape left a dialog open, and once Escape's failure said what the dialog heard, 5 of 60: the dialog was active, but its key handlers heard nothing, the keyboard still on the button in the main window that had opened it. These dialogs place the keyboard once they have read what they show, and until then this headless platform gives a key to whatever had the keyboard, where Windows gives an active window its keys. A person presses Escape in a dialog that has the keyboard, so the tests now wait for that, and none of 120 runs failed; the investigation window's tests wait so too. No product code changed.

Revision 598 names more of a busy graph's nodes where its pane has room (§6.3). The graph drew at most 24 names, the aggregates' first, and the same number decided whether a process's name carried its PID beneath. On revision 588's busy machine, thirty nodes since revision 593, six went unnamed whatever the window's size - Zoom.exe, OUTLOOK.EXE, Spotify.exe, OneDrive.exe, spoolsv.exe and SearchHost.exe - though at 1920 × 1080 there was room for every one: the budget bound, not the room. The placement keeps every name clear of the others and ties one set away from its node by a leader, so a larger budget adds names only where they fit: at most 40 are drawn now. The PID line keeps its own threshold of 24 nodes, since the larger budget had turned it on for thirty and crowded the graph with second lines. The busy machine is named whole at 1920 × 1080, 27 of its 30 nodes at 1456 × 939 where 24 were, and 22 at 1080 × 700, where the room binds first.

Revision 599 lets the keyboard follow an aggregate into its list (§6.3, R15). Since revision 594 No relationships, Other hosts only, Other processes and an opened group's Other members list their processes in the inspector once chosen, but Enter or a double click on one in the graph did nothing - a node standing for many processes opened nothing - so a keyboard on the graph reached the list only by F6. Now Enter or a double click on such an aggregate chooses it, if it was not, and gives its list the keyboard, on the process chosen there or the first, where Enter opens one: graph, list and rung are three keys apart. A process already chosen in the list stays chosen. The node's card says so, as a group's and a process's say what Enter does on them, and a double click on Other hosts only's edge to the other hosts, which opens its source as any such edge does, now says it takes the keyboard there too. A focus's context node still opens nothing; Esc returns from it.

Revision 600 says, in an aggregate's list, which of a process's connections to other hosts it accepted (§6.3). A row said "1 TCP connection to 1 host" whichever end had opened it, so on revision 588's busy machine ntoskrnl.exe, which accepted an SMB connection, read as one more process connecting out. Since revision 574 Other hosts' list says of a host "accepted here at port 3389"; a process's row now says it the same way of its own connections - "accepted here at port 445" where all of its TCP connections were, else how many - beside its connections to other hosts and, under a brush, before saying they are the whole session's. A UDP flow, which nothing opens, is never counted as accepted.

Revision 601 brings the way back from a process opened in an aggregate's list to that list (§3.2, R15). Since revision 594 Enter on a process No relationships, Other hosts only, Other processes or an opened group's Other members lists opens its rung through its group, so the breadcrumb states every rung, but Esc then climbed back as from a descent through the ranked table: to the rung the list was on with the process's group chosen in the table, the aggregate no longer chosen and its list closed - not where the way down began, as §3.2's ascent promises. Now the way back to that rung, by Esc or the breadcrumb, chooses the aggregate again and the process in its list, and gives the keyboard to its row, where E lists its records and Enter opens it again. It is taken once. A descent made another way - by the ranked table, a search or the graph - keeps its own way back, and a drawing that no longer holds the aggregate, or the aggregate the process, chooses nothing. Its Debug run failed P26's window test once: the test read the interval table before the timeline's own count of the view had arrived, and the count, landing with the preview, restated the table in its columns - a change that was not the preview's. The test now waits for that count before reading what it compares, as other window tests do.

Revision 602 is a live pass over revision 588's busy machine, its records spread over time, through the aggregates' lists and the ways back from them (§3.2, §6.3). Revision 601 brought Esc back from a process opened in a list to its row, but two ways back still missed it. E on a process chosen in a list listed its records, and Esc from them left the keyboard on the ranked table's first row: E there is now a way down from the list, as Enter is. Forward from the list's process and back again came back to its group, since the way back was taken once: it is now kept while the ladder's way passes through its rung, forward and back included, and replaced only by a way down that begins at that rung or above it, so the ranked table, a search, the graph, or E on anything else there are ways of their own. The crumb of the rung shown, chosen again, is no way back and chooses nothing. An opened group's Other members, listed since revision 594, comes back the same way at the group's rung, which the pass checked and a test now holds. Other members whose processes have no relationship said so twice - "none with a relationship · no relationship" - and says it once now; all three of its descriptions are tested.

Revision 603 keeps a list's choice through a newer generation (R7, §6.3). A recording publishes a generation every second or two, and each replaces the workspace, which carried the aggregate or Other hosts chosen but not the process or host chosen in its list, nor the way back to the list: through a capture the choice went at every publication, the card and the timeline's highlight fell back to the aggregate's, and Esc from a process opened from the list came back to its group. The window then gave the keyboard to the ranked table, since the row that had it went with the old rows and named no list to give it back to. A newer generation now chooses the process or host there again while its list holds it - saying once, as it says of a process gone, when the list no longer does, the aggregate chosen alone - and keeps the way back; and the window gives the keyboard back to the list a row had it in, on the row still chosen.

Revision 604 keeps a chosen relationship, and processes chosen together, through a newer generation (R7, §6.7). The memento revision 603 extended still let both go: a relationship chosen on the graph or in its table - its edge haloed, the card explaining it, the timeline highlighting its records - went at every publication of a recording, and so did a set built by Ctrl+click or Ctrl+Space. Each is now chosen again, the relationship by its key and the set as far as the generation still holds its processes. A relationship gone is said once, as a process gone is, and so is how many of the set's processes are gone; with none left, nothing is selected. The keyboard returns to the relationship's row in its table, as revision 603 gives a list's row its keyboard back.

Revision 605 is a live pass over revision 588's busy machine recorded in four chunks, a newer generation published with each and choices made between them (§6.3, §6.8). What revisions 603 and 604 keep - a process chosen in No relationships' list, a relationship in its table, a host in Other hosts' list - stayed chosen through every publication, the keyboard on its row. The graph did not hold still: a node the person had seen moved most of the pane's height from one generation to the next, and a quarter of it when seven nodes joined twenty-three. The layout eases a drawn node's anchor as more nodes join, so that opening a group or focusing a process can make room, and a publication adding a few nodes to a small graph was taken for such a restructuring; even a little ease let a node travel several times the nudge its anchor allows, with nothing drawn between, since a publication followed live is drawn at once. A newer generation now asks for its layouts settled - the drawing the person was shown, refreshed - until it has put the view back, and a settled layout keeps every anchor whole however many nodes join: no node the person saw moves more than the nudge, under a tenth of the pane, at a publication. A drawing the person moves to is laid out as a new one, as before.

Revision 606 stressed the whole window suite under load - five runs in Release, three of four cores kept busy - and nothing failed, so revision 601's test reading the interval table early is the only intermittent one found since revision 597. It then fixed how the inspector scrolls to a row the keyboard has (R15). Revision 603 gives a list's row the keyboard back after a newer generation, and the inspector then scrolled three times as far as the row needed - from its foot to near its top, its list's heading out of sight - because the row was brought into view by the list and then by two scroll viewers as it took the keyboard, each reading where the row had been before the first scroll. Arrowed down past the inspector's foot, a list jumped two rows at one step and none at the next. The lists the inspector scrolls now leave a focused row's scrolling to it, and a row given the keyboard is laid out where its scroll put it before it takes it: the inspector moves no further than the row needs, a row at a step. Its first Debug run then failed an export test once: the test looked for the broker's PID, 4120, as any run of those digits in a redacted report, and that run's random identity, written in hex, held them. It, and a like test of a port `icat hosts` must not name, now look for the number as one of its own.

Revision 607 is a live pass over revision 588's busy machine through the search (§6.7, R15). Typed letter by letter, as a person types it, a search left no hit chosen at every second letter. Each letter gives the hits list new rows; the list let go of the row it held and wrote that back as no hit chosen, after the view had chosen the first of the new ones, so Enter in the search box opened nothing and Down reached no hit: "chrom" and Enter opened chrome.exe's group, "chrome" and Enter did nothing. The tests had set a search's words at once, after none was typed. The list's letting go while its hits are restated is no longer taken as the person's, and the list reads no hit chosen until its new hits are in, so it shows the first of them chosen at every letter, as the view has it. The pass also found the graph's summary saying what a search found at the end of its line, after the census: on the busy machine at 1,456 px the line ended in an ellipsis first, "38 with connections to other hosts · 30 found by t…", and a chosen timeline cell's highlight was said there too. What the graph marks for what the person is doing - a search typed, then a cell chosen - now leads the line, the census after it, so the ellipsis cuts what the person read before. The slice's verification found a window test of a process lane's cell failing about one run in eight under load, and as often on the commit before it. A group's lanes are counted as its rung opens, before their names widen the timeline's gutter, and their rows, arriving, made the timeline taller; any change of size asked for the view to be counted again, now at the narrower plot's columns, so a cell chosen before that count arrived was a column the view no longer had, and its explanation and the graph's highlight went while the analysis interval stayed. Revision 462 counts a view in a column per device pixel of its width, and a view is now counted again when its width changes, never for its height alone: a gutter widened for the rows' names leaves the cells a little narrower than a pixel until the view next moves. §19.4's relaxation step now says that a newer generation's layout keeps the anchoring whole, as revision 605 built it.

Revision 608 offers a bare number's port search (§6.7, R15). Revision 607's live pass searched the busy machine for 5353, mDNS's port, which six of its svchost.exe instances sent to, and found nothing: a bare number is a PID or a PID's first digits, and a port is typed after a colon, `:5353`, which the search box's help says and a person holding a port from a firewall's log or netstat does not think to type. A bare number whose port search finds something now offers it among its hits, a row of its own saying how many matches the port has: after a process whose PID the number is, the likelier meaning, else first, since a PID it only begins is the less likely one. Enter on it, from the box or the hits, searches the port in the number's place, the keyboard kept in the box after its words, and Enter again opens the port's first hit. A number that matches nothing else says so, and how to search its port; the keys sheet and the search box's help say that a number offers its port.

Revision 609 rings in the graph the hit chosen among a search's hits (§6.7, R15). Revision 607's live pass arrowed through the busy machine's 31 hits for svchost, and nothing in the graph moved: the search's marks say which nodes hold a found process, as revision 553 built them, but not which holds the hit the keyboard is on, so a person reading "svchost.exe · PID 1010" in the list still had to find which of three marked nodes it was drawn in. As a find marks the match it is on among the others, the nodes the chosen hit's processes are - a process's own, a group's members, a channel's two ends - are now ringed in the ink the search marks nodes in, outside where a selection's ring is drawn: whole on a node every process of which is one of them, broken on one holding them among others, as an aggregate does. A moment or a port the search offers is no process's and rings nothing. The ring follows the hit as the arrows move through the list, and a screen reader hears it in the node's name: the hit chosen in the search, or holding it. Nothing is selected, as revision 553 asks.

Revision 610 is a live pass over revision 588's busy machine through its relationships (§6.3, §6.8, R4). Chosen, a relationship was titled in the inspector by its two ends joined by "↔", whoever connected, and its subtitle was its derivation - the rule, its version and the key of each channel it rests on, eight lines of hexadecimal in the inspector's narrow column where a person looks first - while who connected to whom, which revisions 576 to 579 drew on the graph and wrote in the table, was nowhere in the inspector. A chosen relationship is now titled with the mark its table row carries between its ends - "→" where the first made its connections, "←" where the second did, "⇄" where each did, "↔" where the capture saw none open - and its subtitle says its mechanism, who connected where the capture saw it, and its evidence: "TCP · Code.exe · PID 2400 connected to node.exe · PID 2500 · correlated evidence". How it was derived is the inspector's explanation, under "How it was derived", where a process's binding and a group's grouping are explained, and it goes with the choice. The timeline names the records it highlights for it by the same name. The pass also found that Esc after E on a chosen relationship comes back to its rung with nothing chosen, which is the next slice's.

Revision 611 brings the way back to a chosen relationship (§3.2, R15), which revision 610's pass found missing. E on a chosen relationship lists its records, and Enter or a double click on it or its edge opens its channel, its source process or its linked calls along the ladder from the machine rung; Esc or the breadcrumb then came back with it no longer chosen - with nothing chosen after E, and after Enter with its source's group chosen, the row the ladder had passed through - so a person comparing relationships had to find the one they had left again. Now the way back to the rung that way began at chooses it there again - its edge haloed, the inspector on it, the timeline highlighting its records - and gives the keyboard to its row where the relationship table is shown, as revision 601's way back to an aggregate's list does. Each rung keeps its own way back, so a relationship opened from the machine rung, chosen again at its channel's rung and listed there, is chosen again at both on the way back. A way down that begins at a rung or above it is a way of its own, and the crumb of the rung shown, chosen again, is no way back. A relationship opened at a group's rung is opened along the ladder from its top, through its source's group: where that is the rung it was chosen at, the way back chooses it there, and where the way down replaced that rung with another group's, the way back to it is let go. A newer generation keeps the ways back, as revision 603 keeps the way back to a list (R7). The way back to a list is still one at a time: E on a process chosen in a list further down replaces one begun above it.

Revision 612 is a live pass over revision 588's busy machine through a group's rung and a process's (§6.1, §6.3). At svchost.exe's rung its thirty instances were thirty rows reading "svchost.exe", the PID that tells them apart cut from the rail after "created during", and nothing on a row said what an instance did, so finding the one that resolves names or accepts a remote desktop meant choosing each in turn. Where every process of a group is one program's, each row is now named by its PID, and its second line says first what the instance did - how many relationships it has, and where its connections to other hosts ran, naming the host where it reached one: "← 192.168.1.30 at port 3389", "↔ 192.168.1.1:53", "→ 20.42.65.92:443" - then how it was seen to start. A screen reader hears its own name, and its tooltip, crumb and export keep it; a group of several programs, or of one process, names its rows as ever. The graph's names stood against each other where nodes stood close - "SearchHost.exespoolsv.exe" at the machine rung, "chrome.exechrome.exe" at chrome.exe's - and read as one word: a name now keeps 6 px across and 2 px down from the others where a slot leaves that room, and stands nearer one only where none does, since a name near another is better than none; a chosen name with no such slot takes the one it prefers, and an unchosen name it would stand against steps aside. Edges ran through names, "PID 2010" reading as struck through: each name is drawn over a halo of the pane's colour a pixel round its letters, so an edge passing beneath breaks round them and still meets its node either side. The halo showed a name standing across the ring revision 609 draws round the search's chosen hit, outside the selection's ring that names kept clear of: the hit's name now stands outside its ring.

Revision 613 lists the graph's edges to other hosts in the relationship table (R15), which revision 612's pass found missing. Revision 198 made the table the graph's table equivalent, listing every relationship the rung's graph draws; since revision 542 the graph also draws each process's connections to other hosts as edges to Other hosts, and the table listed none of them - at chrome.exe's rung on the busy machine, twelve of the thirteen edges drawn. Each such edge is now a row after the relationships, the busiest first: its source as the graph names it; the host it reached where it reached one, by its address and the port it ran to - "browser.exe · PID 300 → 142.250.1.1:443" - else Other hosts and how many, "chrome.exe → Other hosts · 8 hosts"; who opened its connections, as its arrowheads say; its transport, records and evidence; and, in the place of a rule, which derives none, how many connections it holds and the hosts it names, who is at them not being observed (P7). Its bytes are not counted, as its edge carries no traffic value. Chosen, a row chooses what a click on its edge does, its source, and stays chosen while that is the selection, a choice made anywhere else or another rung letting it go; Enter or a double click opens what a double click on the edge opens - its process's rung, its group at the machine rung, or an aggregate's processes in the inspector, which then has the keyboard - and the row says which, aloud and beneath the table, or that it opens nothing where it opens nothing. E lists its source's records, as with the node chosen. The table's caption counts its edges to other hosts, and it says why it lists nothing only where it lists no row. The same pass found a group saying "1 drawn on their own" of its one process drawn on its own, which now reads "1 drawn on its own".

Revision 614 is a live pass over revision 613's relationship table on revision 588's busy machine (R15, §3.2). An edge to other hosts chosen in the table came back chosen at the rung E or Enter led to, choosing its process there again: navigation let the choice go without saying so, and the table, whose rows the next rung restated with the same edge listed, chose it again as its rows changed. The table now hears that the choice went before its rows are another rung's. The way back from what such a row opened or listed then chose nothing, where revision 611's chooses a relationship again: Esc or the breadcrumb back to the rung it was chosen at now chooses it again with its source and gives its row the keyboard, an aggregate's edge staying chosen with the process chosen in its list, and a newer generation keeps it chosen while its source is the selection. Each rung's table opened at the scroll offset of the rung left, part-way down with its first row cut in half - revision 111 had fixed this for the ranked table alone - and now starts at its first row, or brings its chosen one into view. The relationships were listed in the order of their keys, the busy machine's busiest pair tenth, while the edges to other hosts after them came busiest first: they are now the most observed first too, then by key.

Revision 615 says what each of the graph's edges to other hosts carried in the relationship table (R15, R21). Revision 613 listed them with their bytes "not counted", as the graph draws them with no traffic value, yet their connections' records measured what they carried, as a host's card says it. Once the table lists them in a session whose bytes are read, each row says what crossed its connections, each transfer counted once at this machine's end - its sends, and the other hosts' as its receives - in a relationship's words: "20 KB sent across", or "no size measured" where only transfers of no stated size did, never a zero; its explanation says what it sent and what it received. One pass over the segments the connections' lifetimes meet reads every reach drawn - one process's connections over one transport to one address - and each is kept for the generation, so a rung whose edges reach no host unread reads nothing again; until they are in, the rows say their bytes are being read, or that they could not be. Under a brush their counts and bytes stay the whole session's, as their edges' do, and the rows now say so. Rows restated in other words as their bytes arrive no longer cost the table its chosen row: its list let the choice go for a moment, as revision 613 found it did not for rows restated in the same words, and the choice is now kept by key.

Revision 616 makes an edge to other hosts chosen in the relationship table stand for its own records (§6.4, I5, R15). Revision 613 let such a row choose its source, as a click on its edge does, so E listed the source's every record - a browser group's relationships, its other hosts and its starts beside the connections the row named - and the timeline highlighted them. A host's scope now names several addresses and, for an edge, the processes whose connections ran to them - "host:142.250.1.1,20.42.65.1@processes:…" - which the evidence scope reads as those processes' records of their connections to those hosts. E from the row adds its transport as a filter of its own, which the breadcrumb shows and can remove, so it lists exactly the connections the row counts; the timeline highlights the same records and the inspector's card counts them, as "Selected edge to other hosts". The pass found a fault older than the table: an evidence rung compared scopes without their narrowing or operation, so removing a lane's mechanism, direction or end filter, or a call's filter on an RPC channel's records, left the narrower list on screen. It now compares them whole, and the wider list is read.

Revision 617 keeps a live session's bytes on screen from one publication to the next (§6.4, R15, R21). Each publication is a new workspace, which reads again the bytes its descriptions state, so the relationship table's rows - relationships and edges to other hosts alike - the inspector's card for a selection, a chosen row, a relationship or a host, a channel's total and a graph mark's card all said "reading bytes…" for the length of a read, about every two seconds on a busy machine. A ranking's measures already stood in from the publication before; now so do the bytes a description read, what the table's edges to other hosts carried and a chosen host's count of its connections, each for the same scope only, until this publication has read its own. The table's caption says "bytes updating" meanwhile, the card, a channel's total and a mark's card say "updating" beside what they state, and a screen reader hears it on each row. A read that fails lets no stand-in remain, and says the bytes could not be read; a publication that follows one which had not read its own yet carries the one before it along. The pass found a newer generation losing a channel or call chosen among a process's rows: restoring the process the rung was reached with made the process the selection again, so on a live session the card, E's scope and the timeline's highlight went back from the channel to its process at every publication. The navigation a generation carries now keeps whether the row was described, and the restore describes it again.

Revision 618 lets a stand-in go once this publication's own read of it has failed (§6.4, R21). A ranking's measures from the publication before stood in, marked updating, until this one's were read - and when that read failed they stayed: the rows ranked by them and the note said "updating" for as long as the workspace was shown, which on a capture's last publication was for good, and an export waited for measures no read would bring and then refused. The failed read now answers in their place, as a failed count of an interval already did: the rows rank by records again and the note says why the metric is unavailable, an export names that ranking, and nothing this publication could not read is carried to the next. An interval's measures go with the counts read beside them. The bytes a description read, the relationship table's edge traffic and a chosen host's count follow the same rule.

Revision 619 reads a publication's bytes once (R11, §12). With a byte ranking and the relationship table or a selection shown, each live publication read the whole session's bytes twice - once for the ranking and once for what the table and the card state - since its restored navigation showed the tables before it put the ranking back, and a description read its own bytes while the ranking read the same. It also showed the tables at the machine rung before re-entering the rung the view was on, so the machine rung's edge traffic and interval bytes were read and cancelled at every publication. A description now states the bytes a byte ranking reads, through that read; a byte ranking chosen once a description has read the bytes ranks the rows at once; a description whose read went with a ranking changed meanwhile reads for itself; and a restored view shows its tables only once its rung and ranking are back. A live session ranked by bytes reads them once per publication, and its edges' traffic only at the rung it shows.

Revision 620 keeps a live session's interval table bytes on screen from one publication to the next (§6.4, R15). The table reads each listed interval's bytes once it is shown, and every publication read them again, so its rows said "reading bytes…" for the length of each read. The timeline's columns keep their places as a live extent grows - multiples of one width from the 1-2-5 ladder - so the intervals a publication lists are mostly the last one's. The bytes the last publication read for the same records over exactly an interval now stand in, marked updating on screen and aloud, while this one reads its own; an interval the last one did not list, or a lane that lists other records, is read afresh. A read that fails lets none stand in, a hidden table states none, and a publication that follows one which had not read its own yet carries the one before it along.

Revision 621 keeps Other hosts' list in its byte ranking from one live publication to the next (§6.3, §6.4). Under a byte ranking the list ranks its hosts by what their connections carried, read for each publication, and until that read was in it ranked them by records - so on a live session its rows moved to the busiest by records and back every couple of seconds, under a person reading or choosing among them. What the last publication read now ranks the list while this one reads its own, for the same byte ranking only, and the heading says "updating", as each row does aloud; a host new since then follows the hosts it holds. Another byte ranking chosen meanwhile is read afresh, a read that fails lets the last one's go and says why, and a publication that follows one which had not read its own yet carries the one before it along.

Revision 622 keeps a process's rung whole from one live publication to the next (§6.4, R7). Its one-sided connections, RPC channels and HTTP exchanges are read for the rung once it is shown, and a newer publication started with none: at every publication the rows to other hosts - on a busy machine most of a process's rows - vanished until they were read, and the row the person had chosen among them could not be chosen again, so the inspector went back to the process. The rows the last publication read for the same process over the same scope are now listed while this one reads its own, and a row the restored view chose that its rung has not listed yet is chosen once it is, described as it was, or its process where that was chosen again over it; anything chosen meanwhile, or another rung, lets it go. A call chosen on an RPC channel's rung is chosen again the same way once its rung has read its calls.

Revision 623 fixes what a live pass over a recording's newer generations in the window found (§6.1, R21). At a process's rung a byte ranking ranks its connections to other hosts together with its channels, but the note beneath the selector summed the channels alone: chrome.exe's rung listed a 1.4 KB connection above an 800 B channel under "800 B on 2 sends", and a process whose only rows are connections to other hosts - most of a busy machine's processes - said "No sends in scope" beside a row of 200 B. The note and its detail now count every row the rung ranks, and are restated once its connections are read. "Bytes sent and received" ranked no connection, so a rung that listed one fell back to its records, still showing its channels' byte figures; a connection now ranks by its sends and receives together, as a channel ranks by its process's own records on it. A count of one says "1 send" rather than "1 sends", and rows with no measured transfer say they hold none, or that their transfers recorded no size, rather than "0 B on 0 measured sends", a zero no record measured. The pass also found that the rung's export holds its channels alone, leaving out the connections, RPC channels and HTTP exchanges it lists; revision 624 takes that up.

Revision 624 has a process's rung export every row it lists (§6.4, R18). The rung lists its paired channels, its connections whose other end no record holds - to other hosts, on a busy machine most of its rows - its RPC channels and its HTTP exchanges, ranked together, but its export held the paired channels alone: a person exporting chrome.exe's rung kept none of the hosts it reached, and a process whose rows are all connections to other hosts said "This rung lists no row, so there is nothing to export." beside them. The export now holds every row in the order the rung lists it, under the ranking they rank by, a connection's known bytes being what its transfers measured. One builder, `ProcessRungRows`, makes the rows for the window's list and for both exports, and `icat export` at the same rung reads the same connections, RPC channels and HTTP exchanges over the same scope, so the two files stay the same byte for byte; their words do not follow the reader's culture, as the ladder's own rows' do not, while the rung shows them in it. An export started while the rung still reads its rows waits for them, and one whose rows could not be read is refused with the reason, which the export button's tip states too, rather than written without them. A byte-ranked export's caveat names the connections and the RPC and HTTP rows, which carry no transport bytes. An RPC channel's and an HTTP exchanges' rung still export nothing while listing calls or exchanges; revision 625 takes that up.

Revision 625 has an RPC channel's rung and a process's HTTP exchanges' rung export what they list (§6.4, R18). Each lists its calls or exchanges a page at a time, yet its export said "This rung lists no row, so there is nothing to export." beside them, and `icat export` could not reach either rung: `--at` named only the rows the ladder projects. Both now export every call or exchange of the scope, whatever the list has paged in, read in one pass of one generation up to the limit an evidence export keeps. Each row says when it began in session time and how it went - a call's duration or state, its procedure, status and other end; an exchange's duration or that its response's end was not recorded, and the bytes recorded of each part - in words that do not follow the reader's culture, built once, in `ChannelRungRows`, for both exports. `icat export --at` reaches either rung from its process's by its key and writes the same file, and `--limit`, which bounded only an evidence export's records, bounds the calls or exchanges too; an export the limit cut short says so, and `icat export` reports it as a partial result. A rung that has listed none yet, holds none in its scope, or could not read them says which instead of offering an export, and an export there refuses in the same words.

Revision 626 keeps a channel's rung whole from one live publication to the next (§6.4, R7), as revision 622 keeps a process's. An RPC channel's rung lists its calls, and a process's HTTP exchanges' rung its exchanges, a page at a time, and each newer publication started with none: the list went empty every couple of seconds until its first page was read, and the call chosen among them came back only then. The calls or exchanges the last publication listed for the same channel over the same scope - every page it had read - are now listed while this one reads its own first page, which replaces them, and the one chosen is chosen again at once. A publication that has not yet read its own hands the same rows on to the next; rows listed for another scope while that scope's are read are never handed on as its own; and a read that fails lets them go and says why.

Revision 627 is a live pass over exporting from the window on the busy machine (§6.4, §6.1, R15). The machine, a group's, a process's and a connection's evidence rung each exported what they listed, under the ranking shown, and the card's line said how many rows or records went where and whether the file is complete. The pass found the Rank by box itself unable to say which ranking it applies: beside its label it had 69 pixels for a metric's name, so "Bytes sent and received" read "Bytes sent …", "Connections made" and "Connections accepted" both "Connectio…", and "RPC calls made" and "RPC calls served" both "RPC calls …". "Rank by", its basis at the line's end, now names the box from above, and the box takes the rail's width beside the per-second toggle, so at the smallest window every metric's name shows whole; Group by stands the same way beneath it, the two boxes still in line.

Revision 628 has the way back from Enter on a process an aggregate lists choose the aggregate's edge to other hosts again (§3.2, R15). With that edge chosen in the relationship table and a process chosen in the aggregate's list, E on the process kept the way back to both, but Enter on it - which opens its rung through its group's - kept only the way back to the list: Esc back up to the machine chose the aggregate and the process again and left the table's row unchosen. Enter now keeps the way back to the edge's row as E does, so the climb back chooses all three and gives the keyboard to the row.

Revision 629 keeps a clicked timeline cell the cell when the timeline cuts its columns afresh (§6.8, §6.4). A cell chosen in a zoomed view is one of that view's own columns, and a wider or narrower window, or another zoom, counts the view in another number of columns, none of which need be the cell's interval. The analysis interval stayed the cell's, but the inspector stopped explaining it, titled it a time range rather than a cell, listed none of its records, and E listed every record of the interval rather than its lane's. The cell is now kept as its lane drew it when it was chosen, until another interval, lane or rung is chosen: it stays explained, its column described as one of those it was chosen in, which the timeline no longer draws, it is titled and spoken as a cell, its records are listed, and E narrows to its lane. Once the timeline draws its column again it is explained as one of them. A newer publication keeps no such cell, since its own count may differ: there a cell is explained only where its columns draw it, as before.

Revision 630 has the way back to a clicked timeline cell choose it again (§3.2, §6.8). E from a chosen cell lists its lane's records, a record listed beside it opens in E, and Enter opens a row over its interval; Esc or the breadcrumb back brought the interval back but not the cell, so the inspector explained the interval as a cell of the selected lane or the machine row instead - at the machine rung, which draws no machine row, as no cell at all, titled a time range - and E from it no longer narrowed to the cell's lane. Each rung left with a cell chosen now keeps the way back to it, as the way back to a relationship does: Esc, the breadcrumb, or forward to a rung an ascent left, chooses the cell again there, explained in the lane it was chosen in, its records listed, and E narrowed to that lane; the crumb of the rung shown keeps it chosen. A way back is taken when it is used, so a cell let go at a rung since is not chosen again later, and a rung entered again by hand rather than forward comes to none; a way down that begins at a rung or above it is a way of its own, and a newer generation keeps them. Building it found that revision 629's kept cell stood in while its lane was not drawn at all - just back at a rung whose lanes were not yet counted again, or after a count failed - where E from a channel end's cell then failed: a kept cell now stands in only while its lane is drawn, in other columns.

Revision 631 has the way back from a record opened from a cell's list give the keyboard back to that record (§3.2, §6.4, R15). Enter or a double click on a record the inspector lists beside a cell opens it in E, selected there with E's table holding the keyboard; Esc came back to the cell with the keyboard on the ranked table, so the next record was F6 and an arrow away rather than one key. The way down from the list is now remembered as the way down from an aggregate's list is: the way back, by Esc or the breadcrumb, gives the keyboard to that record once the cell's records are listed again, chosen there, so an arrow takes the next and Enter opens it - whether the cell was clicked or stepped to. The first listing decides, so a record no longer listed is not looked for later; another interval chosen or a navigation before then lets it go, as does a way down of its own from that rung or above it, and a newer generation keeps it. Revision 472's keyboard walk now comes back to the record it opened.

Revision 632 is a live pass over timeline cells, their columns and the ways back on the busy machine (§6.8, §3.2, §6.4). The window counts the timeline a column per device pixel, so any wider or narrower window cuts its columns afresh, and there revision 629's kept cell held: still titled a cell, explained and listing its records. Three things were fixed. Back from E taken from a cell of a group's process lane, the way back chose the row of that lane's process - the rung E had listed - so the inspector described the process rather than the cell and listed none of its records; E from a cell now begins at no row, and the way back chooses the cell alone, while a process chosen before the cell is still chosen again. A kept cell's column read "so the group's 30 lanes stay within 20,000 cells when it was chosen, a column the timeline no longer draws since it cut its columns afresh", the clause tangling with the column's own words; it now reads "here, as the cell was chosen, one of the lanes' own 666, ...", and a sentence of its own says that the timeline has since cut its columns afresh and no longer draws that one. And at E with nothing selected the inspector's evidence line said "Selected process" and "No evidence selected" beneath the record's own description and scope; the line now shows only when it says something, as in the tour.

Revision 633 keeps a cell a step or the interval table chose as revision 629 keeps a clicked one (§6.8). A step with `[` or `]`, or a row the interval table lists, makes a column the analysis interval, explained as a cell of the lane it follows; a wider or narrower window, or another zoom, turned it into a time range, its explanation and its records gone, as a click's had been before revision 629. Each rung now keeps the cell the analysis interval is in the lane it is explained in, as that lane drew it last - kept when a click chooses it and before the timeline is counted afresh at the rung - so however it was chosen it stays the cell, explained in the columns it was chosen in, while that lane is the one explained and that interval the one chosen. A rung keeps its own: the recount a navigation asks for keeps none, since the rung come to has drawn nothing yet, and a way down of its own lets the cells of the rungs below go, while the rung left keeps its own, which the way back comes to.

Revision 634 is a live pass over stepping, the interval table and resizing by keyboard on the busy machine (§3.2, §6.4, R15). Enter on a row of the interval table, or E with the keyboard there, lists that moment's records in E, E's table holding the keyboard; Esc came back with the keyboard on the ranked table, so the next moment was F6 twice and an arrow away rather than one key. The way down from the row is now remembered as the way down from a relationship's row is: the way back - by Esc, the breadcrumb, or forward to E and back - gives the keyboard to the row again where the tables are shown and it is still the analysis interval's row, at once or once the rung's rows are counted again and list it. E from elsewhere is no way down from the row and asks for nothing; another interval chosen, the tables hidden or a navigation before the row is listed lets that ask go; a way down of its own from that rung or above it, or a way back to another rung at its depth, lets the way back go; and a newer generation keeps it. Revision 472's keyboard walk and the interval table's own Enter walk now come back to the row.

Revision 635 has forward along a way down and back again come where the first way came (§3.2, §6.7, R15). The way back from a record opened from a cell's list gave the keyboard to it once and was spent, so Alt+Right to E and Esc back left the keyboard on the ranked table, while the ways back to a relationship's row, an aggregate's process and, since revision 634, an interval-table row hold until a way down of their own; it now holds as theirs do, let go only where the way back comes to another rung at its depth. And E entered by Alt+Right chose no record, though forward re-enters a rung as it was left (§6.7): a rung now records, beside the interval it was left with, the record chosen on it as an ascent or the breadcrumb leaves it - or the one it was still to choose, its records not yet listed - and forward to it chooses that record again once they are. A record E was still to choose is that E's alone: any other rung shown lets it go, where a record opened and left before E listed it was chosen by the next E to list it, E from the cell itself among them. Revision 472's keyboard walk now goes forward to the record and back.

Revision 636 is a live pass over the busy machine at the smallest window, 1080 × 700, in the light theme and both high-contrast ones (§6.3, §26.3). The themes held at that size - text legible on every surface, the keyboard's focus and the selection plain in each - as did the tables and E; what it found is in the graph, whose pane there leaves too little room for every name to keep its distance. Revision 612 keeps 6 px across and 2 px down between names where a slot leaves that room, and lets a name stand nearer another where none does, since a name near another is better than none; but nearer meant only clear of it, so at the machine rung two names stood edge to edge and read as one word - "svchost.exems-teams.exe" in the light theme, "svchost.exeOneDrive.exe" in high contrast - as revision 612's "SearchHost.exespoolsv.exe" had. A name standing nearer another now still keeps a word's space across and a pixel down from it, as does a name that must be shown - the focused or the one selected - where a slot leaves that; one that may go unnamed and has no such slot goes unnamed rather than stand against another, as revision 612's own pane too narrow for two names side by side now leaves the second unnamed. At the smallest window the machine rung, svchost.exe's group and its busiest instance now draw no name within a word's space of another, though some still stand nearer each other than names keep where there is room.

Revision 637 is a live pass over the keys sheet, the panes filling the column and the inspector at the smallest window on the busy machine (§6.7, §6.8, R15). The sheet read whole and closed back to where the keyboard was, and the graph and the timeline filled the column and gave it back as F11 and their buttons ask; what it found is in the lists and the inspector. Page Down and Page Up in a list only scrolled its rows: the keyboard stayed on a row out of sight, which the next arrow scrolled back to, and a screen reader heard nothing - in the ranked table, which shows two rows at the smallest window, at every press. Every list the application shows now takes them as a list on Windows does: to the last or first row it shows whole, or from that row a page further, as many rows on as it shows less the one kept in sight, chosen as Down and Up choose theirs, in sight and with the keyboard; at an end they stay, and with a modifier, which a window may take for its own - the investigation window's Ctrl+Page Down - they choose nothing. The keys sheet says so of the ranked table and the interval table. And the inspector said svchost.exe's group with its path on a line of its own, which at that width wrapped with "C:" alone on a line: Unicode's line breaking finds no break in a Windows path but the one after its drive's colon. The path now breaks after a backslash a name follows, by a zero-width space neither seen nor said, its drive's colon joined to its backslash, and reads as it is.

Revision 638 has every rung come back as it was left, its row included, by Esc and by forward alike (§3.2, §6.7). Revision 635 had forward to E choose the record chosen there; forward to any other rung chose no row, though forward re-enters a rung as it was left - a group's process chosen there and left by Esc, Alt+Right came back to the group with nothing chosen. A rung now records the row it is left with: the row a way down goes through - so a rung a breadcrumb passes by comes back with it, as the group and the process a relationship opened from the machine runs the ladder through do - or the row chosen as E, Esc or the breadcrumb leaves it, or the one it was still to choose. Forward chooses it again, at once where the rung lists it, or, where the rung reads its rows after it is shown, once it does, described in the inspector as it was. Building it found the way back by Esc losing its place on such a rung: from E taken from a row a process rung reads later - a connection to another host - Esc came back to no row, the rows arriving after the way back looked for it. The way back now waits for the row as forward does, unless another is chosen first.

Revision 639 has the breadcrumb up land where Esc does (§3.2, §6.7). Esc back to a rung chooses the row the way down from it went through, so the keyboard takes the next from there; the breadcrumb up to the same rung chose none, and at the machine rung left the selection the deeper rungs had made - a process the machine's table does not list - in the inspector. Every rung recording the row it is left with since revision 638, a crumb up now chooses that row, once the rung lists it where it reads its rows after it is shown, as Esc does - unless an aggregate's list or a relationship a way down began at is chosen again there, which the crumb tries first and which tells the way more exactly, as revision 628's way back to an edge to other hosts does with its source. A view captured after such a crumb holds the group the way went through rather than the deeper process, so a newer generation without that process has nothing to say of it.

Revision 640 is a live pass over the investigation window at its smallest, 680 × 650, with three sessions, two of them aligned (§8.2, §8.4, R15). Its four pages held - the sessions, with what each one's clock is to the investigation; the candidate joins and the notes, with what finding or writing one does; the timeline, with its lanes, its scale and its column read out - and what it found is in the words for time. A lane said its records ran "from 0.0001 s to 0.000103 s of the investigation's time", each end trimmed to the decimals it needs, so the start read coarser than the end, and so did icat workspace timeline's interval, "0.0001 s to 0.0005031 s". A range now states both ends to one precision, the finer end's and at least milliseconds - "from 0.000100 s to 0.000103 s" - in a lane's sentence, the timeline's interval and the confirmation of a view saved over one; an instant on its own keeps its own.

Revision 641 is a live pass over a wide machine: 253 processes of 80 programs, 298 loopback relationships among 225 of them, connections to 200 other hosts and 100 DNS datagrams (§6.3, §19.4). The session opened in about 0.6 s and each rung in under 0.7 s; what the pass found was in the graph. The machine rung drew its 199 nodes in the left half of the pane with 846 pairs of discs overlapping, by up to 23 px, some hidden under others where the pointer could not reach them. An opened group's 61 nodes overlapped in 23 pairs, and at a display budget of 60 the machine was a ball a third of the pane wide with 171. In a dense component the relationships outpull relaxation's push between discs. §19.4's layout now has a fourth stage, separation: where two discs are too near, the drawing widens about its middle as far as its discs need room, then each pair still too near is pushed apart, a node drawn before giving way a tenth as readily as a new one. The opened group's discs now all stand clear across the pane, as the machine's do at 60 nodes, and up to 100 none overlap. At 199, 212 pairs still do: more discs than the design pane holds at §6.3's radii, which no layout can part. The pass also found a lone component seeded in the left half of the pane, where relaxation's weak pull across never brought it back to the middle; revision 642 seeds it in the middle.

Revision 642 draws a lone component about the pane's middle, revision 641's other finding (§19.4). The seeding grid has as many columns as the region's shape asks for its components, so a lone component, on a region about three times as wide as it is tall, had two and started in the middle of the first, a quarter of the way across; grown from its best-connected node at angles its hashes fix, it leaned further whichever way they fell, and relaxation's pull toward the middle, weak across the width so components pack side by side, never brought it back. Dense drawings of 20 to 60 nodes sat with their middles 0.26 to 0.38 of the way across, the right of the pane empty. A short row of the grid is now centred, so a lone component starts in the middle, and a component, once grown, is set back across on its cell's middle. The same drawings now sit within 0.02 of the middle. The busy machine is named whole at 1456 × 939, 30 nodes where 27 were, one name a pixel below another where no slot left it more room, and 19 at 1080 × 700 where 20 were. Two window tests followed: names staying put when a node is chosen are checked at the smallest window, where processes still go unnamed, a name stepping aside where it would crowd the chosen one's, as revision 636's rule has it; names keeping their room are checked at 1920 × 1080, where every name has a slot that leaves it, and a word's space at 1456 × 939. At 1920 × 1080 the pass also found, with either layout, an edge's ink showing through a name's halo beside a letter: drawn from the letters' own anti-aliased edges, the one-pixel halo covers their corners only in part.

Revision 643 lowers §6.3's display budget from 200 nodes to 100, what the design pane holds with no disc hiding another (§6.3, §19.4). Revision 641's separation parts what the pane has room for, and measured how much that is: on wide machines of 80 programs and about 250 processes related at random, the densest kind, at the design pane, 200 nodes left 188 to 235 pairs of discs overlapping by up to 25 px, a disc's centre under another's where the pointer aims at it, and 120 left 42 to 47 by up to 20 px; at 100 a few touched by at most 5 px, less than the smallest disc's radius, so none hid another. At 80 discs all but stand apart, and at 60 each keeps its clearance. A busy machine past the budget folds more of its groups, lowest activity first, each still counted and opened by Enter, and the ranked table and the search still list every process. The budget is TUNABLE: a larger window has room for more, but the projection is fixed for the drawing, not the window, so membership never changes on a resize. The default window's pane is about a third larger than the design pane.

Revision 644 lays a name's halo twice at its letters' corners, revision 642's last finding (§6.3). The halo is the name drawn in the pane's colour a pixel every way beneath its letters, so an edge passing beneath breaks round them. At a letter's corner the pixel diagonal to it has one copy alone over it, which covers it only as far as the letter's anti-aliased edge reaches: on the busy machine at 1920 × 1080 an edge two pixels above "chrome.exe" showed through there by nearly half, a blue fleck at the corner of an e. The four diagonal copies are now laid twice, twelve in all, and the fleck shows a fifth of the edge's ink, darker than any; a repaint still allocates nothing of its own. The window test of names and the edges beneath them checks the letters at 1920 × 1080 as well as at 1456 × 939.

Revision 645 is a live pass over a heavy machine shaped as one is (§6.3): 255 processes - 80 svchost.exe, six of them serving forty of the others, lsass.exe serving the services, a browser's 35 processes and another's 20 round their main process, which reaches forty hosts, a chat client, an editor's eight processes and ten node.exe servers, a database with its workers and the apps that use it, a container engine, and 84 processes related to nothing. It opened in 0.16 s, and its machine rung drew in 0.36 s as 72 nodes with no disc over another: svchost.exe's 67 and chrome.exe's 35 processes each folded into a node, msedge.exe's star, the editor and its servers, and the database and its users each in a region of its own. What the pass found was a name: at the smallest window Other hosts went unnamed, on this machine and on the busy one, though its second line - from how many processes - is a count the drawing discloses. Names were placed in the keyboard's order within their rank, and the keyboard reaches Other hosts after every aggregate, so the aggregates round it took each slot first. Other hosts is now named first, then the aggregates, then the busiest processes: at 1080 × 700 it is named on both machines and every aggregate still is, the heavy machine drawing 20 names where it drew 22; at larger windows nothing changes.

Revision 646 names an opened group's own processes by their PIDs, the heavy machine's other finding (§6.3). Opened, chrome.exe drew its 35 processes round its main one, and every name it placed read "chrome.exe": the rung's title already, telling the processes apart not at all, where its table names each "PID 3012" and its lanes "chrome.exe · PID 3012". An opened group's own processes, at its rung and at a process's below it, are now named by their PIDs, a reused PID's holder numbered as everywhere else, with no second line repeating it; its peers keep their programs' names, and the halo is laid round the name drawn. A repaint still allocates nothing of its own: each node's name is formatted once per drawing.

Revision 647 names each program a graph draws before it names one twice (§6.3). The heavy machine's names, after Other hosts' and the aggregates', went to its busiest processes whatever they repeated: at the default window Code.exe and node.exe were named eight times each, while lsass.exe, services.exe, spoolsv.exe, OneDrive.exe and four more programs went unnamed, though a person reads the names to learn which programs are there. A process whose name an aggregate or a busier process bears already - in the keyboard's order, the busiest first - is now named after every process that is the first to bear its own: at 1440 × 900 the heavy machine names 16 of the 19 programs it draws as processes, where it named 11, the three left having no slot free; at 1080 × 700 it names 9 where it named 7, and the busy machine as many as before. A process drawn beside its group's node, as one of the busy machine's chrome.exe processes is beside the node its group's eleven others are drawn as, repeats the group's name. The heavy machine is a fixture of the window's tests since.

Revision 648 has the command line name who reached each other host, found on a live pass with `icat` over the heavy machine's session (§6.3, R18). `icat hosts` counted the processes that reached each of its 97 hosts - "1 TCP connection from 1 process" - and named none, so learning which process ran to 52.81.4.10 took `--address` for that host, and for each of the others; `icat overview`'s busiest hosts the same. Each host listed now names the process whose connections there hold the most records, and how many more reached it, as an RPC channel's row names who served it - "msedge.exe · PID 3400 and 4 more" - in a Processes column beside its connections, which no longer count the processes; one that reached it over both transports is one process, its records summed. The JSON lists every one, the busiest first, with its records (`other-hosts-v7`), and the overview's note points to `icat hosts` for every host and who reached it, where it pointed to a command taking an instance id that no text view prints. The pass also found `icat hosts --rank-by` misstating a host a process accepted a connection from. A host read for its bytes was counted from its connections without how each opened, so the accepted connection's port at the host, which the host chose, was listed among its Ports, and its Accepted here count, column and JSON fell to none, where the records ranking has said them since revision 573. A host's traffic is now counted as the census counts it, its connections made and accepted and their ports, whatever the ranking or interval; the window takes its words from the census's host and was not affected.

Revision 649 has `icat processes --pid` list the other hosts an instance's connections ran to, the same pass's other finding (§6.3, §7.1, R18). Its Exchanged with table names each process at the other end and folds the rest into one row - on the heavy machine, chrome.exe's 673,817 B sent to "unresolved: no record in this capture holds the other end" - though the census knows, address by address, the 41 hosts that row stands for, and the window's process rung lists each connection. Beneath it the instance's other hosts are now listed as `icat hosts` lists every process's, over its own connections alone: each address with its connections, the ports they ran to, those accepted at its own ports, its records and what its transfers there measured each way, the most bytes first. Twenty are listed, then how many more and the command that lists each connection, by the instance id the detail prints; the JSON gives every one as `otherHosts`. `icat hosts --address`, which pointed to that command and an instance id no text view printed, now points here by PID.

Revision 650 names each paired channel by the processes at its two ends and which connected to which, the pass's third finding (§7.1, R5, R18). `icat channels` printed a channel's 150-character key and then its endpoints alone - "127.0.0.1:9223 ↔ 127.0.0.1:50113" - so who was at either end took a port looked up in `icat processes`, for each of the heavy machine's 161 channels; the window's channel list named both processes but joined them by "↔" whichever made the connection, though the overview and the relationship table have said who connected since revisions 579 and 581. Both now name a channel alike (`ChannelNames.Ends`): the process that made it first, with an arrow at the one it connected to, its transport, and its endpoints in the same order, compacted - "msedge.exe · PID 3448 → msedge.exe · PID 3400 · TCP · :50113 → :9223 on 127.0.0.1" - or the two joined by "and" where the capture saw neither open it, as for a UDP flow. A page of channels names the processes at its channels' ends itself, as the overview names its nodes, a reused PID's holders numbered apart, so the command line needs no overview for them and the window names a process its own list lacks; the JSON carries them as `processNames`. Each channel's key follows its name on a line of its own, then how it was paired. Pages keep the keys' order, which the overview's channel list shares and a cursor counts by.

Revision 651 has the relationship table name each relationship's maker first, found on a live pass over the heavy machine's tables in the window (§6.8, R5). The table named a relationship's ends in the graph's display order, the mark of who connected between them, so one program's processes each connecting to its browser process read two ways row by row - "chrome.exe · PID 3000 ← chrome.exe · PID 3028" above "chrome.exe · PID 3024 → chrome.exe · PID 3000" - as their instance ids happened to sort, while `icat overview` has named the maker first since revision 581. A relationship whose connections the capture saw only one end make now names that end first, its mark pointing on at the other - "chrome.exe · PID 3028 → chrome.exe · PID 3000" - in its row and wherever its name is read, the inspector's title and the timeline's highlight among them; one made each way, or seen opening neither way, keeps the display order. An edge to other hosts still names its process first, a host's connect marked back at it. The graph's arrowheads and each card's direction line said who connected already.

Revision 652 names a peer of a process's own program by its PID at the process's rung, found on the same pass at chrome.exe's busiest process (§7.4, §6.3). Its channels lead with the process at their other end, and 34 of its 36 peers are chrome.exe's: at the default window the rail cut each row's name before the PID, so every row read "← chrome.exe · Pl…", alike, while the graph beside it named the same processes "PID 3028", as revision 646 has it name an opened group's own processes. A peer of the rung's own program is now named by its PID alone - "← PID 3028", the program being the rung's title - as the graph names it there and the group's rung names its processes; a peer of another program keeps its whole name, and read aloud every peer is named whole.

Revision 653 has Other hosts' list and card name who reached each host, as `icat hosts` has since revision 648, found on the same pass with Other hosts chosen (§6.3, R5, R18). The inspector listed the heavy machine's 97 hosts each as "1 TCP connection from 1 process · port 443 · 6 records", and the card its busiest three so, so which process had reached a host took choosing it, or a search for its address. A host listed, or named on the card, now says who reached it in the words `icat hosts` uses - the process whose connections there hold the most records, and how many more reached it, "5 UDP flows from msedge.exe · PID 3400 and 4 more" - from one helper both read (`HostLinkText.Reachers`, `ReachedBy`), a process that reached it over both transports counted once. Where the connections are one drawn process's, as on its own rung, the rows still name none: they are that process's. A host drawn on its own keeps the card that names up to three who reached it.

Revision 654 wraps the inspector's headings, found on the same pass with a group chosen (§6.8). The headings that read the selection - how its records are counted, its processes grouped or it was derived, the records the evidence and a cell list, and the rung's badge - did not wrap, so "How its processes are grouped", wider than the inspector at the default window, was cut to "How its processes are groupec" at the card's edge. They now wrap as the words beneath them do. The minimum-window test of cut-off text checked each rung after it was opened, with nothing chosen, so no heading of a chosen row was measured; it now checks each row chosen as well, and found this one.

Revision 655 has the investigation window's notes and candidate joins say in their place why they list none, the empty-state text a live pass over a fresh investigation found missing (§8.3, M5). With no note, the Notes tab showed its introduction above an empty box, its one action at the far corner; before a search, Candidate joins showed its box blank beneath the button that fills it. The notes list now says "No note yet. Add a note… writes one: about the whole investigation, or pinned at an instant of a session.", and the candidate list, until joins are first looked for, what Find candidate joins will list there and why: each proposed join, with its evidence, to accept or reject. A search that finds none says so in the summary above the list, and the main window's relationship table says why it lists none in its place, as these lists now do.

Revision 656 has `icat overview` list the busiest pairs of programs, found on a pass with `icat` over the heavy machine's session (§6.3, R18). Four of its five busiest relationships were one program's - chrome.exe's processes connecting to its main one, ten records each - and its text said nothing of which programs talked to which: that Code.exe's processes connected to node.exe's ten times, or svchost.exe's to lsass.exe's twelve. It now also sums the relationships by the two programs at their ends, each program by its executable, as the graph's groups and the edges between them are, and lists the five busiest pairs by their records: each named first by the program whose processes made its connections, "⇄" where each made some and "and" where the capture saw none open, with how many relationships it sums, the processes at either end, what they ran over and their records. A program named at both ends is its own processes connecting to each other; on the heavy machine svchost.exe's, chrome.exe's and msedge.exe's lead, then Code.exe → node.exe and svchost.exe → lsass.exe, of 22 pairs. Where no two programs share more than one relationship, the busiest relationships already say it all, and no pair is listed. The sums are the application layer's (`ProgramPairs`), so the window can list them as well, and a relationship whose process no group holds is refused rather than summed under a guessed program.

Revision 657 has a chosen program's inspector name the programs its processes talked with, found on a live pass over the heavy machine in the window (§6.3, §6.8). Choosing svchost.exe in the ranked table said how many processes it ran and how they were drawn, and that they made 62 connections and accepted 55, but not with whom: its 67 relationships ran from one collapsed node, and which programs were at their other ends was read off the graph edge by edge.
