Beneath the connections, the inspector now lists the chosen program's relationships summed by the program at each end, as `icat overview` sums them since revision 656: the five busiest pairs, each on a line of its own named maker first - "svchost.exe → lsass.exe" over "12 relationships · 72 records" - its own processes connecting to each other among them, then how many more programs. A screen reader hears who connected in words, and under a brush the heading says the list is the whole session's, as the connections line does. Processes grouped by their terminal session are not a program, and list none. A first cut set the pairs as one block of text; at the inspector's width each pair wrapped into the next, so each is now a row of its own, its counts muted beneath it.

Revision 658 adds `icat programs`, which lists every pair of programs a session's relationships join, or the pairs one program is in (§6.3, R18). Since revision 656 the overview names the five busiest pairs and says how many there are, and since revision 657 the window lists a chosen program's, but nothing listed the rest, or answered from the command line what one program talked with. `icat programs <directory>` lists them, the busiest first by their records, each named by which program's processes connected to which, with the relationships it sums, the processes at either end, what they ran over and its records, as many as `--limit` asks, 50 unless it says. `--program` lists the pairs one program is in, the program named as the listing names it, by its executable's file name with or without its extension, or by its path; a file name two programs share - two git.exe in different folders - is refused, naming both as they are listed. Its JSON (`program-pairs-v1`) lists the same, with both programs' paths and how many pairs there are in all. On the heavy machine `--program svchost` says svchost.exe's processes talked with nine other programs, lsass.exe the busiest, and with each other. The overview's note now points to it. `icat hosts` was missing from the command-line tests that read every command's help and refusals; it is among them now, with `icat programs`.

Revision 659 titles a graph edge's card as its row in the relationship table names it, the end that made its connections first (§6.3, §6.8). Since revision 651 the table names each relationship's maker first, and the inspector titles a chosen one so - "client.exe · PID 100 → server.exe · PID 200" - but the card shown while hovering its edge was titled with its ends in the graph's display order, joined by "↔" whoever connected: the card under the pointer and the inspector beside it named one relationship two ways, and an edge to the other hosts read "↔" against its own arrowhead. A card is now titled by who connected, as the table and `icat overview` name it: the maker first with "→", "⇄" where each end made some, and "↔", in display order, only where the capture saw none open. An edge to the other hosts reads "updater.exe · PID 300 → Other hosts" where its process connected out, and "Other hosts → worker.exe" where its processes accepted connections from them. The direction line beneath still counts each way.

Revision 660 has a process a search finds say what tells it apart from its program's other instances, found on a live pass over search on the heavy machine (§6.7). Searching svchost listed fifty of its eighty processes, each hit reading "Process instance of svchost.exe" beneath "svchost.exe · PID 1008": its program a second time, which its name and the hit's kind already said, and nothing to choose one by. A process a name finds now says the relationships it is an end of and the TCP connections it made and accepted, each where there are some - "11 relationships · 11 connections accepted" for one that served, "1 relationship · 1 connection made" for one that connected out - above its records, the counts its card states. Its program is said only where its name does not say it: two executables of one name in different folders, or one whose executable was never witnessed. A process found by an address its connections ran to still says what reached it there.

Revision 661 has an RPC relationship say which process called which (§6.3, §7.4, `contracts/overview-index-v1.md` minor 6). On Windows much of what processes say to each other goes over RPC, and an RPC peers capture links each call to the call that served it (`operations-v1` §5c), so who called whom is known; yet the graph drew an RPC edge in display order with no arrowhead, its card said "display order only; each end's RPC rows say which calls and which served", and its row in the relationship table had no mark. The link totals the overview keeps paired two instances in the order of their ids and dropped which was the client. Each total now also counts how many of its calls each end made, which the persisted overview keeps as its minor 6, after the rows read before its extent, and an RPC edge carries them as a TCP edge carries the connections each end made. The graph draws its arrowhead at the process that served the calls; its card is titled "caller.exe · PID 400 → services.exe · PID 1960" and says "Direction: caller.exe · PID 400 called services.exe · PID 1960, in all 2 of its calls", each way counted where both called; the table names the caller first and reads it aloud as called; the keys overlay's arrowhead says so; and `icat overview`, `icat programs` and the program pairs name the caller first, their notes saying, where RPC relationships are drawn, that an arrow points at the process called. Links an overview kept before minor 6 name no caller, and since an overview never changes an answer a reader does not use them: it follows the calls through the segments again, as where none were kept. The contract's layout had said `minor u16 = 4` since minor 5; it says 6 now.

Revision 662 has `icat processes --pid` say a process's RPC call records and where its calls are listed, found on a pass with `icat` over an RPC peers session (§7.4, R18). Asked about a process whose talk was all RPC - three calls to the service control manager, two of them linked to the calls that served them - its detail said only that it made no transport send or receive record, which is not proof it sent or received nothing, and pointed to a metric of its records by peer: nothing said it had called anyone, or where its calls are listed with the process at each one's other end. Its detail now states "RPC: 6 call records; icat operations <directory> --pid 400 lists its calls, each with the process at its other end", and its JSON counts them for the PID asked about (`rpcRecords`); a process that made no call says nothing of RPC. The command's help says so.

Revision 663 has `icat processes --pid` say who connected to whom with each process at the other end, found on a pass with `icat` over the heavy machine's session (§5.2, §7.4, R18). Asked about postgres.exe, which a pool of node.exe and python.exe clients connected to and which connected to its own workers, its detail counted "7 made · 8 accepted" and listed every process at the other end by the bytes it sent and received, but not which of them it connected to and which connected to it. Each row now counts the connections it made to that end and those it accepted from it, by the rule its own count takes (`relations-v1` §5), so the rows add up to it: one it made to itself is the row of the process itself, named so, both made and accepted; an end it connected with and sent nothing to is a row of its own; and those whose other end no record holds are the row now named "not in the capture (remote or unobserved)". It said "unresolved: no record in this capture holds the other end (a remote or unobserved peer)", 87 characters that set the width of every row. A UDP flow's row says "-", since nothing opens one, and where the session records no connection's open the table has no such column and the JSON's counts (`connectionsMade`, `connectionsAccepted`) are null.

Revision 664 has `icat processes` say what its list is ranked by and rank it by what `--rank-by` names, found on the same pass (§5.2, R18). The list was ordered by the transport bytes each instance sent and received, then its records, and said nothing of it, while `icat overview` ranks its busiest processes by their own records, so the two disagreed after their first two with no word on why; and nothing listed the processes that made or accepted the most connections, which the Desktop's Rank by selector and `icat export` offer. The list now states "Ranked by: transport bytes sent and received, the most first, then records", and `--rank-by` takes records, bytes-sent, bytes-received, bytes-sent-and-received, connections-made or connections-accepted, the names `icat export` and `icat hosts` use, ties under a connection ranking going to the other direction: on the heavy machine `--rank-by connections-accepted` lists chrome.exe, msedge.exe, lsass.exe and four svchost.exe instances first, the processes the most others connected to. A session that records no connection's open ranks nothing by connections, since how many each made is unknown rather than none, and one PID's detail is no list to rank. The JSON names its ranking (`rankedBy`). `icat`'s own help for `export --rank-by` named neither connection ranking, though the command's own help did; it names both now.

Revision 665 has a chosen process's inspector name the processes it talked with, found on a pass over the heavy machine in the window (§6.3, §6.8). Chosen, postgres.exe said "Connections: 7 made · 8 accepted, as the capture saw them open" and nothing of with whom: its fifteen relationships were among the table's 161 rows, and in the graph among discs of which several read node.exe and python.exe alike. Beneath its connections the inspector now lists "Its relationships, the busiest first" - five of them, ties by the other end's name, the rest counted ("and 10 more relationships") - each by the process at its other end, marked from the chosen process's side as its own count of connections reads: "← python.exe · PID 3904" over "1 accepted · TCP · 8 records" where the other end connected to it, "→" where it connected, "⇄" where each did, and no mark where the capture saw none open, as of a UDP flow or a connection to itself, whose edge the graph draws with no arrowhead. Of RPC calls it counts those it made or served. A screen reader hears who connected in words ("python.exe · PID 3904 connected to it: 1 accepted, TCP, 8 records"), and under a brush the heading says they are the whole session's, as a chosen program's list of programs has since revision 657.

Revision 666 has `icat overview`'s busiest processes say the connections each made and accepted (§5.2, R18). Its first table ranks processes by their own records and says what each mostly did, but said nothing of who connected, the question a heavily used machine is opened with: on the heavy machine, chrome.exe's 295 records hid that it made 41 connections, 40 of them to other hosts, and accepted 34 from its own child processes. Where the session records a connection's open, a column now says "41 made · 34 accepted", or "none" for a process the capture saw open none of its own, as `icat processes` and each process's card count them, and a note says where to read with whom: `icat processes <directory> --pid <PID>`, and `--rank-by connections-made` or `connections-accepted` for the processes that did so most. A session that records no open shows no such column, since how many each made is unknown rather than none.

Revision 667 has Enter or a double click on one of a chosen process's relationships choose the process at its other end (§6.3, §6.7, R15). Revision 665's list said whom a process talked with and who connected, but went no further: to follow who connected to whom a person found the other end's node in the graph, among many alike on a heavy machine. The list is now one the keyboard reaches a row at a time, each row saying itself whole - "browser.exe · PID 300 connected to it: 2 accepted, TCP, 8 records. Press Enter to choose the process at its other end." - and Enter or a double click on a row chooses the process at its other end, as a click on its node does: that process's relationships are then listed, the keyboard on the first, so who connected to whom is walked a key at a time, and the process come from is among them where it is among the busiest five. A connection a process made to itself goes nowhere and offers no Enter. The list's own name says what Enter does, and the keys sheet lists the key.

Revision 668 has Esc come back along a walk of who connected to whom (§6.3, §6.7, R15). Revision 667's Enter on a relationship chose the process at its other end, but the way back was in the new list only where the process come from was among its busiest five - on a hub such as the heavy machine's chrome.exe browser process, with 35 relationships, it often was not - and Esc on the machine's rung let the choice go. Each hop is now remembered with the rung it was made at, and Esc, before it climbs the ladder, comes back along the latest: the process it was made from is chosen again, the keyboard on the relationship it was left by. A hop the selection has since moved away from, or one made on another rung, is forgotten with every hop before it, and Esc then climbs the ladder as anywhere. The keys sheet lists it.

Revision 669 has Enter on one of a chosen program's pairs of programs choose the program at its other end, and Esc come back (§6.3, §6.7, R15). Revision 657's inspector named the programs a chosen program's processes talked with, but as text the keyboard could not enter, so the next program's own were found again in the ranked table. The list now takes the keyboard as a process's relationships do (revision 667): Enter or a double click on a pair chooses the program at its other end, with its ranked row on the machine's rung, the keyboard staying on the list, now that program's; the program's own processes connecting to each other go nowhere, and that row says no Enter. Each hop is remembered with the rung it was made at, and Esc comes back along the latest as it does along relationships (revision 668). A live pass on the heavy machine found that Esc letting the choice go, with the keyboard in one of the inspector's lists, left the keyboard on nothing: the list goes with the choice, and no focus event is raised as the row the keyboard was on goes, so the window's rule for a control going from under the keyboard never ran. Esc now gives the keyboard back to what the window offers - the ranked table, on its selected row or its first - without choosing a row. The keys sheet lists the new keys.

Revision 670 has a newer generation keep the way back along a walk of who connected to whom, or of which programs talked with which (R7, §3.2, §6.3). A recording publishes a newer generation every few seconds, and each is shown in a new workspace that replays the navigation the last one carried over. The hops Esc comes back along (revisions 668 and 669) were not part of it, so a walk begun while recording lost its way back at the next publication, and Esc climbed the ladder instead. The navigation now carries them, as it carries the ways back to a list, a relationship or a cell, and each is still checked when Esc comes to it: a hop whose choice or rung is no longer the view's, or whose process or program the session no longer holds, is let go with every hop before it. In the window the keyboard stays on the row it was on, and Esc gives it back to the pair it was left by wherever the newer generation lists it.

Revision 671 has a chosen process's relationships and a chosen program's pairs of programs listed whole on request (§6.3, R15). The inspector named the busiest five of each and counted the rest, so on a hub - the heavy machine's chrome.exe browser process has 35 relationships, and svchost.exe's processes talked with ten programs - the rest could be walked only from the graph or the process's rung. A button under each list now offers every one, the busiest first - "Show all 35 relationships" - and then the busiest five again. At most a hundred are listed, and past them a note says how many more there are and that `icat processes --pid` or `icat programs --program` lists them all. The keyboard goes to the first row the list adds, so Down reads on from where the busiest five ended, and a list asked for whole stays so for its process or program, as the next generation of a recording shows it.

Revision 672 has a newer generation keep the keyboard on the relationship or pair of programs it was on (R7, R15). A probe of a recording found that the keyboard on one of a chosen hub's relationships went to the list's first row whenever a newer generation restated that relationship's counts: the window gives the keyboard back to a list's chosen row, or its first, and these lists hold no choice. It now remembers what the row it was on stood for, and gives the keyboard back to the row for the same process or program wherever the newer counts list it.

Revision 673 has a chosen process's inspector list the other hosts it reached (§6.3, R15). Most of a busy machine's connections run to other hosts, which the graph draws to one Other hosts node, so which hosts one process reached was read there among every process's, or on its rung connection by connection, while `icat processes --pid` lists them by address (revision 649). The inspector now lists them beneath the process's relationships, the busiest first: each by its address, then its scope where one is known, what reached it, the ports at the address, those the process accepted said apart at its own ports, and the records - "203.0.113.1", "2 TCP connections · port 443 · 4 records". Enter or a double click on one finds every process that reached it, as Enter on a host Other hosts lists does. The busiest five are named, or every one on request as the process's relationships are, and a newer generation keeps both, with the keyboard on its host. Revision 671's note past a hundred relationships said `icat processes --pid` lists them all; it lists each process the process exchanged data with, and the note now says so.

Revision 674 has a chosen program's inspector list the other hosts its processes reached (§6.3, R15). Revision 673 listed a chosen process's; a busy program's - the heavy machine's chrome.exe runs 35 processes - were read only on Other hosts' list, among every program's. The same list now follows a chosen program, its heading saying they are its processes', and each host names the busiest of them that reached it and how many more, where several did: "52.0.0.9", "2 TCP connections from browser.exe · PID 400 and 1 more · port 443 · 2 records". Enter on one finds every process that reached it. A program's list is listed whole on request apart from any of its processes', a newer generation keeping it so, and past a hundred its note says that `icat hosts` lists every host and who reached it.

Revision 675 has `icat hosts --program` list one program's hosts (§6.3, R18). Past a hundred hosts revision 674's inspector pointed a chosen program to `icat hosts`, which lists every program's hosts, among which its own were read one by one. `--program` now takes one program's processes alone, named as `icat programs` takes one - as listed, by its executable's file name with or without its extension, or by its path - and lists the hosts they reached, or with `--address` those of them that reached one. Its heading and a Program field name the program and how many processes it ran, a byte ranking reads its connections alone, and the JSON describes it as `icat programs` does (`other-hosts-v8`). A name two programs share is refused, saying how each is listed. The inspector's note points there, and every command the window or icat cites for a person to type names a program so cmd and PowerShell take it whole: a program two folders tell apart, "git.exe (cmd)", or an executable named with a space is put in double quotes, where `icat programs --program` was cited with a name a shell would split.

Revision 676 lists paired channels busiest first (§7.1, R18). `icat channels` and the window's channel browser paged a session's channels in their keys' order, which revision 650 kept: keys are hashes, so a busy machine's heaviest connections came in no order a person could use - the heavy machine's 161 opened on msedge.exe's connections of six and four records, where chrome.exe's busiest held ten. A page now lists the busiest first, by the records at a channel's two ends, channels of as many records by key: one order within a generation, which its cursor pages through as before. The page's identity names the order, so a cursor given for the keys' order restarts rather than shifting a channel. `icat channels` says what it is ranked by and its JSON's contract is `channel-page-v2`; the browser's heading says the order, and a process's channels are headed by the process, as the workspace names it, where they were headed by its instance id. The overview's own channel list keeps the keys' order, which no list shows: each rung ranks its own rows.

Revision 677 says at which of its own ports a process accepted its connections (§6.8, §7.4, R18). A process's card, the inspector and `icat processes --pid` counted the TCP connections it made and accepted but not where they arrived, so which process served which port - the question netstat answers first - took its rung's rows one by one; only those accepted from other hosts named their ports, in Other hosts' lists. Each process now holds the ports of its own its accepted connections arrived at, read from the same openings its counts read, whoever connected - a process of the capture at a paired connection's accepting end, or another host - and the card, the inspector and `icat processes --pid` say them with the count: on the heavy machine "0 made · 9 accepted at port 135, as the capture saw them open" for svchost.exe PID 1020, "16 made · 19 accepted at port 9223" for msedge.exe PID 3400. A program says its processes' ports together, each once, the lowest first, past three the rest counted, and the JSON lists an instance's as `acceptedPorts`, null where the session records no connection's open. A port search still finds a process by the ports it accepted other hosts' connections at alone; finding it by those it accepted its own machine's at is the next slice's.

Revision 678 has a port search find the processes that accepted connections at that port of their own whoever connected (§6.7, §6.3). Since revision 575 ":3389" found a process that accepted another host's connections there, read from its host links, but a port its own machine's processes connected to found none: ":9222" on the heavy machine listed chrome.exe's channels and not chrome.exe PID 3000, which accepted 34 connections there. A port searched alone now finds a process by the ports revision 677 gives it, the hit saying "TCP connections accepted at the port searched" and ranked as one found by another host's connections at it, the busier first; the same rule marks it in the graph and its lanes. An endpoint still names the address at the other end, which the ports a process listened at do not hold.

Revision 679 has `icat processes`' and `icat overview`'s tables say where each process accepted its connections (§5.2, R18). Revision 677 said the ports in `icat processes --pid`, one process at a time, while the tables a person reads a busy machine by counted the connections alone. Their Connections cells now say the ports of its own each accepted at, in numbers as `icat hosts`' cells say a host's, so `icat processes --rank-by connections-accepted` reads as a list of who served where - on the heavy machine "0 made · 11 accepted at 135" for svchost.exe PID 1008, "7 made · 8 accepted at 5432" for postgres.exe PID 4000 - and each table's note says so.

Revision 680 adds `icat processes --port`, the command line's search for a port's listeners (§5.2, §6.7, R18). Revision 678's search finds the processes that accepted connections at a port of their own whoever connected, and `icat hosts --address` the processes whose connections ran to a port at another host, but no command named a port's own listeners. `--port 135` lists the instances whose accepted connections arrived at 135, as the window's search finds them - the heavy machine's six svchost.exe instances - ranked by the connections each accepted unless `--rank-by` names another measure, under an Accepted at line; a port none accepted at says so and points to `icat hosts --address`. It takes the port after a colon or not, refuses one that names no port or stands beside `--pid`, and where the session records no connection's open says which instances listened is unknown rather than none. The JSON names the port, null for every instance.

Revision 681 is a live pass over listening ports and the port search on the heavy machine at the default window (§6.7, §6.3). The ports held: chrome.exe PID 3000's inspector reads "41 made · 34 accepted at port 9222", svchost.exe's program "62 made · 55 accepted at port 135", and ":9222" lists chrome.exe PID 3000 first, its group's node ringed open as one of whose 35 processes the search finds. What it found is in the hits that follow: each of the 34 channels ":9222" names was titled "Channel" beneath a CHANNEL eyebrow, the processes at its ends said in its detail as "Channel between" two, whoever connected. A channel's hit is now titled by them as the channel list names them - "chrome.exe · PID 3028 → chrome.exe · PID 3000", the maker first with an arrow at the one it connected to, or the two joined by "and" where the capture saw neither open it - and says "TCP channel whose endpoint the search names", never the endpoint (P16); a channel whose ends no process holds is named by its relationship's, as before. A hit's name now wraps within the rail rather than being cut, which cut a channel's other end at the rail's edge.

Revision 682 has a relationship say at which port its connections arrived (§6.3, §6.8, §7.4). Since revision 576 a relationship says who connected to whom, and since revision 677 a process at which of its own ports it accepted, but a relationship's own row, card and inspector line left where its connections arrived to its channels' rows: "client.exe → server.exe", not that the client connected to the server's 8080. Each TCP relationship now holds the ports its connections arrived at, at the end that accepted each, read from the same openings its made counts read, so none for a UDP flow, RPC calls, connections seen opening neither way or a process connected to itself. The relationship table says them beside what it ran over - "TCP at 8080", "TCP at 8080 and 9000" where each end connected to the other - cut with the whole in a tooltip where the column is narrow, and so does a chosen relationship's subtitle; its edge's card begins "TCP at 8080", and a chosen process's relationships say "2 accepted · TCP at 8080 · 8 records". The command line's tables are the next slice's.

Revision 683 has the command line, and a chosen program's pairs in the inspector, say at which port relationships' connections arrived (§6.3, §6.8, §7.4, R18). Revision 682 said it in the window's relationship table, a relationship's card and a chosen process's relationships, but `icat overview`'s busiest relationships and pairs of programs, `icat programs` and `icat processes --pid` still said only "TCP", so which program served which port - svchost.exe's endpoint mapper at 135 for every program that reached it on the heavy machine, chrome.exe's main process at 9222 - took `icat channels` connection by connection. `icat overview`'s busiest relationships now say "TCP at 7209", and its pairs of programs and `icat programs` say a pair's ports together, each once, the lowest first, past three the rest counted - "Code.exe → node.exe" over "TCP at 7200, 7201, 7202 and 7 more" - a list of ports set apart by a comma from a mechanism after it, "TCP at 80 and 443, and UDP"; their notes say what the ports are only where some are said. `icat processes --pid` says each end's connections at the ports they arrived at, those it made at the end's - "1 made to 135" for msedge.exe PID 3400's to svchost.exe PID 1020, "15 made to 443" to other hosts - and those it accepted at its own, "1 accepted at 9223"; its JSON lists them as `madeToPorts` and `acceptedPorts`, null where the session records no open, and `icat programs --json` lists a pair's `acceptedPorts`. A chosen program's pairs in the inspector say what they ran over too: "12 relationships · TCP at 49668 · 72 records" for svchost.exe → lsass.exe.

Revision 684 adds `icat ports`, the session's netstat of listeners (§6.3, §7.4, R18). Since revision 677 a process says at which of its own ports it accepted connections, and since revision 680 `icat processes --port` lists one port's listeners, but no list named the ports themselves: which ports this machine served at, and for whom, took each process's detail in turn. `icat ports` lists each port its processes accepted TCP connections at, as the capture saw them open, read from the same openings a process's card counts: the process that accepted them there - one row for each process and port, so a port two processes listened at reads as two - how many, and who connected: the processes of the capture, one named and several counted with the programs they ran, named where three or fewer; other hosts, one by its address; and processes on this machine the capture did not observe, at loopback or at the listener's own address, told apart from other hosts as the census tells them. On the heavy machine chrome.exe PID 3000's 9222 leads, "34 from 34 processes of chrome.exe", then msedge.exe PID 3400's 9223 and lsass.exe PID 700's 49668, "13 from 13 processes of svchost.exe and services.exe". The busiest come first, ties by port; `--limit` lists that many, and `--json` (`listening-ports-v1`) names every process and host that connected, with how many each made. A session that records no connection's open is refused, since who listened is then unknown rather than none, and one whose processes accepted none says so; `icat overview`'s busiest processes, `icat processes --port`'s help and its empty answer point to it.

Revision 685 has other hosts' words say which way the connections went (§6.3, §6.8, R18). A live pass over the demo after revision 684 found `icat overview` saying the demo server's processes made "2 TCP connections from 2 processes to 1 host" when the capture saw that host connect to both, at api.exe's 443 and files.exe's 8443: a process's connections to other hosts were said to run to them whichever end opened them, words older than revision 573's count of those accepted from them, which said the accepted ones apart but left the words they sat beside untouched. They are now said as the capture saw them open: to the hosts where it saw none accepted from them - those it saw opening neither way said as most are, made by the processes - from them where it saw every one accepted, "2 TCP connections from 1 host to 2 processes", "1 TCP connection from 1 host", and with the hosts, or between the processes and the hosts for several, where it saw some each way; a UDP flow, which nothing opens, is with its hosts as before. `icat overview`'s field and table, a process's and a program's cards, an aggregate's process rows and the relationship table's edges to other hosts say them alike, and the overview's column reads "Connections with other hosts".

Revision 686 has a host's own lines say which way its connections went (§6.3, R18). Revision 685 made a process's connections to other hosts say which way they went, but a host's own lines, which name the processes at the other end, still said its connections came from them: a window pass over the demo server found Other hosts' card listing "10.0.0.10 (private) · 2 TCP connections from api.exe · PID 5100 and 1 more · accepted here at ports 443 and 8443", though that host connected to both. A host's line now puts the processes as the capture saw the connections open: from them where it saw none accepted from the host, to them where it saw every one accepted - "2 TCP connections to api.exe · PID 5100 and 1 more" - and with them where it saw some, or saw them beside UDP flows, which are from the processes as before. Other hosts' card and list, a host's card once drawn on its own - "1 TCP connection to 1 process" - and a chosen host's records say it alike.

Revision 687 adds `icat ports --investigation`, which names who on another machine connected to each listener (§6.3, §8.3, R18). Since revision 570 `icat hosts --investigation` names an other host's candidate other ends - the processes of another member's capture whose connections mirror the member's to the host - but address by address, so on the demo server the client's address named both of the client's processes, "demo client: browser.exe · PID 4100 and sync.exe · PID 4200", without saying which connected to which listener. A candidate join's own connection names its listener - the process that accepted it and its own port - so `icat ports --investigation` names each listener's own: the api's 443 the client's browser.exe, the files' 8443 its sync.exe, as far as the captures' endpoints say and never as an established join. A listener no other host connected to says -, one whose hosts no candidate mirrors says none, a join a person rejected names no end and one they accepted says so; the JSON lists each listener's `candidateOtherEnds`, null without an investigation, and an investigation the session is no member of, or a file that is none, is refused as `icat hosts` refuses them. The README's demo tour runs it.

Revision 688 captions the graph's host nodes by which way their connections went (§6.3, R18). Revisions 685 and 686 made the words for other hosts' connections say which way they went, but the caption beneath Other hosts, and beneath a host drawn on its own, still counted the processes its connections came from: on the demo server Other hosts read "from 2 processes" though both processes only accepted that host's connections. Each host node now holds how many of its connections the hosts opened, from the same openings its edges' arrowheads are drawn by, and is captioned from the processes where none was, to them where every one was - "to 2 processes" on the demo server - and with them where some were, or where UDP flows run beside them; the heavy machine's Other hosts, whose hosts were all connected to, still reads "from 21 processes".

Revision 689 heads a process's list of other hosts by those that connected to it, where every one did (§6.3, R18). The same window pass found the demo server's files.exe listing "Other hosts it reached" though its one connection with another host was that host's, which it accepted: the heading said every host was one it reached, whichever end opened the connections. A chosen process's list, and a program's, is now headed "Other hosts that connected to it" - "to its processes" for a program - where the capture saw every one of its connections with them accepted from them, and "Other hosts it reached" otherwise, since then it reached some: those it accepted beside them are said apart in their rows, and a UDP flow, which nothing opens, is one it reached. `icat processes --pid` heads its list of an instance's other hosts alike.

Revision 690 lists the session's listening ports in the window (§6.3, R15, R18). Since revision 684 `icat ports` lists the ports a session's processes accepted connections at and who connected to each, but the window named a process's ports only on its own card and in its own inspector, so which process served at which port, and for whom, took each process in turn there. With nothing chosen on the machine rung, the inspector now lists them, the busiest five or a hundred on request: each port in a column of its own, as netstat sets it, beside the process that accepted there, how many connections it accepted and from whom - the processes of the capture, other hosts by address, or processes on this machine the capture did not observe - in the words `icat ports` uses, which both now share. Enter or a double click on one chooses that process, whose relationships, or else the other hosts that connected to it, then have the keyboard, and Esc comes back to the port, in a newer generation too; a port of InterCat's own is set aside with its process. The overview carries the listeners, derived from the same relations its processes' accepted ports are, so the list reads no segment.

Revision 691 is a live pass over the window's listening ports on the heavy machine and the demo (§6.3). The list held: the heavy machine's 32 listening ports read busiest first, chrome.exe's 9222 with 34 connections from 34 of its own processes; Enter on it chose PID 3000, the keyboard on its first relationship, one of those processes' connections to it at 9222, and Esc came back to the port. The demo server lists api.exe's 443, db.exe's 5432 and files.exe's 8443, and the demo client, whose processes accepted nothing, none. Two things read badly at the inspector's default width. The port column, as wide as the widest port, left the process beside it so little room that "PID" broke from its number - "chrome.exe · PID" above "3000" - and who connected ran four lines a row. A row now keeps each PID to its number, and says who connected beneath its port and its process, across the row's width: two lines where it took four.

Revision 692 names each listening port's candidate other ends in the window, for a session opened from its investigation (§6.3, §8.3, R15, R18). Since revision 687 `icat ports --investigation` names, beside each listener, the processes of the investigation's other captures whose connections mirror those it accepted, and since revision 571 the window names an other host's, but its listening ports named none. The read that proposes the hosts' now proposes the ports' too, from the same comparison of every member's connections, so it compares nothing more, and a later publication takes it over as it took the hosts': on the demo server, api.exe's 443 names the client's browser.exe and files.exe's 8443 its sync.exe, and the database's 5432, which only the server's own api.exe reached, none. A note beneath the list says once what they are, never an established join, and rows restated as they arrive keep the keyboard on the port it was on.

Revision 693 lists a chosen program's processes' listening ports in its inspector (§6.3, R15). The machine's list answers which process served at which port, but which of a program's processes did - which of the heavy machine's eighty svchost.exe processes accepted at 135, and from whom - still took each process's card in turn. A chosen program's inspector now lists its processes' ports as the machine's list does with nothing chosen, under "3 listening ports of its processes", each list asked for whole on its own. Enter on one chooses the process, and Esc comes back to the program, its ranked row chosen again and the keyboard on the port; a program reached along a walk of programs is come back to first and the walk after, so Esc takes the way back to the port's program before the program hop that reached it, and a program a newer generation no longer holds lets the way back go. Building the rows found that each one looked its processes and programs up by a scan of the session's for every end that connected, and was built again at every read: they are now built once for the list's scope, whether it is asked for whole and the candidate other ends read, from lookups built once. And a list not yet said for any scope took the first choice made for no change, so choosing a process first left the machine's list shown; it is now said for its scope the first time. The suite found a window test's cleanup failing under load: a session opened from its investigation reads its candidate joins in the background, and a read let go stops at its next step, so one could still be reading the demo's sessions as the test removed them, and re-establish a session's evidence lease guard mid-removal. The reads are counted, the test waits for none to be running, and the tests' temporary folders try their removal again, briefly, until it holds.

Revision 694 lists one program's listening ports at the command line, as its inspector does (§7.4, R18). A chosen program's list in the window pointed past its hundredth port to `icat ports --limit` with the machine's count, which lists every process's ports with the program's among them: on the heavy machine, svchost.exe's beside every other program's. `icat ports --program` now takes one program's processes alone, named as icat programs lists it - svchost.exe - or by its executable's path, as `icat hosts --program` takes it: the ports they accepted connections at, under a Program field that names the program and counts its processes, and the document names it too. A name two programs share is refused with how each is listed, so one can be named as listed, and a program whose processes accepted nothing says so by its name. The window's hint for a program's list now names that command with as many as the program has, the program quoted where a shell would split its name - `icat ports --program "server.exe (Apps)" --limit 102`.

Revision 695 has a program's rung, with nothing chosen there, describe the program it opened (§6.3, R15). A live pass over the heavy machine found that choosing svchost.exe on the machine rung named it and listed the programs its eighty processes talked with, the other hosts they reached and the six ports they accepted connections at, but Enter, which opens it, left the inspector saying "Nothing selected" over the very processes it had just described: what the program did was a rung up, and going back for it left the rung. An opened channel's rung already described its channel with nothing chosen there; a program's rung now does the same - its name, how its processes are drawn there, how they were grouped and the connections they made and accepted, and its pairs of programs, other hosts and listening ports, each asked for whole on its own and pointing past the most it lists to icat by the program's name. The program is described, not chosen: no node is highlighted for it, a process chosen at the rung is described instead, a process's rung below describes no program, and Esc still climbs back to the machine with the program chosen. Enter on one of its ports or pairs chooses that process or program there, and Esc comes back to the program described - nothing chosen - rather than choosing the program at its own rung, which would have highlighted every one of its processes and taken an Esc of its own.

Revision 696 lists one program's instances at the command line (§7.4, R18). Which of a program's processes did what - which of the heavy machine's eighty svchost.exe instances made and accepted the most connections - took the window's rung for the program, or `icat processes` listing every instance on the machine for the eighty to be picked out by eye. `icat processes --program` takes one program's instances alone, named as icat programs lists it - svchost.exe - or by its executable's path, as `icat hosts`, `icat ports` and `icat programs` take it, under a Program field that names it and counts its instances, and the document names it too. With `--port` it lists those of them that accepted connections at that port, and where none did it says so by the program's name; beside `--pid`, which shows one PID's instances in full, it is refused. The four commands that take `--program` now resolve it, and refuse a name that is blank, names no program or names several, in one place, where three had each said so in a copy of their own.

Revision 697 has Esc on the machine rung let go of the ranked table's row with the rest of the choice (§6.7, R15). A live pass over the heavy machine found that Esc there said "Nothing selected" in the inspector and took the graph's ring away, while the ranked table still showed chrome.exe's row chosen, so the table and the inspector disagreed about what was chosen. Letting go of the choice now lets go of the row it made - before a brush let go with it restates the rows, which would have chosen the row again - and the keyboard stays on that row, so Down chooses the one after it and Enter opens the row the keyboard is on, as it always did with no row chosen. A press on the graph's empty space still only gives the graph the keyboard and keeps the choice, as §6.3 has it, so a press made to reach the graph's arrow keys throws nothing away. The same pass over the command line - icat processes, ports, hosts and programs on the heavy machine, with and without --program - found nothing to fix. The status document's slices for revisions 620 to 679 moved to a sixth historical log, whole and in order.

Revision 698 moves the plan's revision paragraphs out of §7.4 into this log. Each revision from 173 on had added its paragraph after the one before it, and they had come to run on for some 1,400 lines at the end of §7.4, among the correlation contracts, in several interleaved runs with a line-each index in their midst: over half of the plan's 1.2 MB, which a reader of the contracts had to scroll past. They moved here whole, the index first and the full paragraphs in the order of their revisions, checked to carry each of the 715 paragraphs exactly once. Each index line said which paragraph its revision's followed, which that order would have made wrong; it now says whether the paragraph is below or, for a revision that changed a section's own design, in the plan. The plan keeps one sentence where they were, pointing here, and its other sections keep the revisions that changed their own design. A live pass in the window over the investigation demo's opened programs, each listing its port with the candidate other end its connection mirrors, and a check of the heavy machine's inspector lists at the smallest window for a PID parted from its number, found nothing to fix.

Revision 699 has a ranked export name the `icat metric` query its rows answer (IC-018, R18, I16). An export named its session, generation, rung, breadcrumb, filters and interval, but not the manifest that pins that generation's contents, nor the specification its numbers answer, so nothing but a reader's reconstruction tied a ranked file to the command line's answer. Every export, the window's and `icat export`'s alike, now names the manifest digest, and a ranked export of the machine rung names the request whose groups its rows are: its identity, its canonical specification and the command that asks it, with its interval in native ticks and its evidence policy written out where it is not the default; run, that command prints the same identity over the same generation. Records rank as the observations metric, bytes sent, received or both as the byte metrics, peers as the active-peers metric, RPC errors as the errors metric, and call and serve times as the median duration of the calls completed. Calls made or served alone and connections made or accepted are counts no request makes; a group's, a process's or a channel's rows are no request's groups; rows from which InterCat's own processes were set aside are not an answer's, which counts them; and rows read from a generation the session has since moved past are not named by the newer one's identity. Each such export says why it names no query, and one whose session cannot be read to name it is exported regardless, saying so. A redacted report names neither the digest nor the identity. CSV keeps both in two last columns; the JSON says why where no query is named. A property test over random machines - two instances of one program in different terminal sessions, a service, a process with no witnessed executable and one naming no session, a reused PID, one-sided and unmeasured TCP, unpaired RPC calls and calls whose stop carried no status - checks every row against the named answer's groups, value for value, whole and in an interval, grouped by executable and by terminal session. The row of processes with no witnessed executable, or naming no session, is the answer's unattributed group for that reason, whose distinct peers the answer does not count. The test found one inconsistency in `icat metric` itself: an error count over calls none of which carried a status was unavailable, rightly, but grouped it then listed no group at all, where a byte total none of whose contributions was measured lists each group unmeasured; it now lists them, as the window's rows do.

Revision 700 has an evidence export name the `icat evidence` query its records answer, and closes IC-018's acceptance (R18, I16). An evidence page's identity - the digest of its scope, rules and derivation that its cursors are bound to - was in `icat evidence --json` and nowhere a person or an export could see it, and two scopes the window's evidence rung reads had no command at all: a process's HTTP exchanges, whose key `icat evidence --channel` refused, and records bound under an evidence policy a person chose, which it could not bind. `icat evidence` now takes an HTTP exchanges' or one exchange's key, as it took an RPC key, and `--evidence-policy`, and states each page's manifest and query identity, as its JSON had them. An evidence export, the window's and `icat export`'s, names that identity and the command that lists the same records - its channel, host, RPC or HTTP key, its owner processes, its interval, a lane's narrowing and a policy other than the default each written out - in JSON beside a ranked export's query, its form named, and in the CSV's last column. A test turns nine scopes, the whole session's, a group's, one process's in an interval, a paired channel's first end, a lane's mechanism and direction, another host's, an RPC channel's, a process's HTTP exchanges and candidates counted, each into its command, runs it, and finds the same identity, manifest and records. With revision 699, this meets IC-018's acceptance: the query output of an export matches the command line's, ranked rows value for value with `icat metric` and records record for record with `icat evidence`; the canonical form has its golden corpus (FX-QUERY-001); evidence and channel cursors are bound to their query's identity, generation and manifest, and restart rather than shift (revisions 89 and 95); and §21.2's artifacts exist. The window's numeric reads are keyed by the generation they read and the scope they count, each answer of an earlier publication shown only as such and a superseded count never applied (P21), rather than by a canonical form of each pane, which nothing compares across the window and the command line; such forms for the graph projection, the viewport and the query generation stay, as `query-identity-v1` §9 has them, for a later canonicalization version that a consumer needs.

Revision 701 keeps a level of the tile index spanning a generation's segments (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md` minor 4, S4). A brush counted each segment's section: at its two ends down to the records, and between them each segment's top tiles from their tallies, so its cost grew with the number of segments a session was written in, which a long live capture, compacted at about 250,000 rows a segment, makes hundreds or thousands. At 1,000,000 records of the scale gate's generator in 400 segments of 2,500, a brush of a third of the session took 152 to 195 ms, against 3 to 32 ms in four segments. Where two or more segments hold timed records and none's readings go backwards, the index now keeps a section of tiles over the whole generation. Its finest tiles are the narrowest power of ten no narrower than any segment's finest that spans the readings in at most 2,048 tiles and one for each 256 records, each the sum of the segments' tiles of that width: their counts, and both parts of their tallies where each takes at most a third of the bytes of the records beneath. It keeps no records. A zoom, a brush and a focus count it from its top down, as they count a section, and a finest tile they cannot count whole leaves its range to the sections whose readings meet it, which the extents it states find without reading every section's header; ranges with no tile counted whole between them are one. That counts every record once, exactly as reading every row does. The same brush now takes 5 to 7 ms, a whole session's group lanes at the window's 256 columns 207 to 239 ms against 398 to 504, and its busiest connection's ends 168 to 231 ms against 414 to 460. At 2,000 columns every finest tile lies across a column boundary, and a focus counts the sections as before; four large segments count as before. The level took 3.9 MB of the million records' 17 MB index. While it is written its finest tiles' tally entries are bounded, about 24 MB, and past the bound they are made ten times wider. An index of minor 3 is still read, section by section, and a checkpoint publishes it again. Tests compare a zoom, a brush, a group's lanes and a channel's ends over many segments with their rows under every policy, count what the level and the sections beneath it read, keep the level within its bound and refuse a damaged or inconsistent spanning section. All twenty-two mutations were caught.

Revision 702 has a reader that meets a session's pointer while a writer publishes acquire the generation it names, rather than refuse a session that verifies (`contracts/store-v1.md` §6). Revision 701's suite found it: a window following a capture failed at its first step, opening the evidence session as its first generation published. Opening read the pointer and found none, found no last-known-good either, and then, finding the pointer the writer had just written, concluded that a pointer naming nothing usable was there and refused the session. A reader now reads the pointer again when what it names fails: where it reads otherwise than it did, a writer published meanwhile, and where it is there but cannot be read - which Windows can make so for an instant while a file is replaced - it is read after a few milliseconds; either way the generation it names is acquired again, up to three times in all, before the last-known-good is verified or the session refused. A pointer that stays unreadable is refused, or rolled back from, as before. A test reads a session's pointer through a directory that refuses to open it once, twice and always, and finds the generation it names, the newest one and not the last-known-good, and the refusal or rollback a pointer that cannot be read gets.

Revision 703 lays a pinned node out exactly where it was pinned (R12). Revision 702's suite found a graph motion test drawing a node pinned at 0.95 of the pane at 0.9499999999999998 once the layout landed, a hair off the place it was pinned to, and only when the layout landed before the test looked. The layout works in drawn units, the pane 2.6 wide, and read a pin into them and back out, which can change a fraction's last bits. A pinned node's place is now the pin itself, never moved, and every other node's is read back as before. A test pins a node at 205 places, 0.95 and 0.9 among them and 200 drawn at random, and finds each laid out exactly there, in a process graph and in a drawn one.

Revision 704 keeps the bytes a tile's records measured in the tile index (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md` minor 5, S3, S4). A zoom ranked by bytes counted its records from the persisted tiles but read every segment its interval met to measure its lanes' bytes, as the interval table did an interval's: at 1,000,000 records the whole session in 2,000 columns took 88 to 99 ms once its four segments were cached and 585 ms before, and 141 to 406 ms over 400 segments. Each tile now keeps its records' transport-observed bytes summed for each mechanism and accounting side - the bytes measured, how many records measured them and how many declared a size they did not record (R3) - and each finest tile's records keep their measure. A zoom's byte lanes, and an interval's bytes of every record or one mechanism's, add a tile's sums whole where its records fall in one column and read records only where a column boundary falls among them, from the level spanning the segments first where it keeps their bytes: 3.6 to 10.9 ms, and 8.4 to 19.7 ms over 400 segments, opening no segment. A segment whose rows a byte measurement refuses keeps no bytes in its section, and its rows are read and refused as before. The index grows from 17.5 to 21.1 bytes a record at 1,000,000 records in four segments. Its tallies are weighed against what a count reads of the records, their measures left out, so they are kept where minor 4 kept them; weighed with them, tiles of a few hundred records would have kept tallies too, and the index taken 27.2 bytes a record. An index of minor 3 or 4 is read as before, its bytes measured from the segments, until a checkpoint publishes it again. Tests add up random sessions' bytes from their tiles and from their rows - over many segments and the level spanning them, with a section keeping no bytes, and from indexes damaged or rewritten as minors 3 and 4 wrote them - and find them the same. A brushed ranking by bytes and a group's or a process's byte lanes still read their rows, whose owners no sum keeps apart.

Revision 705 reuses a completed import (§18.4, IC-013). `icat import <etl> --into <folder>` refused any folder that was not empty, so an import run again - after it finished, or after it stopped - always failed, and a folder whose staged files a review had removed still held the lock the review took, which kept every later import out. What the folder holds is now read before a schema is compiled or a record read (`contracts/import-v1.md` §8). A session's identity is derived from its import's - the file's bytes, the evidence kept and the contract versions - so a folder holding a session of that identity holds this import: every file its generation names is verified and the session reused, the file hashed and read no further and nothing written, and where its newest generation does not verify its last-known-good one is reused as a partial result. The file is hashed only when the folder holds a session to compare it with. Another session, an import of the same path made from other bytes, a session that does not verify, what an unfinished publication staged, files a writer is staging, and anything else are refused, each with what was found and what to do instead; a folder holding only a session's lock files is new. `--json` and `--output` write `import-reuse-v1`. Because all of it runs before anything that needs Windows, the command-line tests now run `icat import --into` itself, which nothing tested on the machine that builds InterCat. A catalogue of imports across folders is still not kept, so an import into a new folder does not find the same import in another.

Revision 706 measures a brush ranked by bytes from the tile index (§12.1 S3, S4; IC-017). Revision 704's byte sums keep a tile's bytes by mechanism and side, which add up a zoom's byte lanes but cannot say whose they were, so a brushed ranking by bytes still read every row in its interval: at 10,000,000 records 1,336 ms the first time and up to 513 ms after, past §12's 250 ms. Since minor 6 (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md` §1) every tile also keeps its records' bytes tallied by owner key, channel key and accounting side - how many measured them, how many declared a size they did not record, and the bytes they measured - and a brush adds a tile its interval holds whole from them: each entry to the instance its owner key binds where the evidence policy admits the binding's strength, otherwise to the records no instance takes, and to the end of a drawn channel that instance holds. It reads records only where one of its ends falls among a tile's, from the level spanning the segments first where its finest tiles' byte tallies fit its bound. A brush's bounds place a record by the first native reading at or after each (`metrics-v1` §5), which a tile's tick places alike but for a record before the capture's epoch in the tick a bound falls in, so a segment holding an untimed record, or, for a brush from the epoch or before it, a record before the epoch, is measured from its rows. At 10,000,000 records a brushed ranking by bytes takes 3.0 ms the first time and 1.9 to 12.5 ms after, opening no segment, and the scale gate measures it; the index grows from 21.1 to 22.3 bytes a record at 1,000,000 records. An index of minor 5 or earlier is read as before until a checkpoint publishes it again. Tests measure random sessions' brushes - under every policy, over many segments and the level spanning them, past its bound, from before the epoch of a nanosecond clock, and from indexes damaged or rewritten as minors 3 to 5 wrote them - from the tiles and from the rows, and find them the same. A group's or a process's byte lanes still read their rows.

Revision 707 adds up a group's process lanes under a byte ranking from the tile index (§6.2, §12.1 S3; IC-017). Revision 706's byte tallies keep a tile's bytes by owner, channel and side, which hold each process's bytes apart, but a group's lanes still read every row their interval met: at 10,000,000 records a 40-process group in 2,000 columns, its lanes in 500, took 2,366 ms the first time. A tile all of whose records fall in one column of the machine row's and one of the lanes' now adds its byte tallies - every entry to the machine row, and each of a process with a lane whose binding the evidence policy admits to that lane - and a tile a boundary falls in gives way to its children or its records, from the level spanning the segments first, each section by a worker of its own. The lanes take 641 ms the first time and 68 ms at the median after, from 114, opening no segment, and the scale gate measures them. Their slowest, 142 ms, is about the rows' 163: the scale generator's records take turns among 200 connections record by record, so only tiles of thousands of records keep byte tallies, and a column boundary reads the records of the tile it falls in. That rule - a tile keeps its byte tallies as it keeps its tallies, where they take at most a third of the bytes of the records beneath it - has been the writer's since revision 706, whose contract said a section kept them whatever their size; it now says the rule. Tests add up random sessions' lanes - under every policy, in the machine row's columns or fewer, over many segments and the level spanning them, with a section keeping no byte tallies, and from indexes damaged, stating another derivation or rewritten as minors 3 to 5 wrote them - from the tiles and from the rows, and find them the same. A process's direction rows under a byte ranking still read their rows: the byte tallies do not hold source directions apart.

Revision 708 adds up a process's direction rows under a byte ranking from the tile index (§6.2, §12.1 S3; IC-017), the last of a finished session's byte views that read every row its interval met: at 10,000,000 records the busiest process's rows in 2,000 columns took 1,817 ms the first time. Revision 706's byte tallies keep a process's bytes apart but not the direction its source states, so since minor 7 (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md` §1) an entry is of one owner key, channel key, direction code and side, written as its channel key times 32 plus its direction code times 4 plus its side. A process sends outbound and receives inbound, so its entries are as many as before, and the index at 10,000,000 records grows by 0.04%. Its rows then add each tile that falls in one column from its byte tallies - every entry to the machine row, and each of the process whose binding the evidence policy admits to its direction's row - as a group's lanes do, through the same walk: 312 ms the first time and 51 ms at the median after, from 82, opening no segment. A minor 6 index still serves a brush and a group's lanes from its byte tallies, and its direction rows read their rows until a checkpoint publishes it again. Tests add up random sessions' rows under every policy, over many segments and the level spanning them, from indexes damaged or rewritten as minors 3 to 6 wrote them, the latter's byte tallies merged across directions as revision 706 wrote them, from the tiles and from the rows, and find them the same.

Revision 709 keeps a catalogue of completed imports (§18.4, IC-013). Since revision 705 an import into the folder that already holds it reuses it, but an import of the same file into any other folder read it again, because nothing listed where a user's imports were. `icat import` now keeps `import-catalogue-v1.json` in the user's own InterCat folder, or in the file `--catalogue` names (`contracts/import-v1.md` §8.1). Every import published or reused is recorded - its import digest, session identity, folder, source and length - and an import into a new folder looks there first: the file is hashed only when an import of a file its length is listed, and a listed folder is reused only once it holds this import and every file its generation names verifies, as the folder `--into` named would be. Nothing is written into the new folder, the report names both, and `--again` imports the file there all the same. The catalogue is a hint, never evidence: listed folders that no longer hold the import are forgotten when it is next written, and one whose session cannot be read is named and kept, since it may yet be recovered. Two choices of the first design were changed before it was committed. A file that is not a catalogue of this contract is named once and never replaced, where the first design started a new catalogue over it, which would have overwritten whatever file `--catalogue` named by mistake. And writers lock a file beside the catalogue that stays, where the first design deleted it on close, which let a writer that opened it just before the deletion hold a lock on a file no longer there while another created and locked a new one; a writer replaces the catalogue whole, so writers recording at once all stay listed and a reader never meets half of one. An import that publishes on Windows records itself the same way, which the machine that builds InterCat cannot run, like the rest of an import's Windows path.

Revision 710 makes a session's derivation incremental at commit while it is still being recorded (§12.1 S1, S4, §10.2). A finished session reopens from its checkpoint and persisted overview in about 0.02 s, but one a follow was still writing derived its processes, relations and counts from every record: at 4,000,000 records in sixteen segments, 1.5 to 1.7 s of an open that took 1.4 to 2 s. A checkpoint is not carried into the next generation, since store-v1 names an index only in the generation that published it, so a damaged one costs at most a rollback and never fails a live writer's next commit; and publishing one in a generation of its own races the live writer. So a live follow - the window's, `icat capture`'s and `icat follow`'s - now stages a fresh one into each generation it publishes, beside the chunk that generation adds: a derivation checkpoint of the generation it extends, named for the new one (`contracts/derivation-checkpoint-v1.md` §2). It is the follow's own derivation of that generation, extended chunk by chunk as the window's is, and in the window it is that very derivation, shared through the derivation cache. A reader of the new generation extends it over the chunk it adds, as the contract's prefix rule always allowed: wherever a derivation kept in memory would extend, and in full where that would not, after a late record or a compaction, to the same answer. At 4,000,000 records a cold open now derives in 0.2 s and takes about 0.5 s whole; what remains is counting the overview from every segment's tiles, 0.16 to 0.2 s, which a finished session's persisted overview avoids. A checkpoint in every generation would have left one orphan per publication, since nothing removed a superseded generation's indexes; a writer that removes a superseded manifest now removes with it every index no kept generation names, whole or not at all, so the manifest a later publication finds still leads it to them (store-v1 §9). A recording's own writer, the broker's or `icat record`'s, stages none, since deriving would put analysis on the writer that holds the capture.

Revision 711 runs whole what a coverage pass over revisions 699 to 710 found no test ran (R10), and fixes what that found. Of the 2,993 lines those revisions added that a test can reach, 143 ran in none. Most guard against files no writer makes, and some are Windows paths, but four ran nowhere though real data reaches them: a tile holding more than 64 byte tally entries - an instant of a busy machine's many connections - read by growing its buffers; a live checkpoint that cannot be derived, which is not staged; a ranked export whose session cannot be read again, which names no query; and a catalogue of imports that cannot be read, which is left as it is. Each now runs whole. Running the tile index's byte sums whole found the rest. It keeps no sums of a segment whose sizes add up past what a sum holds, as reading its rows would refuse it, but reading its rows did not refuse it: a brush and a whole-session byte ranking ended with an overflow, as did any lane, interval or direction row whose column held such sizes; the window's read of them failed unhandled, so its card and tables never said they could not be read, and icat ended with a stack trace; a process's connections to other hosts wrapped silently into a small total; and the checkpoint of such a finished session ended with an overflow, while for a single record measured in another unit it published nothing, so the session reopened by deriving every record. Since this revision a measurement refuses a side past what a sum holds, naming the row that took it there, as it refuses two units (`contracts/segment-v1.md` §6); every total added up from measurements - across segments, processes, columns, a connection's or an HTTP exchange's parts, and icat metric's - refuses alike, as unreadable input marked as a refusal to sum rather than damage; each byte answer is checked to add up within a sum, so no total a view takes of its parts can pass it; a byte view sums bytes only, refusing a segment that measures them in another unit as icat metric refuses two; a tile index's section keeps sums only of bytes; and a checkpoint whose measurement refuses publishes all the same, keeping no bytes (`contracts/tile-index-v1.md`, `contracts/overview-index-v1.md`), so such a session still reopens from it. No index is set aside for such a refusal. The window says the session's bytes could not be read, and why, and icat exits as for corrupted input, saying why. What stays untested here: guards against tile indexes, overviews and segments no writer makes - a negative size, a size missing without a reason, a malformed spanning section - the import paths that publish on Windows, and races between listing a folder and reading it.

Revision 712 runs whole what a coverage pass over the journal and follow code found no test ran (R10), and fixes what that found. A follow's compaction while it follows - due every 64 small publications of a long capture - ran in no test, nor did one that fails. A recording reports a compaction that fails and records on (ADR-026), but a follow let it end the follow: in the window a compaction that could not be published, as on a nearly full disk where writing its coalesced copy fails first, stopped the capture as interrupted, and icat capture and icat follow ended with an error although every chunk had been mirrored. Since this revision a follow's compaction that fails is reported and not tried again, as a recording's is: the follow mirrors every chunk on, its steps carry why, and the window's capture card, its finish of an interrupted capture, icat capture, icat follow and icat record say so in one wording, their documents naming it as `compactionFailure`. A follow ticket's renewal emptied the file before writing it, so a rewrite that failed part way left nothing a later launch reads as a ticket, where live-follow-v1 promised the earlier expiry; it now writes over the ticket in one write, a shorter one ending in spaces, and keeps its renewed expiry only once written, so the next renewal writes it again (`contracts/live-follow-v1.md` §1). InterCat's generated demo said, in icat session and icat rederive, that an earlier version had published it before plans were kept; it now says its rows were written as generated, beside records made to match them, so there is nothing to re-derive (`contracts/demo-investigation-v1.md` §3). The demo's source identity moves to the store, where the journal's readers can check it. Tests now also run a renewal on every platform - the one test that renewed a ticket stopped first on Linux, where a held ticket can be opened - a session folder not made yet, a ticket rewritten since it was found, a process image path cut to what a record holds, a status a source declares, and the discriminator each mechanism keys its rows by. What stays untested here: the window's capture card while it records, which no test follows through 64 publications; icat capture's summary, which runs on Windows only; and a ticket rewrite that fails part way.

Revision 713 keeps a capture recording through a moment when a file of its follow cannot be read or written (R10). A follow step that could not read or write a file - another command, such as icat pin or icat retain, publishing into the session at that moment, or a program holding a file just written, as a scanner or a backup can - ended the window's capture as interrupted and icat capture with an error, and in the window a view of a published generation that could not be read just now did the same. A follower moves on only past a chunk it published, so a pass that fails part way can be tried again as it was. Since this revision the follows of a capture still recording, the window's and icat capture's, try the pass again after pauses of 0.25, 0.5, 1 and 2 s (ADR-027), and one that outlasts them ends the capture as before, saying it failed each time it was tried; icat capture says each pause. A view that cannot be read is said on the capture card, and the one shown stays until a later generation's is read. Testing it found that a session opened while one of its files is held is read at its last-known-good generation, or refused when that generation needs the same file, as if the file were damaged: a store waiting out such a hold is left for a later revision. What stays untested here: icat capture's pauses and the capture card's words, which run on Windows only.

Revision 714 waits out a file of a session that another program holds for a moment (R10, `contracts/store-v1.md`). Revision 713 found that opening a session while one of the files its generation names is held - as a scanner or a backup can hold a file just written - treated the file as damaged: the session was read at its last-known-good generation, saying the newest did not verify, or refused when that generation needed the same file, and a capture whose window opened its evidence at that moment ended. A writer acquiring its own session the same way would find it read at a generation it may not publish over. Since this revision a dependency that is there but cannot be opened is waited for: the generation naming it is acquired again after pauses of 10, 20, 40, 80 and 160 ms, 0.31 s in all, before the last-known-good is, as a pointer met while it is replaced already was. A file that is missing is not waited for, and one held for longer is fallen back from as before, saying it could not be read. Each wait holds the store's lock, so a reader of the same store waits with it. What stays untested here: a manifest held the same way, which is still read as missing.

Revision 715 renders the capture card's statements of revisions 712 and 713 at the smallest window, 1080×700 (§6.8). The card's detail shows six lines, 84 pixels, and scrolls for the rest. Each of the new statements carries a reason the system gives, and on Windows that names the file it concerns by its whole path - a capture's session folder under the user's profile runs to about 100 characters before the file's own name, a 32-digit identifier among them - which wraps through four or five of those lines. So a compaction not published said that it was not published and then the reason, leaving that the session is whole below the lines shown, as a saved session's card did too; a view not read put its reason before the generation and that the view shown stays; and a capture ended by a file held through every pause showed only the file's path before it was scrolled. Since this revision the compaction's words and the unread view's say what they mean first and why last, in the window and from icat alike, and the card names a file within the session's own folder from that folder down, "…\journal\chunk-000017.icj", since the path line beneath names the folder whole: a capture's updates and an opened session's are read against the folder the card names. A path elsewhere, such as the broker's evidence folder, is named whole, and the reason's tool tip, on the retry button and the status strip, keeps it whole. At 1080×700 a saved session's card now says the session is whole, and that icat compact coalesces it, within its six lines, and an interrupted capture's says why and how often it was tried. What stays untested here: the runner's own calls that compose these words during a capture, which run on Windows only, and the card's other details - an export's, a package's or a pin's failure - which still name a path whole.

Revision 716 takes revision 714's wait to the pointer and the manifest (R10, `contracts/store-v1.md`), and found doing so that a publication could remove the generation it falls back to. A store read its pointers and manifests as documents that are there or not: one another program held for a moment - as a scanner or a backup can hold a file just written - read as missing. Opening a session whose manifest was held read it at its last-known-good generation, saying the manifest was missing, and a held pointer was read again after 5 and 10 ms before the same. Since this revision both are waited for as a held dependency is, after pauses of 10 to 160 ms, 0.31 s in all, and one held for longer gives the platform's reason. Worse, a publication writes the pointer to the generation it falls back to and, a moment later, reads it to learn which superseded manifests it may remove: held just then, the pointer named nothing, so the last-known-good's manifest went with the superseded ones, and with it the rollback the pointer was kept for. The sweep that reports a session's orphans, and removes them when asked, likewise called the files only that generation names unneeded. Now, while that pointer or the manifest it names is there but cannot be read, what that generation needs is not known: a publication removes no manifest until a later one reads it, opening reports no orphan, and `RemoveOrphans` removes none. A held file's reason no longer ends in two periods where a rollback is reported. What stays untested here: a hold on the real file system of Windows, where only a simulated one runs.

Revision 717 runs whole what a coverage pass over revisions 573 to 716 found no test ran (R10): who connected to whom, the ports processes accepted connections at, pairs of programs, and the window's lists of them. Of the 8,728 lines those revisions added that a test can reach, 204 ran in none.
