Most guard against what no writer makes, run only on Windows - icat capture's and icat record's own paths, the window's capture runner - or are a record's declaration. Eleven ran nowhere though a person reaches them, and each now runs: ranking the window's machine by connections made or accepted over a brushed interval, which reads the interval's connections beside its counts; a byte ranking's note of the bytes on records no process holds, said beneath the machine's rows and no other rung's; the card of Other processes, the least active processes folded together past the graph's budget; a double click on a process in an aggregate's list, which opens its rung, and on a relationship in a chosen process's list, which chooses the process at its other end, as revisions 599 and 667 said they do; a process rung's export ranked by bytes sent and received, which states what its rows rank by; a pair of programs no connection was seen opening between, as a screen reader hears it; a ranked export whose session cannot be read again to name its query; icat ports and icat programs given a folder that is not there; a live follow stopped while it pauses, which stops at once rather than when the pause ends; and a publication that cannot read the manifest of the generation it falls back to just as it reads what that generation needs, which removes no superseded manifest. None was wrong. What stays untested here: a lease naming a superseded manifest when the removal lock is held, which no lease can while it holds the evidence guard; ways down from a chosen row or list that fail part way; an export of a process's rung still being read after four waits; and a projection icat ports or icat programs is refused, which no session here reaches.

Revision 718 holds a journal's write and decode loops to R11 (§19.4), which until then only IC-019's Windows measurements spoke for. Measured here, following a session allocated 8.3 to 8.5 KB a record. Resolving a record searched its plan's descriptors with LINQ and its schema and policy tables with a lambda each, 344 bytes; reading its projection copied the body into a stream and a reader and checked its policy by building sets, 792 to 968 bytes; deriving its row hashed its fact key's name afresh, held the record's slots and measurement in objects of their own and looked its status up with a lambda, 1,656 bytes where the row is 392; and a record with source fields read its projection a second time for them. A replay read every frame, a megabyte for a full batch, into an array of its own; reading rows back boxed each of a row's dozen codes and split its descriptor's text, and writing a segment formatted that text for every row, though its comment said once per descriptor. Writing a record's envelope built a stream and a writer, 1,536 bytes where the envelope is 264. Now a plan indexes its descriptors by stream, provider, event and version when it is read, and a journal's tables index theirs by reference; a projection is read and written where it lies, and its policy checked without sets; a mechanism's fact key is hashed once; a record's projection is read once for its row and its field rows; a replay reads its frames into one pooled buffer; and a row's codes and descriptor are read back without allocating. Resolving and reading a record allocate nothing, deriving it only its row, name and field rows, writing it only its envelope, and reading a row back only the row, and tests hold each to that, warm, to the byte. Following the same session allocates 4.4 KB a record. Every output is byte for byte what it was: 3,000 records of seven descriptors, with names, addresses, identifiers, extended items and slots the record lost, went through writing, resolving, reading, deriving, publishing, rebuilding and reading back on the code before and after, and the two runs' outputs matched. The work found that a record two descriptors both select, one naming no opcode and one naming the record's, failed with an error that named no record and that a follow or a rebuild did not treat as damaged evidence; it is refused now as an interpretation the plan does not give (`normalizer-plan-v1`). What stays: a replay builds each record's envelope, about 450 bytes, some 190 of them its body's array, because the shared pool keeps far fewer than the 4,096 bodies a batch holds at once; compaction reads rows back whole to write them again; and the callback's own admission runs only on Windows.

Revision 719 derives every record a journal holds, or refuses its plan as data, rather than stopping at a row the segment refuses (R3, `normalizer-plan-v1`, `segment-v1`). Revision 718's equivalence probe, which left slots out of its records at random, found that a record without its declared status could not be derived at all: the row said its status was present, as the capability report did, the segment refused it, and the refusal was an error no follow, rebuild or import handles, so the derivation stopped at that record. The row's rules showed three more ways to the same stop: an eight-byte byte value of 2^63 or more, which a row would hold as a negative size; a status the report calls present that the descriptor did not admit; and a retained plan whose own codes or labels a row cannot carry. Now a status slot the record lost is lost (`EventLost`), as a byte slot's is; a status the report names but the descriptor did not admit carries the report's reason, `NotExposed` where it says present, as a byte field does; an eight-byte value of 2^63 or more is not decoded (`SchemaUnknown`) and keeps its slot's domain, side and unit; and a plan with a code §23 does not define, a byte slot without its domain or unit, a reported field said not to apply, or both IPv4 and IPv6 addresses is refused when it is read, naming the descriptor, before any record is. Of these a capture of this build reaches only the count: a file operation's completion carries an eight-byte field the catalog reads as completed bytes, and an operation other than a read or a write may leave another value in it. Its admission reads every numeric slot or refuses the record, and its catalog compiles none of those plans. No row derived before changes, since each of these records stopped its derivation.

Revision 720 rents an envelope's bytes from a pool that keeps a batch's worth of them (R9, R11, §18.1), which revision 718 left as what a replay still allocated. An envelope's body and extended items came from the shared pool, which keeps a few dozen arrays of a length per core; a journal batch holds 4,096 envelopes at once - a writer's pending, a replay's decoded - so the shared pool handed most of a batch's arrays out new and dropped them as the batch returned them. A batch's round trip of 8,192 buffers allocated every array. They now come from a pool of their own that keeps two batches' worth of each length up to 4 KiB, which every buffer an envelope holds fits - a projection of at most a kilobyte, an item of at most 64 bytes - and a longer one still comes from the shared pool. Warm, the same round trip allocates its 8,192 leases alone, which a test holds it to, and a replayed record allocates 306 bytes, its envelope's objects, where it allocated about 450. A capture's callback rents from the same pool, so a batch of records waiting in its queue rents its bodies again rather than allocating them; that path itself runs only on Windows, where IC-019 measures it. The pool keeps at most 8,192 arrays of each length, a few megabytes for the lengths a capture's projections and items take.

Revision 721 refuses as damaged evidence a sealed record that names a stream or an ordinal no admitted record has, or keeps more of an extended item than the item held (`normalizer-plan-v1`): what reading a record's path from the journal to its row after revision 719 found could still stop a derivation outright. Resolving such a record's descriptor and reading it back converted its stream and its ordinal with overflow checks, and handed its items to a copy that checks their lengths: a stream past 2^31, an ordinal past 2^63, or an item whose kept bytes outnumber its stated length failed with an overflow or an argument error, which no follow, rebuild or import treats as damaged evidence, so it stopped there instead of refusing the chunk and saying why. A capture of this build writes none of these, and a journal's frames are sealed, so only a journal written otherwise reaches them. Now each is refused as data, naming the record; a stream past every source's resolves to no descriptor, as one before the first does.

Revision 722 writes ADR-050, which §16 listed as owed: the palette's contrast and separation targets, why each is the number it is, and how `theme/` holds them (§6.6, R14, R20). Writing it found three things the tests did not hold. A target could be lowered and the report regenerated with every test passing, since each measurement was checked against the target the code stated. The committed files were held to the code only through their measured ratios, to two places, so a glyph, a label, a token's hex, a target or a summary row could change in the code while `theme/` kept the old one. And the summary showed for each check the measurement of least value, not the one nearest its own target: a high-contrast mode holds its fills to 4.5 to 1 and its divider to 3, so a fill failing at 4.4 could hide behind a divider passing at 3.2. Now the files' text is written by the theme's own code, which the report tool calls, with line feeds on every platform; a test compares the three committed files with it whole, another holds each target to ADR-050's value, and the summary shows each check's measurement nearest its own target. The committed files were already what the code writes, so none changed. The plan's family table gains the application family, which theme 1.4.0 introduced in revision 235 and revision 238 gave HTTP exchanges, and three comments that called the theme §21.2's item 9 now say item 11.

Revision 723 writes ADR-051, which §16 listed as owed: how fixtures are named, declared and traced, and what the checks hold (§13.5, P16, P27). §13.5 named fixtures for an `EN-Mechanism` code, yet five of the fourteen are named for the area they serve - IDENTITY, JOURNAL, QUERY, CONTENT; it put each declaration beside the fixture's data, yet every declaration is an entry of `fixtures/index.json`; and its second check, that milestone gates name only fixtures that exist, had nothing to read, since the gates name criteria. Nothing checked a fixture's `covers`: ten fixtures made 18 claims that none of their listed tests asserts, and one listed a test whose contract it did not claim. A fixture now covers exactly the contracts its listed tests are named for. The tests that read the fixtures' evidence but were not listed now are - the tier test for FX-TCP-001, and `icat verify`'s tests for the four transport fixtures - and a new test holds each transport fixture's committed evidence to the loopback flows its truth names (P16); together they back 10 of the claims. The other 8 are removed, and the 7 contracts that listed tests assert are claimed. The checks now also hold IDs well formed and unique, directories and entries one to one, milestones and backlog items to the plan's, and every fixture ID that code, tests, tools, contracts, ADRs, capability reports or bench results name to the index. That found `FX-IMPORT-001`, a local import probe holding machine data, which bench results of 2026-09-22 and 2026-09-23 name as their input and which was never committed or declared; it is retired with that reason, so its number is never given to another fixture. The checks are a component of the architecture tests that names each problem it finds, and a test hands it an index broken in each way it checks.

Revision 724 writes ADR-052, which §16 listed as owed: the broker believes the operating system about its client and nothing its client says (§20.3, R19, R22). Writing it from the code found two places where the plan and the contract said more than the broker does. §20.3 had the broker verify the client's integrity level and elevation against the owner, where it compares the user and the logon session: a lower token cannot open the pipe for writing under mandatory integrity, and an elevated one is in another logon session, so the plan now says what is compared and why. And the contract required a revert on every path, where only a failed read ended the broker when the revert itself failed; after a successful read the failure was an exception, which would have left the thread running as the client. A revert that fails now ends the broker on every path. The contract's paragraph promising prepared tokens, idempotent requests and durable ownership in the future tense now points to the section that implements them, and its statement that the runtime was only an interface with a fake names the runtime that drives real ETW. The ADR also says what the boundary lets through: a process of the capturing user in the same logon session, at medium integrity, passes the checks the window does while a broker serves. Two rows of §16's list of records not written were decided by records already written - the query identity by ADR-015, retention and pinning across ADR-010, ADR-024, ADR-043 to ADR-046 and ADR-048 - and are gone; an export's reserved disk allowance, which §20.2 also asks for, is not implemented. A test now holds the table and `docs/adr` to each other, numbered from ADR-001 without a gap, and every ADR the plan, the contracts, the code, the tests or the tools name to a record that exists.

Revision 725 writes ADR-053, which §16 listed as owed: the graph draws a bounded projection of one published graph, laid out by a deterministic, anchored force-directed pass (§6.3, §19.4, R7, R8, R12). The projection and the layout were built and measured over many revisions, and §6.3 and §19.4 describe them; the record says why this projection and this layout - force-directed rather than layered, seeds from hashes rather than a stored generator, anchors rather than a fresh layout on every publication, a separation stage after revision 641's wide machine, a budget of 100 after revision 643's measurement. §13.6 asks that a determinism claim be tested at worker counts 1, 2 and N and across repeat runs; the layout's tests held its output to the order of its inputs, but each ran on one thread. A test now lays out the same graph, fresh and as a settled refresh with every node drawn before and one pinned, twice on each of one, two and eight threads at once, and requires every position and the energy bit for bit; a seed that read the thread, which no earlier test would have noticed, fails it. Writing the record also bounded the claim: seeding and the parting of coincident nodes read the runtime's sine and cosine, whose last bits may differ between platforms, and 120 steps can carry that into a visibly different layout of unpinned nodes, so the determinism holds within one runtime on one processor architecture. An investigation carries its pins exactly, not the positions of nodes nobody pinned.

Revision 726 writes ADR-054, which §16 listed as owed: a finished generation publishes four indexes that answer exactly what its segments would, and the caches beneath them are budgeted (§10.2, §12.1 S1-S4, §19.3). The derivation checkpoint, the persisted overview, the tile index and the operation index each have a contract; the record says how they fit - published together by the same writers, each exact or unused, dependents of evidence released with its segments, each file bounded, tiles keeping common summaries and never a cube - and why: an index that could differ from a full derivation would be a defect nobody could locate, so each is a matter of speed only, which a test can hold. Writing it found one cache §19.3's rule does not fit. The rule budgets each cache by bytes, not entry count alone; the segment readers are budgeted so, but the derivations of the four most recently used generations are kept by count. A derivation grows with a session's processes and relations, not its records: at 10 million records of 400 processes, all the heap retained beyond the reader cache was about 35 MiB. §19.3 now names that exception and its basis, and the record notes that a machine of thousands of processes is unmeasured, as is the 100-million-record tier, both waiting on M5's disk.

Revision 727 writes ADR-055, which §16 listed as owed: every correlation states one of five strengths, which its rule names for each case it can meet (§7.4, §6.6, R14, P8). Each correlator's keys were fixed in its own record as it was measured - process binding, transport relations, RPC calls and their other ends, joins across captures - and the record states the model they share: the strengths are claims about the evidence, not scores; a reusable value or time proximity alone joins nothing; an evidence policy chooses what counts; strengths combine downward; and the canvas shows strength without hue. Writing it found two things. The canvas drew every strength weaker than correlated dotted, and ringed candidates and unresolved relations so neither reads as asserted, but not a conflicting one, which a policy admitting every strength draws: dotted without the ring, it read as more asserted than a candidate though its evidence disagrees with the join. It is ringed now. And §7.4 bounds pending joins by count, age and memory, with an eviction state, but every correlator derives over a generation's segments, so its open joins are bounded by that generation and the analysis memory budget; the count and age bounds and `EvictedUnresolved` wait for the first correlator that joins from a stream, which §7.4 now says.

Revision 728 writes ADR-056, which §16 listed as owed: content is kept only when a capture asks for it, read only when a person asks, and leaves the machine only in a file a person saves (§11, I21, R17, P2, P15, P16). Each part was decided as it was built - content as restricted evidence (ADR-036), WinINet's exchanges (ADR-037), the broker's own-process rule (ADR-049), the report, redacted session and original evidence package of revisions 104, 106 and 154, and the support bundle of revisions 391 and 392 - and the record states the policy they make together. §11.1 says the product has no automatic upload path; nothing held it. A test now requires that no product code opens a connection, sends a request, resolves a name or fetches symbols, and that product code is built only on reviewed packages, so a package added later is reviewed for what it sends. The record also lists what §11 asks that is not built: encryption at rest, a secure erase, session access narrower than its folder grants, and §19.5's name and symbol enrichment, which would be the first code to reach the network.

Revision 729 writes ADR-057, the last of §16's owed records that can be written now: a view reads one published generation at a time, asks only for its latest request, and keeps what it shows until the newer answer is read (§19.3, §6.2, R7, I16, P25). §19.3 suggested coalescing gesture requests over 30 to 50 ms, publishing live analytic snapshots at up to 4 Hz and ranking at up to 1 Hz, to be benchmarked. The window does otherwise, and §19.3 now says so: during a gesture it starts no query, but redraws the cells it holds at display rate and asks for the viewport's own count once the viewport has rested 150 ms, as §6.2 states; each kind of question holds one latest-request slot whose late answers are dropped; and a live view changes with each publication, at most every two seconds. The record also states what §19.3 asks that is not built: no question has priority over another, and the follower's derivation shares the thread pool with the window's queries, which matters once a background refinement exists. The one row of §16's owed records left is the byte accounting's canonical owner, which waits on per-transfer associations.

Revision 730 covers I17, which the contract ledger listed as uncovered and owed by IC-016: late evidence creates a new revision, and earlier snapshots stay reproducible under their own identities (§7.4, §13.2, §24). Every answer was computed over the store's current generation, under a lease it took and let go, so nothing could ask what an earlier generation said once a newer one was published, and nothing tested that it would say the same. A metric can now be answered over the generation a caller's lease holds. A test publishes a client's connection in one generation and the server's side of it in the next, recorded later in the stream though one record is older than the client's send. The newer generation is the new revision: the client's records now have their other end, and the answer and its identity differ. The generation held from before answers exactly as it did, under the same identity, with the same records unresolved; each record it read keeps its observation identity, segment and row in the newer one; and its manifest and every file it names are unchanged, byte for byte. A lease that has let go, by release or by its term, is refused, since retention may already have removed what it named, as is a lease of another session. The suites also found an allocation test that measured one thread's rents from the process-wide envelope pool while other tests in its assembly held thousands of its buffers; it now runs with the pool to itself.

Revision 731 covers P12, which the contract ledger listed as uncovered and owed by IC-011: no query, name resolution, symbol lookup, unpooled allocation or blocking I/O on the capture callback path (R9, §9.3). The callback's code was written to that rule - names copied into an inline buffer, content into pooled arrays, the clock checked once - but only a comment on the sink's interface said so, and ETW's callback cannot run off Windows. A test now walks the IL of everything the callback can run, from the admitter where ETW enters InterCat, through every implementation the broker is built with of the sink and the delivery observer: the capture's queue and its coverage tally. It reaches about 140 methods and none calls the store, the journal, a query, a file, the network, a name or symbol lookup, an event's decoded payload or names, or a wait. What it allocates outside the building of an exception, and the locks it takes, are each pinned to a reviewed site: the tally's entry for a descriptor, made once per descriptor up to the ledger's bound; the capture's clock, checked against its first record and a refusal said once; and the tally's lock, which a publication holds only while it reads the tally into a ledger. A second test drives the capture's sink with that tally through its queue: once the descriptors have been seen and the queue has grown to all it holds, as a burst grows it once in a capture, 4,096 rounds of every outcome a callback reports - a record admitted, one omitted, one undecodable - allocate nothing on the callback's thread. The admitter's own reads of an event cannot be run off Windows, so they are held by the walk alone.

Revision 732 covers P13, which the contract ledger listed as uncovered and owed by IC-009: never silently disable a provider, switch to sampling or change an effective profile without opening a new coverage epoch (§9.3). Nothing can change a running capture's configuration, which is why a live capture's ledger states one epoch, but nothing held it. Three tests now do. One finds, in the IL of everything the broker and the recorders are built from, every call to TraceEvent's session controls and to InterCat's owned sessions: a provider is enabled in one place and kernel flags in another, each reached only through an owned session, which enables a provider or asks for its state only while its capture starts; a session's settings are set only as it is created, or as a dead capture's session is reclaimed to be stopped; and nothing disables a provider. Because that walk loads only the capture's own assemblies, a second test holds that no other project's source names TraceEvent's session type or a call that enables or disables a provider. The third records a capture whose second provider its session refuses: the capture starts, says which provider was refused and why, and publishes one epoch that counts only the enabled provider's descriptors collected, so the refused one's mechanism reads as not collected rather than as covered and quiet. Sampling and adaptive profiles do not exist; the first change to a running capture opens a second epoch, which `coverage-v2` already allows.

Revision 733 extends a live recording's HTTP exchanges from the generation before it, as revision 544 extends its RPC calls (P25, I14, `contracts/http-exchanges-v1.md` §2a). A live generation grouped its exchanges from every WinINet record it named, so a window following a capture grouped the whole session again at each publication that its HTTP listing, a process's HTTP rows or their lane asked about. Grouping a number's buffers into exchanges needs every buffer of the number in order, and a number's next buffer can come in a later chunk, or later still as a late record. A grouping now keeps, beside its exchanges, each number's last use whose response has not ended or whose last buffer reads within 15 seconds of the latest buffer read, with whether that use holds every buffer of its number, and every number it has read. A generation whose segments and source fields begin with the files the earlier one read reads only those it adds: a number its added buffers name is told apart again from its kept last use on, or from nothing when no buffer named it before, and every other exchange stands, bound again to the generation's instances by its first buffer's reading. Whatever an extension could not add exactly is grouped in full: a buffer of a number read before and no longer kept, a buffer read before a split use's first, a segment the generation no longer names as the earlier one read it, and a source field naming no HTTP record the generation added. A property test cuts random WinINet captures - numbers reused, buffers lost, request bodies finished after their responses, records delivered a chunk or two late and a few far past the window - into chunks, and holds every extension to a grouping of every chunk at once; others hold each decline, and a live capture recorded in four chunks to an extension at each later generation. What an extension keeps is held in memory, not published, so a process that opens a live generation with nothing grouped before it, such as a command against a session still being recorded, still groups from every record.

Revision 734 answers the whole session's ranking by connections made or accepted without reading a row (§12.1 S3, P25, `contracts/relations-v1.md` §5). The connections each process made and accepted already came from the relations, but the ranking also reads the TCP records behind them, and over the whole session it read every segment for them: a finished session's every time it was asked, and a window following a capture at each publication, since a new generation's derivation binds every row again. A process's TCP records are its own TCP records that bind to it as the evidence policy admits, and the derivation's activity (`process-activity-v1`, revision 166) counts exactly these, by the same binding, for every instance and mechanism: kept with a finished session's checkpoint and extended from one live generation to the next. The whole session's ranking now reads them there, and reads rows only for a time scope, from the segments it meets. At a million rows in ten segments it takes 0.6 to 3.3 ms, where reading the rows took 56 ms at each new generation and 7 ms after; a reopened session answers in 16 ms opening no segment. A property test holds the counts to a pass binding every row of random captures, PIDs reused, under every policy, and the activity's count of one mechanism to its rows, lifecycle records among them. The whole session's ranking by peers still reads every segment, which a later revision keeps with the persisted overview.

Revision 735 answers a finished session's ranking by peers without reading a row (§12.1 S3, P25, `contracts/overview-index-v1.md` minor 7). A process's peers are the distinct instances at the other end of its records, beside the records whose other end is unknown, and a group's are counted the same over its members; they rest on the evidence policy, the grouping and the processes a view sets aside, so no count of them could be kept for every view, and the whole session's ranking by peers read every segment, a finished session's included. What is kept instead is how the records bind: for every record but a lifecycle record, its owner's binding and its other end's, each an instance at a strength or none, with how many records bind so. A record's part in a count of peers rests on those two bindings alone, so applying a policy, a grouping and the processes set aside to the kept pairs counts exactly what reading every segment does. A finished session's publication counts them in one more pass and keeps them with its overview, unless there are more than 50,000 pairs; a reader uses them for the whole session when its checkpoint holds every instance they name, and reads the segments otherwise, and for a time scope. At a million rows, 600 pairs, a reopened session ranks by peers in 44 ms the first time and 1.4 ms after, opening no segment, where reading its rows took 391 ms and 99 ms. A property test holds the answer to a read of every segment of random captures under every policy, with and without processes set aside; others hold the file's refusals, an older overview's falling back to the segments, and bindings naming an instance the checkpoint lacks. The connection ranking's tests, which clear the shared derivation cache, now run in the collection that holds it. A live generation's whole-session byte and peer rankings still read every segment.

Revision 736 lists a whole session's connections to other hosts without reading a row (§12.1 S3, P25, `contracts/derivation-checkpoint-v1.md` format 1.4, `contracts/relations-v1.md` §5b). A one-sided connection's summary - its sends and receives, the bytes they state and how many state none - was counted from the rows of every segment its lifetime met, so a process's connections, Other hosts ranked by bytes, the bytes on each edge to other hosts, `icat channels --one-sided` and an investigation's connections read rows even for the whole of a finished session. The relation derivation already reads each of those records once, to place it in its incarnation; it now counts there what the record sent or received and the size it states, and refuses a sum past what one holds exactly as a pass over the rows refuses it. The counts extend with the relations from one live generation to the next, and the checkpoint keeps them after every other section, ending with how many it holds, so format 1.3 is format 1.4 without them. Relations read from a 1.3 checkpoint keep none, nor once extended, so their connections read the rows, and the next publication writes 1.4. A time scope still reads its rows. At a million rows, a thousand hosts reached by 200 processes, a reopened session ranks Other hosts by bytes in 36 ms the first time and 9 ms after, opening no segment, where reading its rows took 332 ms and 38 ms; a process's connections take 1.5 ms where its rows took 9. A property test holds every one-sided connection's counts to a pass over its rows, over random captures whose sizes are now and then left unstated, whether the relations were derived whole, extended chunk by chunk or read back from a checkpoint; others hold a refused sum through a checkpoint and an extension, a checkpoint's contradictory counts refused, and a reopened session's listings the same from its checkpoint, from an older one's rows, and once published again.

Revision 737 answers a live generation's whole-session byte and peer rankings from what its derivation counted (§12.1 S3, P25, I14, `contracts/relations-v1.md` §5, `contracts/entities-v1.md` §4a). A finished session's persisted overview keeps its bytes before any policy and how its records bind at their two ends (revisions 163 and 735), but a live generation has no such overview, so it read every segment again for them - on every publication while a selection or the tables needed bytes. The activity now counts every record by the instance it binds to and how strongly, with the transport bytes it measured as a byte view measures them, and each incarnation counts its own records the same way. Both extend with their derivations, each instance's counts moved as its records' binding moves, and an extension now holds every PID an incarnation's records name to binding alike. From them the kept bytes and peer bindings of a persisted overview are sums - each instance's bytes at each strength, each end of every paired channel from its two incarnations, each incarnation's records moved to the other end they all have - and the rankings read them as they read a persisted overview's. A record a byte view refuses leaves the bytes to the rows, which say why. The process, relation and count extensions now tell which segments they read by the identities the generation records, so an extension opens only the segments it adds where it opened every one. At a million rows in ten live chunks, a new generation's byte ranking took 104 ms, the extension over its chunk included, opening that chunk's one segment; its byte and peer rankings then took 2.1 ms each, where reading the rows took 218 ms and 332 ms. A property test holds the counted bytes and bindings to a pass over every segment of random captures, derived whole and extended chunk by chunk; others hold a live generation's rankings to its rows', counts read back from a checkpoint keeping none, and refused bytes left to the rows. A derivation read back from a checkpoint keeps no such counts, so a generation opened from one still reads every segment for them.

Revision 738 keeps the counts of records by owner in the derivation checkpoint (§12.1 S3, P25, I14, `contracts/derivation-checkpoint-v1.md` format 1.5). Revision 737's counts lived in memory alone, so a derivation read back from a checkpoint kept none: a viewer opening a session another process was recording - whose every generation after its first names a checkpoint of the generation before - read every segment for its byte and peer rankings at every publication. The checkpoint now keeps, after the transfers, each incarnation's records by owner with the PIDs it names when it names more than one, and each instance's, ending with the bytes they take, so format 1.4 is the same bytes cut short. They are written in canonical order, so a derivation made at once and one extended write the same bytes, which the checkpoint's own property test holds; a reader refuses records by owner that are not their incarnation's, name no instance or another, or are out of order, bytes below none or beside a refusal, PIDs that contradict whether an incarnation names two, and a section that does not take the bytes it says. A checkpoint of format 1.4 is read without them and replaced at the next publication. Staging a live generation's checkpoint now opens a segment only where the derivation still has to read it. At a million rows recorded in ten chunks, a viewer opening the newest generation ranked by bytes in 90 to 133 ms, opening one segment, where the rows took 176 to 386 ms, and by peers in 16 to 24 ms where the rows took 139 to 158, with the same answers. The counts cost room: they made the checkpoint a third to two fifths larger, 110 to 230 bytes for each incarnation holding records with the instances' own (3.4 MB at 10,200 incarnations, read in 80 ms and written in 12 ms), which a live recording writes at every generation.

Revision 739 keeps a live session's tiles as it is recorded (§12.1 S1, S4, P25; `contracts/tile-index-v1.md` §6, store-v1 §5). S4 asks for the overview pyramid to be updated incrementally at commit, but a generation names an index only if it published it, and a live recording's tiles grow with its records, so no publication could write them afresh: a session opened while it was still being recorded counted its overview from tiles built from every segment's rows, 0.16 to 0.2 s of a 4,000,000-record open by revision 710's measure and in proportion to the session, and its first zoom of the whole extent did again. Each live publication now also stages the tiles of the chunk the generation it extends added: a tile index of that chunk's observation segments, named for the chunk and the publication, which binds no record, since a later derivation binds them otherwise, and so serves only the counts no binding changes. Unlike every other index it is carried, by each later generation that names the chunk's segments, and a release gives it up only with them. It decides no generation: acquiring one passes over it, its reader checks every part it reads and reads rows where it cannot, and a commit drops one that no longer measures as recorded and tiles the chunk again later - otherwise a damaged one would send a reader back past every generation since. A publication also tiles up to three older chunks no tiles describe, newest first, within a million rows, so a session recorded before this revision, or compacted, is tiled as it goes on. The overview of a generation without a persisted one, and its zooms' whole timeline, count each chunk-tiled segment from its section and the newest chunk's from its rows, and the overview now hands its derivation segments opened as they are read, as other queries have since revision 737, so a cold open of a live session reads only its newest chunk. At 1,000,000 records in ten chunks the tiles took 7.4% of the segments' bytes and tiling a chunk 34 to 94 ms of a publication; a cold open's overview counted in 7 ms opening one segment, where every segment's rows took 42 ms and opened ten, and a zoom of the whole extent at 256 columns took 17 ms. A live generation's brushes, focuses and zooms' byte lanes still read the rows of the segments their interval meets, since a chunk's tiles bind no record, and its operations are still derived from every segment when it is opened with nothing derived before it.

Revision 740 runs whole what a coverage pass over revisions 718 to 739 found no test ran (R10), and fixes what that found. Of the 1,762 lines those revisions added that a test can reach, 61 ran in none. Most guard against what no derivation makes - a channel end's bytes, or those of the records no instance holds, summing past what a sum holds, which an owner's own sum always passes first; a relation of a mechanism the transport rule does not read; an instance no record binds to - or run only on Windows, as the ETL import's one list of source fields reused record by record does. Five ran nowhere though a session reaches them, and each now runs: a live follow stopped while it stages a publication's checkpoint and chunks' tiles, which gives up what it staged for a cleanup to remove; a zoom of a live session's whole timeline over a chunk whose readings go backwards, which reads that chunk's rows; a metric asked of a folder in which no generation was published, which says so; an evidence policy none of the four, which every count by policy refuses; and a checkpoint giving records by owner to an instance it counts no records of. That last was read and used. A checkpoint could count no records for an instance, and since revision 738 could give an instance records by owner other than those it counted for it, at a strength the instance binds no record at, or none, so a generation opened from it could rank by bytes or by peers otherwise than by records. Every instance is witnessed by a record that binds to it, so every instance has records counted and records by owner, and these are the counted ones, lifecycle records apart: as many binding directly as are counted directly, and the rest at the strength the instance binds them at (identity-v1). Since this revision a checkpoint that says otherwise is refused, as its other contradictions are, and the generation is derived from its rows (`contracts/derivation-checkpoint-v1.md` §3). An owner's bytes past what a sum holds, which leave a byte ranking to the rows, also have a test of their own now, beside a byte view's refusal. Of the 1,774 lines a test can reach since, 30 run in none: 26 guards no derivation reaches and the four Windows lines. The suites found one window test failing now and then: ranking by bytes a session whose sizes add up past what a sum holds sometimes left its note saying it was reading the bytes after the ranking had finished. The ranking and the inspector's description read the session's bytes through one read, shared while it runs and once it has answered (revision 619), but a read that failed before the ranking noted why was read again for the description, which left the ranking's read superseded with nothing to say and read the bytes twice. Since this revision a read that failed is shared too until its failure is noted, and one noted is read afresh when asked for, as before. What stays untested here: those guards, and the ETL import's list of source fields, which runs only on Windows.

Revision 741 lets a session's first view in the window draw without waiting for its RPC calls to be followed (§12.1 S3, P25). A capture that collected ALPC draws an edge between a process and the process that served its calls, and a finished session keeps those links with its persisted overview (revision 229), but a session still being recorded keeps no summary of them: a viewer opening it cold paired every call and followed every ALPC message over every segment before it drew anything, the one whole-session scan left in that first view once revisions 710, 737 and 739 took its processes, rankings and counts from what each generation stages. The first plan was to stage the operations too: a live operation index of the generation before, with what a later generation can still change - the calls' kept keys and records, every ALPC record the other ends were linked from, the exchanges' open last uses and every number read - which a viewer extends over the newest chunk as the window extends them between generations. It was built and measured, and set aside. At 200,000 records holding 25,000 calls (a Debug build in a container), the index of the generation before took 9.4 MiB, and each two-second publication spent 400 to 500 ms more: 155 ms extending the operations a chunk in memory, which `icat capture` and `icat follow` would do for it alone, and 260 ms writing and hashing the index. It grows with the session, since every call and every ALPC record is in it and the late window keeps every recent record, so a busy capture outgrows any bound worth writing every two seconds within minutes; and the cold open it buys took 548 ms where following took 917, since reading, binding and linking every call remains. What would scale keeps what can no longer change once, chunk by chunk, and pairs and links again only what added records can change. This revision does what S3 asks of what no summary serves: it is progressive and never blocks. The window projects a session's first view without following its calls where the generation keeps no links and none are followed yet: its graph draws the rest and says, in its summary, its full scope and beneath an empty graph, that which process called which over RPC is still being followed through ALPC. Off the window's thread the generation is projected again, following them, and the view gains their edges while keeping its rung, selection, pins and interval, as a newer publication of it does. Revision 227 had followed them before the first view because a graph that grows edges after it is drawn would move under the reader; a newer view of the same session now keeps every node still drawn where the reader last saw it, so the edges join without moving them, as a live capture's publications add edges every two seconds. A policy chosen meanwhile is counted from the same following rather than a second one, a following that fails says why and leaves the other relationships drawn, and opening another session or starting a capture stops it. `icat` still follows the calls before it answers, and the overview `icat overview --json` prints says so (`RpcCallsPending` false). What stays slow is the following itself: a cold open of a long live session still pairs and links every call before its RPC views answer.

Revision 742 extends RPC calls and their other ends between live generations at a cost that follows what a generation added rather than every call (§12.1, I14, P25; `operations-v1` §4 and §5c). Since revision 544 an extension read only the added segments and paired again only the keys a late record can touch, but then merged every standing call with those paired again by comparing first records, bound every call again, grouped every call through a dictionary of its group's identity, sorted every group's durations, gathered and sorted every server call's start, and linked every client call again: at 800,000 calls an extension by 60,000 records took 0.45 s for the calls and 0.38 s for their other ends in a Release build, in proportion to the session. A call index now ranks its calls from 0 by their first records' canonical order, and keeps beside them the ranks of the calls its kept records made, which a later generation withdraws, and each group's durations, sorted. An extension finds where each call paired again falls among the standing calls by comparing first records only there, galloping from where the last one fell, and so has every call's rank from its rank before, the calls withdrawn before it and the calls paired again before it. Where every reading a group's calls bind by binds to the generation's instances as it did - to the same instance, as strongly, or to none for the same reason, which a group's identity holds - each group's standing calls are carried in their order, their instance renumbered where an earlier PID's instance was added, the calls paired again dealt in by rank and the group's durations merged rather than sorted again; where one could bind otherwise, as a rundown or a process start read late can make it, every call is bound and grouped again as a derivation groups it. The index extended says where each call of the one it extended went, and the other ends extended with it carry the server calls' starts and the standing links to those places, merge the added ALPC records into those read in runs between where each falls, and keep what a standing client call reached when it stopped no later than every record added and every server call's start the rule now reads differently. A server call held open and paired again each generation keeps its start, interface and procedure, so it changes nothing the rule reads; only the other client calls are linked again, and a server call two client calls reach is neither's, counted over them all as before. Random live captures in which a client exits and its PID is used again, another first calls halfway through, server calls stay open for chunks and two clients reach one server call extend, chunk by chunk, to exactly what a derivation of every chunk gives, and write the same operation index; a call made after its process exited is grouped again when its PID is used again, though it stands, and a server call freed by a send read late serves the client left, whose call stands. At 800,000 calls the same extension now takes 0.16 to 0.32 s for the calls and 0.07 s for their other ends, measured as a live follow's generations run, reusing memory the generation before freed. What still grows with the session is copying the calls, 144 bytes each, and their links forward: this container's virtual machine charges up to 28 µs for each page of memory a process first touches, which is the range above, and a layout of blocks a generation could share with the one before would avoid even that.

Revision 743 lets a window follow a session that another process records (§6.4, §19.3, ADR-057; `live-follow-v1` §1). Someone who records in one window, or with `icat capture`, and looks in another window had seen the generation that window read shown as a saved session: "Saved session open", a package offered, "No coverage ledger · loss unknown", and nothing the capture published after it. The plan's live view rules covered a window following its own capture and a session that has finished; they said nothing of a session still recorded elsewhere, and §6.4 now does. The ticket a running follow holds beside its session, shared only for reading, says whether a capture still records into it: no ticket, one held, or one left by a follow that ended early (`LiveFollowTicket.StateOf`). A window that opens a session whose ticket is held follows it as it follows its own capture. The card says it is still being recorded and that the view follows it, and the health strip reads "Recording elsewhere", following live, paused or held while records are read. Pause view and the held banner act as for its own capture, the loss is stated once recording stops, and a package waits for the recording to end. The window looks for a newer generation every two seconds, the shortest interval a live capture publishes at, and leases and projects it off the window's thread. A look that fails is tried again at the next, and one that finds nothing newer leaves the card as it is, so where an export was written stays said. Once the follow lets its ticket go, the newest generation, published or held while the view was paused, is the session as saved. It is shown unless records are being read, and the card says the recording ended, or that the capture stopped before the session held all it published, which the card offering to finish an interrupted capture then offers. Opening another session, starting a capture or closing the window stops the following. The window tests stand in for the follow's state, because only Windows enforces a ticket's sharing and the tests hold on any system; a test of the ticket itself holds that one held reads as running on Windows.

Revision 744 lets a live session's owner focus count a chunk from the tiles a live follow keeps of it (`tile-index-v1` §6, minor 8; S4, P25). Since revision 739 a chunk's tiles bound no record, so they served a live generation's overview and its zooms' whole timeline, and every focus, brush and byte lane read the rows of the segments its interval met: a group's lanes over a live session of 1,000,000 records in ten chunks opened all ten chunks' segments. A chunk's tiles now bind its records' owners under the derivation of the generation that tiles them, and certify each: for each owner key, PID and kind, the instance's identity and the first and last native reading of its records. A later derivation counts a chunk's owners from them only where it binds every owner's records as they were bound: to the instance of the same identity and PID, at every reading from the first to the last, as strongly, a lifecycle record directly; or, for records bound to none, to none still. A PID's binding changes only where one of its lifetimes starts or ends, and lifetimes are half-open, so that is decided from the derivation's instances alone, at an owner's first reading and wherever a lifetime of its PID starts or ends among its readings, once for each chunk a generation's queries read. Where every owner binds alike, the tiles' owner keys, a tally's, a byte tally's or a record's, are read as the counting derivation's instances; where one does not, as when a lifecycle record read late ends an instance, or begins a PID's next one, among a chunk's readings, the chunk's segments are read from their rows, and the answer is the same. A group's lanes, one process's with its direction rows, a group's byte lanes and a process's byte direction rows so count every chunk but the newest from its tiles, and a zoom's byte lanes add up their byte sums, which bind nothing; a cold open's focus opens only the newest chunk's segments. A directory that certifies its owners out of order, twice, or as no derivation of its instances binds them, and tiles that keep an owner key it does not certify, are refused as data. A brush still reads rows, since a chunk's tiles bind no channel. On traffic in bursts a group's lanes took 28 to 33 ms warm at 1,000,000 records, where the rows took 38 to 49, and 88 ms cold where they took 195; where each record is another process's than the one before, which no narrow tile tallies, the tiles' records are read, and the lanes took 88 to 100 ms warm where the rows took 51 to 59. Binding and certifying the owners made tiling a chunk of 100,000 records take 133 to 154 ms, from 34 to 94, and the tiles 8.0% of the segments' bytes, from 7.4%.

Revision 745 lets a live session's brush count a chunk from the tiles a live follow keeps of it (`tile-index-v1` §6, minor 9; S4, P25). Since revision 744 a chunk's tiles bound their records' owners but no channel, since a channel's number is its place among every channel the session's relations hold, which a later generation renumbers; a brush, which counts channels, read the rows of every segment its interval met. A chunk's tiles now key each record's channel by the end it was made at instead: by the span of that end's records within one of its incarnations under the derivation that tiles them, numbered as the chunk's records first meet them, and their directory lists each span - its end's protocol, family, own and remote address and port, and the place in the canonical order of its first and last record - as many as the channels their derivation states. A later derivation finds each span's channel among its own: an end's connects, accepts and disconnects divide it into incarnations, and where the incarnation holding a span's first record holds its last, every record of the span belongs to it, and so to its channel, or to none while its other end is undecided. Where every span holds and every owner binds alike, the tiles' channel keys, a tally's, a byte tally's or a record's, are read as those channels, and a brush counts the chunk's segments from them, its counts and its bytes by process and channel end; where a lifecycle record read later falls among a span's records, or a record read later binds an owner otherwise, the chunk's segments are read from their rows, and the answer is the same. A cold open's brush opens only the newest chunk's segments. A directory that lists an end span no derivation gathers, more or fewer than it holds, or another number than the channels its derivation states, is refused as data. At 1,000,000 records in ten chunks a brush over the whole extent took 6 to 9 ms warm and 27 to 33 cold, where every segment's rows took 8 to 10 and 43 to 52, and its bytes 17 to 18 ms warm where the rows took 37 to 56. Naming the ends made staging a chunk's tiles take 200 to 248 ms where each record is another process's than the one before, from 133 to 154, and the tiles 9.0% of the segments' bytes, from 8.0%. A live channel focus still reads rows.

Revision 746 lets a live session's channel focus count a chunk from the tiles a live follow keeps of it (`tile-index-v1` §6; S4, P25). Since revision 745 a chunk's tiles name each record's end by the span of that end's records, which a later derivation maps to its own channel, and a live brush counted from them; a channel's focus - its records, at both ends or at one, of one mechanism or source direction - still read the rows of every segment its interval met. It now counts each chunk from its tiles wherever the counting derivation's relations hold every span of the chunk's ends within one incarnation, whatever the chunk's owners bind to, since it counts no owner, and from its rows otherwise, as an owner focus counts a chunk where its owners bind alike; the answer is the same, and a cold open's channel focus opens only the newest chunk's segments. With it every query a finished generation counts from its tile index - the overview, a zoom's timeline and byte lanes, an owner's or a channel's focus, a brush and its bytes - a live generation counts from its chunks' tiles, reading the rows only of its newest chunk, of a chunk whose tiles are gone, and of one a record read later binds otherwise, so incremental updates at commit are done. On traffic in bursts a channel's focus over 1,000,000 records in ten chunks took 27 to 30 ms warm and 97 cold, where every segment's rows took 49 to 58 and 208. Where each record is another pair's than the one before, no narrow tile keeps channel tallies, and a chunk of one segment keeps no level spanning it, so its tiles' records are read: 135 to 146 ms warm, where the rows took 74 to 85 and a finished session's tile index, which counts its spanning level's tallies, 11 to 16. A level spanning each chunk's own sections would close that gap.

Revision 747 measures a live session's chunk-tile queries at steady state (`tile-index-v1` §6; S4, P25). Revisions 744 to 746 gave as warm a query's second and third run, and on traffic where each record is another pair's than the one before, a live channel's focus over 1,000,000 records seemed to take 135 to 146 ms where the rows took 74 to 85. Run ten times, it took 36 to 40 ms over the sixth to tenth runs, where the rows took 24 to 31: the runtime compiles a method's optimized code only after it has run it, and the earlier runs had paid for that. Measured so, at 1,000,000 records in ten chunks, whose segments all stay held in memory, a live focus took up to two and a half times what the rows did and a brush and its bytes no more than they did; at 4,000,000 records in forty chunks, on traffic in bursts, a channel's focus took 30 to 43 ms where every segment's rows took 95 to 105, a group's lanes 23 to 30 where they took 39 to 65, a brush's bytes 38 to 58 where they took 285 to 319, and the first channel focus after opening 120 ms where the rows took 1.2 s; where each record is another pair's than the one before, a focus took up to twice what the rows did, all forty segments held, and a brush and its bytes less. A level spanning a chunk's one section, the remedy revision 746 proposed, was built and measured and set aside: a spanning tile keeps its tallies only within a third of its records' bytes, as a section's does, so on such traffic it keeps none and leaves every record to be read. The contract's figures are restated; nothing else changed.

Revision 748 corrects stale statements of S4's state. §12.1's paragraph said the current implementation recomputes the minimap level with each generation's overview rather than persisting it, and that S4's pyramid remained open; a finished generation's overview index has persisted the main and minimap columns since revision 163, its tile index the deeper levels since revision 456, and since revisions 739 to 746 a live generation counts every level from its chunks' tiles but for its newest chunk. The same paragraph said a live generation still built the finished one's levels; IC-017's open gap listed a live generation's levels; and the metric selector's paragraph said a live session's byte lanes were still read when asked for. Each now says what holds, and what S4 still owes: the pyramid's bounded open measured at T3, where a live session counts one section for each of its compacted segments.

Revision 749 runs whole what a coverage pass over revisions 741 to 748 found no test ran (R10). Of the 1,430 lines those revisions added that a test can reach, 49 ran in none. The overview and the zooms have read around a chunk's tiles whose section cannot be read under test since revision 739; the queries revisions 744 to 746 count from those tiles read around them too, untested until now. A brush and its bytes, an owner's and a channel's focus, a group's byte lanes and every mechanism's bytes each read the chunk's rows when a damaged section's header is refused, and a test holds each to the answer of a session recorded without tiles. Gathering a chunk's end spans refuses a row naming an end the gathering derivation does not hold, since it gathers only the rows it read; each comparison of a record's place in the canonical order says what comparing says; and a segment's owners asked into buffers a row short are refused. The window let go, untested, of a look at a recording elsewhere still under way when another session was opened: a look that finds the recording ended would otherwise have said, on the other session's card, that its recording had ended. Two pieces of code went: a count of end spans nothing read, and a search's handling of equal places among a generation's withdrawn calls, whose places are distinct, since each call has a place of its own. Of the 1,422 lines a test can reach since, 14 run in none: a ticket held by a running follow, which only Windows detects (Linux's file sharing is advisory), and four guards in the RPC extension (revision 742) that its construction never reaches. A standing call's instance is mapped wherever its readings fall, since the two derivations are compared at every lifetime boundary between its process's first and last call; two groups cannot come to one, since that mapping is one to one; a withdrawn server call is always paired again from its own start record, which fixes what the peer rule reads of it; and two server starts read alike keep their order, since their calls' groups do. Each would answer rightly if reached - by binding again, by linking again from the call's start, or by sorting again - so they stay as guards.

Revision 750 has a compaction copy each segment's columns as they are rather than read its rows back whole (R11, §20.1). The status listed it under R11 beyond paint and aggregation as what stays. A live compaction step rewrites up to a segment's 250,000 rows, and it materialized each as a row object of some 380 bytes, then wrote it again column by column: measured with the sessions in memory, so no disk stood in the way, a step of 250,000 rows from 260 publications took 1.4 to 1.7 s and allocated 1.5 KB per row, 355 MiB, holding the recording's writer for that long. A segment's plain columns are stored as a builder stages them, a null as zero, so a copy takes each column's bytes and its rows' presence as they are, interns a schema or a name once for each code that names it, and reads each row's place in canonical order from its columns. It cuts its segments where writing the rows one by one would, before the row that would reach a segment's rows or staged bytes, counting each row's name and whether it widens the segment to observation-v2, so what it publishes is byte for byte the same: a randomized test of both tables, three schemas, names pooled, unique and absent, IPv4 and IPv6 ends, fields of numbers and of text and segments cut by rows and by bytes holds every file to a twin compacted by writing each row again, and to the rows as recorded; a segment whose names passed a dictionary's bytes, kept in its chunk, is copied so too. The step took 0.7 to 1.0 s and 1.1 KB per row, 270 MiB. Building a segment, which every publication does, sorts its rows' places with their keys through a comparer rather than a delegate and writes each column with its encoding chosen once, its schema codes mapped once, rather than row by row; the bytes are the ones the golden segments hold. What a step still costs is mostly hashing each segment twice, for its own trailer and for the manifest, and making it durable, which on this machine's processor, about 300 MB/s for SHA-256, and disk is most of the rest.

Revision 751 keeps what queries read of every chunk's tiles in one byte budget, so a live focus on a busy machine's traffic reads each part once (`tile-index-v1` §4 and §6; S4, S2). Revision 747 measured that where each record is another pair's than the one before, which no narrow tile tallies, a live focus took up to twice what every segment's rows did. A profile of one process's focus at 1,000,000 such records in ten chunks found 52% of its time decoding blocks, each read again from the file: a chunk of 100,000 records spaced so each finest tile holds ten has some 350 blocks across its levels, each chunk's tiles kept 64, and a full cache was cleared, so every focus read every block again. With the blocks kept, the reads of each block's owner tallies, 46%, and records, 13%, came next, one read a block for every query, and then the lock a shared cache took for every block, 33%. What queries read of a tile index - its blocks decoded, and their tallies, byte sums and records as read - is now kept for the queries after them within 64 MiB charged in bytes, §19.3's rule for a cache, which every chunk's tiles share and a generation's own tile index has apart, read without a lock and written under one; past the budget what a query reads takes the place of a part chosen at random, which on a scan half again as large as the cache finds a third to a half of it again where clearing it, or giving up what was read least recently, finds none, and a query opens a tile file only to read a part it does not find kept. Each tile's records are still checked at every read. Over 4,000,000 such records in forty chunks, the tiles keeping 60 MiB, a channel's focus took 61 to 87 ms where every segment's rows took 122 to 214 and the tiles had taken 92 to 112, one process's 52 to 73 and a group's lanes 52 to 90 where the rows took 30 to 77 and 48 to 84 and the tiles had taken 77 to 107 and 64 to 82, and a brush's bytes 25 to 53 where the rows took 104 to 333; on traffic in bursts nothing changed. At 1,000,000 records, whose ten segments the rows hold in memory, an owner focus still takes up to twice what the rows do, 17 to 21 ms where they take 10 to 18, from up to two and a half times: what remains is decoding each packed record, against columns already read, which keeping a chunk's records decoded would spend about three times their bytes to save.

Revision 752 begins M12 with a comparison of two intervals of one session, row by row (`contracts/comparison-v1.md`, ADR-058, `docs/reviews/M12-release-review.md`; plan M12, §15.2, R3, R21, I16, P1). Two windows of one session come first because they need no entity mapping: a process instance and a relationship keep their identity throughout. A row is a relationship between two processes, as the overview draws it, or one process's connections over one transport to one other host's address, as the census counts them: the first try compared relationships alone and found nothing to compare on the demo's client, whose every connection runs to another host, as most of a busy machine's do. Each value's rate is over its whole interval (`metrics-v1` §7), and two rates are compared in integers - the compared value times the baseline's length against the baseline value times the compared length - so intervals of different lengths compare without rounding. A change is stated only where both intervals covered the row's mechanism, RPC calls' ALPC too, and every epoch either spans collected it through the same descriptors, which `SessionCoverage.CollectedOtherwise` reads from the ledger: two covered epochs can still collect different records of one mechanism, and only their descriptors show it. Elsewhere both values stand with the first reason that applies. Bytes add the sends that measured a size, those without one counted apart as a lower bound, which states a change only where it alone decides it. A ledger's loss count was considered as a bound per row, since normalizer-v1 derives one row per record, and set aside: a lost lifecycle record, or a connection's only record at one end, moves the attribution or pairing of other records, so a gapped interval's records are neither an upper nor a lower bound of a row's own, and a capture that lost events compares nothing in the epochs that lost them, saying so row by row, as the demo's server shows. Every read is of one generation, read again up to three times when a live session publishes between reads. The window's view of a comparison comes next.

Revision 753 brings the comparison into the window (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8a; plan M12, R18). A person works in the window, and the question it answers on a busy machine - what changed - begins at the interval they are looking at, so Compare with… (Ctrl+B) sits beside the time scope wherever it is an interval and compares it with a baseline: the same length just before it by default, or just after it, where either lies within the session's time; the whole recording, or the span of its records; or one typed. Its rows are `icat compare`'s, the command line's formatting moved into the application layer (`ComparisonText`) so both surfaces say the same values, changes and reasons, but for two processes no open was seen between, joined by "↔" as the window's relationship table joins them. Enter on a row opens what it compares - a relationship as a double click on its edge does, or the process whose connections to another host it compares - so a change found leads straight to its evidence. The table's value columns could not share their widths through `SharedSizeGroup` as set from code, which a probe found laid out each line alone, so each column is measured from its widest cell, the heading's included, in the window's own font.

Revision 754 shares a comparison as a redacted report (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8b; plan M12's shareable redacted reports, §11.3, I22). A comparison is the kind of finding a person hands to someone else, who should learn what changed but not the machine's process names, paths or addresses; so it is written under the sharing report's redaction policy rather than a new one, as a third kind of that report beside rankings and evidence, its processes, executables and other hosts random tokens. An executable's token is keyed by the path the process's group is keyed by, not by its name, since two executables of one name in two folders are two programs. The report records what M12 names - scope, byte domain, accounting, missing sources and alignment revision - the last as null with the reason: both intervals are read on one session's clock, and no alignment of captures applies until two captures are compared. Each row keeps its reason, since a value that cannot be compared is the comparison's most important caveat; the reasons are fixed sentences whose only variable parts are mechanism names, coverage reasons, epoch numbers and counts, which the comparison's tests already pin. A file holds every row unless `--limit` says otherwise, as the window's report holds every row rather than the 200 it lists: a reader of a file cannot ask for more.

Revision 755 compares by executable (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §2a, ADR-059; plan M12's entity grouping). Processes restart all the time on a busy machine, and a comparison by process turns a program's steady traffic into one relationship that disappeared and another that appeared; grouped by the path their image was witnessed at, the program keeps one row. The plan forbids establishing identity from the same executable path (P6), so the rows are a grouping and say so: no process is taken for another, two programs of one name in two folders are two rows, and a process whose executable was not witnessed is in no row at all, since putting every such process in one group's row would merge strangers - its relationships are counted apart, with their records, so nothing is left out silently or read as zero. The same keys are what a comparison of two captures can pair its rows by, still a grouping rather than an identity, which is why this came first.

Revision 756 compares two captures (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §2b, ADR-060; plan M12). The plan asks for a qualified mapping of entities between sessions and forbids identity from an equal name or the same executable path (P6), so the mapping is the one key both captures share, stated as what it is: each capture's processes grouped by the path their image was witnessed at, a row of one paired with the other's of that path, said to be a grouping that establishes no identity, with whether the two captures name one host identity said beside it. No process is mapped to another, and processes no path names are counted apart in both. Each interval keeps its own capture's clock - rates over whole intervals need no alignment - and each capture is read at a generation of its own. The rule that a change of the capture's profile never reads as a workload's now runs across the two ledgers too: each capture's own epochs must have collected a mechanism alike, and then the two captures through the same descriptors, which is M12's exit test for captures; another is that identical captures compare as equal, and both are named tests.

Revision 757 brings the comparison by executable and of two captures into the window (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8a; plan M12, R18). A person works in the window, so both forms the command line gained are there too: Rows chooses processes or executables, and Another saved capture… sets a saved session's whole recording as the baseline - its whole recording rather than any interval of it, since the window's other choices name intervals of the session shown, and the command line takes any. Two captures compare by executable alone, so Rows says so rather than offering a choice that does not exist. A row of executables opens an executable's rung, which exists only where the window groups its processes by executable and a process of the session shown ran it; elsewhere Show says why, rather than opening nothing silently. Refusing a folder puts the earlier baseline back without reading it again, since its comparison is still the one shown.

Revision 758 adds M12's saved comparison specifications (`contracts/workspace-v22.md` §7, `contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8c, ADR-061; plan M12, §8.4, R22). A person who compared the hour before an update with the hour after it comes back to the same question, and typing four bounds, two folders and two options again is where mistakes are made. The investigation keeps the question - two intervals, a metric and a grouping - by revision under a name, as it keeps a saved view, and never an answer, which a growing live capture or a relink would make stale. Each interval names its session and is in that session's own ticks rather than the investigation's time: two captures compare with no alignment (ADR-060), and an alignment withdrawn or revised later would otherwise move or erase it. Running one reads each session where the investigation finds it then, so the comparison follows a relinked or packaged session; its name, a person's words, is shown wherever it runs, counted in a package's disclosure, and never carried by a redacted report. The window saves and reopens them next.

Revision 759 brings saved comparisons into the window (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8c; plan M12, R18, R22). A person works in the window, so the comparison shown is saved from it, in the investigation its session was opened from - the only investigation that window knows - under a name asked for, the prompt saying before anything is written whether the name replaces one. Opening a saved comparison shows its compared session at its compared interval and the Compare window on that interval as saved, rather than on whatever scope the window had: the interval saved is the question, even where the session's records end before it, whose rows then read not comparable rather than disappeared. A session already open on its own still lets the opened comparison be saved again, since it came from the investigation, while a comparison of its own is not offered one. §8c moves after §8b, where the contract's order puts it; it had been inserted before §8a.

Revision 760 adds M12's baseline and difference timelines on the command line (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8d, ADR-062; plan M12, R18, R21, I16). A comparison says how much a row changed; the timeline says when within the two intervals. Each interval is cut into the same number of equal columns and the k-th of one set beside the k-th of the other - the same share of each, the one alignment two intervals of any lengths, or of two captures on two clocks, always share - and each column pair is compared as two intervals are, by the same judge, so a column the capture did not collect reads as not comparable rather than as a disappearance. The columns are read in the same read as the whole intervals, which the engine now makes from any two intervals of one read, so a row's columns add up to its whole values and name the same generation. A redacted report holds no timeline: its policy admits rows over whole intervals, and a timeline would widen it.

Revision 761 draws a row's difference timeline in the Compare window (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8a and §8d; plan M12, R15, R21). The window reads the comparison with its columns only when Timeline is checked, since each column is an interval read of its own, and keeps the listed rows' columns so choosing another row draws at once. Both intervals' bars share one rate scale, so a taller bar is a higher rate whichever interval it is of, and a column pair that cannot be compared is hatched across its bars, as the timeline hatches what a capture did not cover, rather than drawn as quiet. The chart is a drawn pane like the timelines: its column cursor moves from the keyboard, and the line beneath, which a screen reader also hears as the chart's name, states the column in the command line's words.

Revision 762 shows a comparison on the graph (`contracts/comparison-v1.md` §8e, ADR-063; plan M12, R14, R15, R21, P6). A comparison states rows and the graph draws a compaction, so each drawn edge is marked from the rows it draws: their change where they agree, their direction where each went one way or stayed, not comparable where one of them is, and up and down otherwise - never a sum of their values, which could rest on lower bounds or cancel two changes into a quiet edge. A comparison by executable marks every edge of its processes, its ring broken and its words saying so, since marking only rows drawn in one edge would leave a graph of processes unmarked. Every mark is a glyph and is said in words beneath the graph's header, on the edge's card and in the relationship table; an edge without one is said to have no record in either interval. A glyph is laid out in the graph's own text cache, as its labels are: a shared static cache of formatted text, drawn first into an offscreen bitmap by a test, crashed the test host when the window drew it next.

Revision 763 adds recurring communication patterns (`contracts/patterns-v1.md`, ADR-064; plan M12, R18, R21, P6). A pattern's signature is structure alone - a mechanism, a service port or an RPC interface and procedure, and whether the server end is on this machine or another host - because processes restart, addresses change and clients take new ports, while the kind of communication is what recurs. Each source is normalized by what it records: TCP's server is the end the capture saw accept, UDP's service port is the one outside the dynamic range since nothing opens a flow, and an RPC call's side is its role. A connection seen open at neither end is in no pattern rather than given a direction no record shows, and it is counted apart with flows between dynamic ports, calls no start named and what the policy did not admit. Recurrence is said as observed - more than one occurrence, in so many tenths of the session - and no period, cause or anomaly is inferred.

Revision 764 lists the recurring communication patterns in the window (`contracts/patterns-v1.md` §7, ADR-065; plan M12, §6.7, R18).
